Scattering towards the singularity for the wave equation and the linearized Einstein–scalar field system in Kasner spacetimes
Warren Li

Abstract
We consider the scalar wave equation □ g ​ ϕ = 0 \square_{g}\phi=0 and the linearized Einstein–scalar field system around generalized Kasner spacetimes with spatial topology 𝕋 D \mathbb{T}^{D} . In suitable regimes for the Kasner exponents, it is known that solutions to such equations arising from regular Cauchy data (e.g. at t = 1 t=1 ) have certain quantitative blow-up asymptotics near the initial time (i.e t = 0 t=0 ) singularity of Kasner. For instance, solutions to the wave equation behave as ϕ ⁡ ( t , x ) ≈ ψ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ log ⁡ t + φ ∞ ​ ( x ) \phi(t,x)\approx\psi_{\infty}(x)\log t+\varphi_{\infty}(x) near t = 0 t=0 . This article provides a description, and proof, of a scattering theory for the above equations, linking Cauchy data at t = 1 t=1 and suitable asymptotic data at t = 0 t=0 in Kasner. For the scalar wave equation, this means a Hilbert space isomorphism between ( ϕ , ∂ t ϕ ) (\phi,\partial_{t}\phi) at t = 1 t=1 and the functions ( ψ ∞ , φ ∞ ) (\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty}) . A curious detail is that certain quantities e.g. ψ ∞ \psi_{\infty} , feature a gain of 1 / 2 1/2 a derivative when compared to ∂ t ϕ \partial_{t}\phi at t = 1 t=1 . The study of the linearized Einstein–scalar field system reveals further interesting phenomena, including differences between diagonal and off-diagonal components of certain tensors in the scattering theory, and that the losses of derivatives feature a sensitive dependence on the anisotropy of the background Kasner spacetime. In fact, though our result holds for the entire subcritical regime of background Kasner exponents, the number of derivatives lost and gained in the scattering theory can become unbounded as one nears the boundary of this regime.



1
Introduction

1.1
Background

The (generalized) Kasner spacetimes
[
42
]
are a finite-dimensional family of spacetimes, defined on the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}=(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{T}^{D}
(we view
\mathbb{T}^{D}
as
[-\pi,\pi]^{D}
with endpoints identified) and equipped with the following Lorentzian metric
g_{Kas}
:

g_{Kas}=-dt^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{2p_{i}}(dx^{i})^{2}.

(1.1)

The parameters
p_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
are called the
Kasner exponents
.

We consider the Kasner metric (
1.1
) as a family of cosmological
1
1

1

Following the relativistic literature, the word cosmological refers to a gloally hyperbolic spacetimes having a closed spatial topology.
solutions to the Einstein field equations coupled to a massless scalar field
\phi:\mathcal{M}_{Kas}\to\mathbb{R}
:

\mathbf{G}_{\mu\nu}[g]\coloneqq\mathbf{Ric}_{\mu\nu}[g]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{R}[g]\,g_{\mu\nu}=2\,\mathbf{T}_{\mu\nu}[\phi],

(1.2)

\mathbf{T}_{\mu\nu}[\phi]\coloneqq\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\phi\,\mathbf{D}_{\nu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}\phi\,\mathbf{D}^{\sigma}\phi\,g_{\mu\nu}.

(1.3)

Here
\mathbf{D}
,
\mathbf{Ric}_{\mu\nu}[g]
and
\mathbf{R}[g]
denote the Levi-Civita connection, the Ricci curvature and the scalar curvature of the spacetime metric
g
, while
\mathbf{T}_{\mu\nu}[\phi]
denotes the
energy-momentum
tensor of the scalar field
\phi
. It follows from the contracted Bianchi equation and (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) that the scalar wave equation holds:

\square_{g}\phi\coloneqq\left(g^{-1}\right)^{\mu\nu}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\mathbf{D}_{\nu}\phi=0.

(1.4)

The Kasner metrics (
1.1
), complemented by the following expression for
\phi
:

\phi_{Kas}=p_{\phi}\log t,

(1.5)

are solutions to the Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.4
) if and only if the following two algebraic conditions, known as the (generalized)
Kasner relations
, between the Kasner exponents
p_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and the scalar field coefficient
p_{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}
, hold:

\sum_{i=1}^{D}p_{i}=1,\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{D}p_{i}^{2}+2p_{\phi}^{2}=1.

(1.6)

The Kasner spacetimes feature a spacelike past curvature singularity at
t=0
, and play an important role in the physics literature regarding singularity formation for Einstein’s equations, see
[
44
,
13
,
12
,
28
]
. In particular, at least in certain regimes, it is expected there exists a reasonably large class of spacetimes containing spacelike singularities which are locally described by a Kasner spacetime, though whose Kasner exponents have spatially dependence. We return to these heuristics in Section
2.3
.

In this article, we determine a
scattering theory
(between Cauchy data at
t=1
and suitable asymptotic data living at the
t=0
singularity) for two model
linear
hyperbolic problems in the Kasner spacetimes
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
, as an initial step towards understanding the analogous scattering problem for the nonlinear Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.3
). By scattering, we mean a Hilbert space isomorphism between Cauchy data for our hyperbolic evolution equations at the
t=1
Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma_{1}=\{1\}\times\mathbb{T}^{D}\subset\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
of Kasner, and suitable asymptotic data which can be thought to live at the
t=0
boundary of Kasner, between Hilbert spaces which must be defined as part of the theory.

The first of our model problems will be the scalar wave equation in a fixed Kasner background:

\square_{g_{Kas}}\phi\coloneqq(g_{Kas}^{-1})^{\mu\nu}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{\beta}\phi=0.

(1.7)

In the
(t,x^{i})
coordinates of (
1.1
), this can be written as the following PDE:

-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi-\frac{\partial_{t}\phi}{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{-2p_{i}}\partial_{x^{i}}^{2}\phi=0.

(1.8)

The standard theory of linear hyperbolic PDE allows one to study the following initial value problem: if one poses regular Cauchy data
(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{D})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{D})
on the spacelike Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma_{1}
, then there exists a unique regular solution to (
1.7
) on the entire spacetime
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
attaining the initial data. Standard hyperbolic regularity theory yields that the solution
(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
obeys:

\left(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}).

However, since the metric
g_{Kas}
degenerates towards
t=0
, the scalar field
\phi
may blow up as
t\to 0
, even for smooth Cauchy data. In fact, one obtains e.g. from
[
2
,
53
,
56
]
the following asymptotics for
\phi
as
t\to 0
:

Theorem 1.1

(Asymptotics for the wave equation in non-degenerate Kasner spacetimes)
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{M}_{Kas}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth solution to the wave equation (
1.7
) on a non-degenerate
2
2

2

Non-degenerate means that the Kasner exponents are not such that all but one of the Kasner exponents
p_{i}
vanish. The degenerate case with e.g.
p_{1}=1
and the remaining exponents vanishing is special in that the
t=0
boundary becomes a null Cauchy horizon rather than a spacelike singularity.
Kasner spacetime
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
, arising from smooth initial data
(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)|_{\Sigma_{1}}=(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{D})^{2}
. Then there exist smooth functions
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
such that as
t\to 0
,
\phi
obeys the following asympotics:

\phi(t,x)=\psi_{\infty}(x)\log t+\varphi_{\infty}(x)+o(1).

(1.9)

Remark
.

Theorem
1.1
implies that solutions to (
1.7
) asymptotically behave like solutions to the velocity dominated equation, meaning the equation (
1.8
) with the spatial
\partial_{x^{i}}
-terms ignored, i.e.

-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi-\frac{\partial_{t}\phi}{t}=0.

We make further comments on asymptotically velocity dominated behaviour in Section
2.3
.

The asymptotics (
1.9
) obtained in Theorem
1.1
suggest a notion of asymptotic data at the
t=0
singularity for the problem under consideration, namely the functions
\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty}:\mathbb{T}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
. The full scattering problem for the wave equation may thus be formulated as follows:

(i)

(
Existence of the scattering operator
):
For what class of Cauchy data
(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})
does there exist corresponding scattering data
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
such that the solution
\phi
to the wave equation attains the asymptotics (
1.9
) in a suitable sense? What can be said about the map
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}:(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})\mapsto(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
?

(ii)

(
Asymptotic completeness
):
If instead we are given
(\psi_{\infty},\phi_{\infty})
, when is it possible to find Cauchy data
(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})
such that the solution
\phi
to the wave equation attaining the Cauchy data obeys the asymptotics (
1.9
) with the given
\psi_{\infty}
and
\phi_{\infty}
? What about the map
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}=(\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow})^{-1}:(\psi_{\infty},\phi_{\infty})\mapsto(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})
.

(iii)

(
Scattering isomorphism
):
Can we find Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{C}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
such that the scattering operator
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}
can be extended to a Hilbert space isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}:\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
with inverse
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}
?

This article provides a complete resolution to the scattering problem for the wave equation in non-degenerate Kasner spacetimes, see Theorem
1.3
below.

The second model system addressed in this article is the linearized Einstein–scalar field system. This is given by the Fréchet derivative of the Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.4
) with respect to the metric
g=g_{Kas}+\varepsilon h
and scalar field
\phi=\phi_{Kas}+\varepsilon\upphi
. Inserting these into (
1.2
)–(
1.4
) and considering the resulting
O(\varepsilon)
term, one finds the formal linearization to be

\left(g_{Kas}^{-1}\right)^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{\beta}h_{\mu\nu}+\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\mathbf{D}_{\nu}\left(\tr h\right)-2\mathbf{D}_{(\mu}\left(\divergence h\right)_{\nu)}+2\,\mathbf{Riem}^{\alpha\phantom{\mu}\beta\phantom{\nu}}_{\phantom{\alpha}\mu\phantom{\beta}\nu}\,h_{\alpha\beta}=4\mathbf{D}_{(\mu}\upphi\mathbf{D}_{\nu)}\phi_{Kas}.

(1.10)

Here and in the sequel the Levi-Civita connection
\mathbf{D}
, the Riemann tensor
\mathbf{Riem}
, and the raising and lowering of indices, will all be associated to the background (non-degenerate) Kasner spacetime
g_{Kas}
. We also have the following linearization of the wave equation
3
3

3

Just as the wave equation (
1.7
) follows from (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) and the (twice contracted) Bianchi equation, one can derive (
1.11
) from (
1.10
) and the linearized Bianchi equations.
:

\square_{g_{Kas}}\upphi=h^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{\beta}\phi_{Kas}+\left(\divergence h\right)^{\alpha}\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\phi_{Kas}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{D}^{\alpha}\!\left(\tr h\right)\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\phi_{Kas}.

(1.11)

To view the system (
1.10
)–(
1.11
) in the context of a well-posed initial value problem, we must impose a choice of (linearized) gauge, most often inherited from a suitable gauge for the nonlinear Einstein–scalar field system.
In this article we to adopt a
(1+D)
-dimensional ADM-type gauge with Constant Mean Curvature (CMC), where the spacetime is foliated using a time function
t
whose level sets
\Sigma_{t}=\{t\}\times\mathbb{T}^{D}
have constant mean curvature equal to
\tr k=-t^{-1}
.

In this gauge, the key evolutionary variables of the linearized problem will be
h_{ij}
,
\kappa_{ij}
,
\upphi
,
\uppsi
, which represent linearized versions of the first fundamental form, the second fundamental form, the scalar field, and the normal derivative of the scalar field with respect to the foliation.
With this choice of CMC foliation, together with a choice of spatial gauge, one may cast (
1.10
)–(
1.11
) as a well-posed initial value problem of elliptic-hyperbolic type. See
[
8
]
for an introduction to elliptic-hyperbolic problems in general relativity.

In particular, if one poses Cauchy data
((h_{C})_{ij},(\kappa_{C})_{ij},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})\in H^{s+1}\times H^{s}\times H^{s+1}\times H^{s}
on the Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma_{1}
, which moreover satisfies various (linearized) constraints, then this data launches a solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.11
) in
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
, with

\displaystyle\left(h_{ij},\kappa_{ij}\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}),

\displaystyle\left(\upphi,\uppsi\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}).

Now consider the asymptotics of these quantities as
t\to 0
.
It turns out the analogue of Theorem
1.1
will only hold if we assume the Kasner exponents of
g_{Kas}
to satisfy the following inequality, related to the subcritical regime of
[
28
]
:

\max_{i\neq j}\left(p_{i}+p_{j}-p_{k}\right)<1.

(1.12)

We defer the further discussion of subcritical exponents to Section
2.3
. Assuming that this relation between the exponents holds on the background Kasner spacetime, we have the following result, where for now we define
\kappa_{ij}
and
\uppsi
as logarithmic time derivatives in the following sense:

\kappa_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2}t\partial_{t}h_{ij}+l.o.t.,\qquad\uppsi=t\partial_{t}\upphi+l.o.t.

Theorem 1.2

(Asymptotics for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system in subcritical Kasner spacetimes)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
be a Kasner spacetime whose Kasner exponents
p_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
obey the subcriticality relation (
1.12
). Suppose
(h_{ij},\kappa_{ij},\upphi,\uppsi)
is a
smooth
solution to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
1.10
)–(
1.11
) around
(g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
in a CMC ADM-type gauge, arising from smooth Cauchy data
((h_{C})_{ij},(\kappa_{C})_{ij},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})
on
\Sigma_{1}
satisfying suitable constraints.

Then there exist smooth
(1,1)
tensors
(\Upupsilon_{\infty})_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}
,
(\upkappa_{\infty})_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}
and smooth functions
\uppsi_{\infty}
and
\upvarphi_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
such that the solution obeys the following asymptotics
4
4

4

The expressions (
1.13
)–(
1.14
) do not, at first, appear symmetric in
i
and
j
; the fact that they are follow from the symmetry constraints in Lemma
4.5
.
as
t\to 0
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{ij}=\begin{cases}\left({(\kappa_{\infty})}_{{{i}}}^{\phantom{{{i}}}{j}}+2p_{{i}}{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{{i}}}^{\phantom{{{i}}}{j}}\right)t^{2p_{{i}}}+2p_{{i}}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{{i}}}^{\phantom{{{i}}}{j}}t^{2p_{{i}}}\log t+o(t^{2p_{i}})&\text{ if }p_{i}=p_{j},\\
-\frac{p_{j}}{p_{i}-p_{i}}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}t^{2p_{j}}+\left(\frac{p_{i}}{p_{i}-p_{j}}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+2p_{i}{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\right)t^{2p_{i}}+o(t^{\min\{2p_{i},2p_{j}\}})&\text{ if }p_{i}\neq p_{j},\end{cases}

(1.13)

\displaystyle h_{ij}=\begin{cases}-2\left(\left(\upkappa_{\infty}\right)_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}\log t+\left(\Upupsilon_{\infty}\right)_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}\right)t^{2p_{j}}+o(t^{2p_{j}}),&\text{ if }p_{i}=p_{j},\\
-\left(\frac{\left(\upkappa_{\infty}\right)_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}}{p_{i}-p_{j}}+2\left(\Upupsilon_{\infty}\right)_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}\right)t^{2p_{j}}+\frac{\left(\upkappa_{\infty}\right)_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}}{p_{i}-p_{j}}\,t^{2p_{i}}+o(t^{\min\{2p_{i},2p_{j}\}}),&\text{ if }p_{i}\neq p_{j},\end{cases}

(1.14)

\displaystyle\uppsi=\uppsi_{\infty}+o(1),

(1.15)

\displaystyle\upphi=\uppsi_{\infty}\log t+\upvarphi_{\infty}+o(1).

(1.16)

Theorem
1.2
is a corollary of
[
57
, Theorem 7.1]
, at least for the mildly anisotropic range of exponents included in that result. For the full range of Kasner exponents satisfying the subcriticality condition (
1.12
), Theorem
1.2
will follow from our scattering theory, and the proof is presented in Section
7.4
. Note an analogous result for the fully nonlinear Einstein–scalar field equations (for the full subcritical range of exponents) can be deduced from
[
35
]
.

Given Theorem
1.2
, we can make sense of the associated scattering problem as follows: what are the properties of the map
((h_{C})_{ij},(\kappa_{C})_{ij},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})\mapsto((\upkappa_{\infty})_{i}^{\phantom{i}j},(\Upupsilon_{\infty})_{i}^{\phantom{i}j},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
and, if well-defined, its inverse? Just as in the case of the wave equation, we may ask about (i) existence of the scattering operator, (ii) asymptotic completeness and (iii) the scattering isomorphism.

In the scattering problem for linearized gravity, one encounters additional subtleties due to the constraint equations and the choice of spatial gauge. As part of our scattering theory, we must understand how the constraint equations are realized on the asymptotic data
((\upkappa_{\infty})_{i}^{\phantom{i}j},(\Upupsilon_{\infty})_{i}^{\phantom{i}j},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
, and also understand how one fixes the spatial gauge throughout the scattering process. We return to these issues in Section
1.3
.

1.2
Scattering for the wave equation in Kasner

We first return to the simpler setting of the wave equation (
1.7
) on
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
; our goal in this section will be to state the scattering theorem in (non-degenerate) Kasner spacetimes. The precise scattering theorem will involve the symbol (or pseudodifferential operator)
\mathcal{T}_{*}
which we define as follows; see Appendix
A
for a review of the Fourier decomposition and symbol calculus on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
.

Definition 1.1
.

Firstly, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, let
t_{\lambda*}>0
be the unique solution of

\tau_{\lambda}^{2}(t_{\lambda*})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{D}t_{\lambda*}^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}=1
, and let
t_{0*}=1
.
Then set
\mathcal{T}_{*}
be the symbol associated (in the sense of Definition
A.3
) to the function
T_{*}:\mathbb{Z}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
which has
T_{*}(\lambda)=t_{\lambda*}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
. For any exponent
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we also define
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\alpha}
and
\log(\mathcal{T}_*)
following the standard symbol calculus.

Remark
.

In the language of
[
41
, Chapter XVIII]
, we show in Lemma
A.1
that
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\alpha}
lies in the symbol classes
S^{\max\{0,\alpha(1-p)^{-1}\}}
, where
p=\max_{i}p_{i}
, while
\log(\mathcal{T}_*)
lies in
S^{\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. In particular, for suitably chosen
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
the operators
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{\alpha}
and
\log(\mathcal{T}_*)
are bounded operators from
H^{s_{1}}
to
H^{s_{2}}
.

1.2.1
The wave equation as a first-order system

It will be advantageous to rewrite the wave equation (
1.7
) as a first-order (hyperbolic) system of evolution equations for the variables
\phi
and
\psi\coloneqq t\partial_{t}\phi
. Multiplying (
1.8
) by
t^{2}
, one gets the system:

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\phi=\psi,

(1.17)

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{2-2p_{i}}\partial_{x^{i}}^{2}\phi.

(1.18)

Next, recall from Theorem
1.1
that although
\psi
remains bounded as one approaches
t=0
,
\phi
itself will blow up as
O(\log t^{-1})
. Thus as
t\to 0
, instead of considering
\phi
itself we consider the renormalized variable
\varphi
, defined as:

\varphi(t,\cdot)=\phi(t,\cdot)-\log t\cdot\psi(t,\cdot).

(1.19)

Due to Theorem
1.1
one expects
\varphi(t,\cdot)
to remain bounded as
t\to 0
, in fact it will converge to
\varphi_{\infty}
.

1.2.2
The scattering theorem

Having set up our system, we now state Theorem
1.3
, our scattering result for the scalar wave equation (
1.7
) in non-degenerate Kasner spacetimes. The proof of Theorem
1.3
will be in Section
3
.

Theorem 1.3

(Scattering for the wave equation in non-degenerate Kasner spacetimes)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
be a non-degenerate Kasner spacetime. We consider solutions
\phi:\mathcal{M}_{Kas}\to\mathbb{R}
to the wave equation (
1.7
) on
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
. Then we have the following scattering results:

(i)

(Existence of the scattering operator)
Let
s>\frac{D}{2}+1
. Given Cauchy data
(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})\in H^{s+1}\times H^{s}
, there exists a unique (classical) solution
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
to the system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) in
\mathcal{M}
attaining the Cauchy data
\phi(1,\cdot)=\phi_{C}(\cdot)
and
\partial_{t}\phi(1,\cdot)=\psi_{C}(\cdot)
. Let
\varphi(t)
be defined by (
1.19
).

Then there exist unique functions
\psi_{\infty}(x)
and
\varphi_{\infty}(x)
in
H^{s}
such that

\psi(t,x)\to\psi_{\infty}(x)\text{ in }H^{s},\qquad\varphi(t,x)\to\varphi_{\infty}(x)\text{ in }H^{s}\quad\text{ strongly as }t\to 0.

(1.20)

Furthermore, one has that

\psi_{\infty}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\varphi_{\infty}+\log(\mathcal{T}_*)\psi_{\infty}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}.

(1.21)

(ii)

(Asymptotic completeness)
Let
s>\frac{D}{2}+1
, and let
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
be functions in
\mathbb{T}^{D}
with Sobolev regularity as in (
1.21
). Then there exists a unique (classical) solution
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
to the system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) in
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
, with regularity

\left(\phi,\psi\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}),

such that if
\varphi(t)
is defined as in (
1.19
), then the asymptotics (
1.20
) hold, strongly as
t\to 0
.

(iii)

(Scattering isomorphism)
For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, define the following Hilbert spaces together with their norms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}=\{\left(\phi,\psi\right):\phi\in H^{s+1},\psi\in H^{s}\},\quad\|\left(\phi,\psi\right)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}}^{2}\coloneqq\|\phi\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi\|_{H^{s}}^{2},

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\infty}=\{\left(\psi,\varphi\right):\psi\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}},\varphi+\log(\mathcal{T}_*)\psi\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\},\quad\|\left(\psi,\varphi\right)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}}^{2}\coloneqq\|\psi\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\varphi+\log(\mathcal{T}_*)\psi\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

(1.23)

Then the map
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}
, which is defined to map
(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})
from (
i
) to
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
in (
1.20
), may be extended to a Hilbert space isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}:\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\infty}
, whose inverse
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}=\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}^{-1}
is exactly given by (a suitable extension of) the map taking
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
in (
ii
) to
\left(\phi(1,\cdot),\psi(1,\cdot)\right)
.

Remark
.

A remarkable feature of Theorem
1.3
is the gain or loss of half a derivative from Cauchy data to scattering data. In particular, the renormalized time derivative
\psi=t\partial_{t}\phi
lies in
H^{s}
at any fixed time
t>0
, but its limit
\psi_{\infty}
at
t=0
has its regularity increased to
H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}
. This as a sign that spatial derivatives not only become negligible compared to temporal derivatives near
t=0
, but actually become better behaved as one approaches the singular boundary at
t=0
.

Note than an almost-sharp correspondence between Cauchy data and asymptotic data was already found by Ringström in
[
56
, Propositions 8.1 and 8.9]
, however in this work the derivative gain is from
H^{s}
to
H^{s+\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
rather than the sharp
H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}
. In order to achieve this sharp result, it is essential that we modify our Hilbert spaces using the symbol
\log(\mathcal{T}_*)
, a feature not present in
[
56
]
. This final modification was partly inspired by a scattering result for the wave equation in higher-dimensional de Sitter spacetimes
[
23
]
.

Remark
.

One could also entertain scattering between Cauchy data at
t=1
and a suitable notion of asymptotic data at
t=+\infty
. The issue is that although one may obtain upper bounds for
\phi(t)
and
\psi(t)
as
t\to+\infty
, it is difficult to upgrade these to asymptotics since both
\phi(t)
and
\psi(t)
oscillate at late times. Moreover different Fourier modes will oscillate at different frequencies, see e.g. the Bessel function type behaviour suggested in Section
1.4
or the partial results of
[
53
]
.

Another reason we do not study
t\to+\infty
scattering is that the Kasner spacetimes are expected to be unstable in the
t\to+\infty
direction as solutions to the Einstein–scalar field system, see
[
48
]
. One could conjecture that generic perturbations lead to either isotropic expansion or recollapse into a singularity. This is related to the “cosmic no-hair conjecture” in the presence of a positive cosmological constant, see
[
10
,
16
]
.

1.3
Scattering for linearized gravity in Kasner

The second goal of the article will be to describe and prove a similar scattering theory for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.3
), where the dynamical variables
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
are linearized perturbations around the background Kasner solution (
1.1
) and (
1.5
). We first introduce more precisely the linearized system, and formulate it as a first-order system in the sense of Section
1.2.1
.

1.3.1
Linearized Einstein–scalar field as a first-order system

As stated previously, we employ a (linearized) CMC ADM-type gauge. More precisely, our scattering theorem, Theorem
1.4
, is with respect to a (linearized)
Constant Mean Curvature Transported Coordinate
(CMCTC) gauge. The nonlinear version of this gauge has coordinates
(t,x^{i})\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{D}
and a Lorentzian metric

g=-n^{2}dt^{2}+\bar{g}_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}.

(1.24)

Here
n:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is known as the
lapse
, while
\bar{g}_{ij}=\bar{g}_{ij}(t)
are a time-dependent family of Riemannian metrics on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
.
Our time function
t
, and thus the lapse, is fixed since we choose our foliation by hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}=\{t\}\times\mathbb{T}^{D}
to be one of constant mean curvature equal to
\tr k|_{\Sigma_{t}}=-t^{-1}
. In this gauge
the Einstein–scalar field equations (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) may be written as a system of second-order evolution equations for the
\bar{g}_{ij}
and
\phi
, coupled to an elliptic equation for the lapse
n
that arises from the CMC condition. There are also the usual Hamiltonian and momentum constraints.

This system of evolution equations plus elliptic equations plus constraints can be rewritten as a first-order system of evolution equations for the variables
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\mathbf{N}\phi)
, where
k_{ij}
is the second fundamental form and
\mathbf{N}\phi
is the derivative of
\phi
normal to the foliation, coupled also to an elliptic equation for
n
and with
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\mathbf{N}\phi,n)
satisfying several constraints. The resulting system will be written in full detail in Section
2.1
.

For now, we first present the first-order system for the
linearized
Einstein–scalar field system, where the variables are linearized perturbations of a fixed generalized Kasner solution of the form (
1.1
), (
1.5
) satisfying the Kasner relations (
1.6
). Quantities associated to this background Kasner spacetime
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
are denoted with an overhead circle, for instance
5
5

5

Here, and in the sequel, underlined indices mean that there is no summation. Also, we often write
\mathring{g}_{ij}
in place of
\mathring{\bar{g}}_{ij}
.

\mathring{g}_{ij}=\mathring{\bar{g}}_{ij}=t^{2p_{\underline{i}}}\delta_{\underline{i}j},\quad\mathring{n}=1,\quad\mathring{\phi}=p_{\phi}\log t,\quad\text{ etc.}

The variables in our linearized Einstein–scalar field system are chosen to be the following (see already Definition
4.1
in Section
4.1
):

\displaystyle{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\left(g_{ik}-\mathring{g}_{ik}\right),\quad{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\coloneqq t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}-t(\mathring{\bar{g}}^{-1})^{jk}\mathring{k}_{ik},

(1.25)

\displaystyle\upnu\coloneqq n-\mathring{n}=n-1,

(1.26)

\displaystyle\upphi\coloneqq\phi-\mathring{\phi},\quad\uppsi\coloneqq t\left(\mathbf{N}\phi-\partial_{t}\mathring{\phi}\right).

(1.27)

The linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
1.10
)–(
1.11
), with respect to these variables, will be derived in Proposition
4.2
, and are roughly categorised as follows. Firstly there are the evolution equations for the linearized metric and matter variables
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
:

t\partial_{t}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}={\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}){\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}){\upeta}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu,

(1.28)

t\partial_{t}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{il}\partial_{j}\partial_{l}(\tr\upeta)-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{i}\partial_{b}{\upeta}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{aj}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{b}}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\upnu-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu,

(1.29)

t\partial_{t}\upphi=\uppsi+p_{\phi}\upnu,

(1.30)

t\partial_{t}\uppsi=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upphi-p_{\phi}\upnu.

(1.31)

Next, we have the elliptic equation for
\upnu
,

\left(1-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\right)\upnu=-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(\tr\upeta)+2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{b}\partial_{c}{\upeta}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}}.

(1.32)

Finally, we have the constraint equations and gauge conditions, which are propagated by (
1.28
)–(
1.32
). To keep the exposition brief, we do not write these here.

Remark
.

The system (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) is written in a tensorial form in order to apply tensorial estimates, and in the hope that one could generalize to spatial topologies other than
\mathbb{T}^{D}
. The downside is that the equations appear a little mysterious, due to terms featuring
t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
. To demystify, note that in the usual coordinates
t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=-p_{\underline{i}}{\delta}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{j}}
, where
p_{i}
are the Kasner exponents.

For instance, (
1.28
) may be rewritten as:

t\partial_{t}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}={\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+(2p_{\underline{i}}-2p_{\underline{j}}){\upeta}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{\underline{j}}}-p_{\underline{i}}{\delta}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{j}}\upnu.

In particular, even ignoring the
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
term, one expects a term of order
t^{2p_{i}-2p_{j}}
in the asymptotics of
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
.

1.3.2
Renormalized variables

In light of this remark, not all components of
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
will generically remain bounded as
t\to 0
, hence
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is not a suitable choice of asymptotic data at
t=0
. This is true also for
\upphi
, which is
O(\log t^{-1})
near
t=0
. As a result, we make the following definitions which do have suitable limits as
t\to 0
:

Definition 1.2
.

For a solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
to the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations (
1.28
)–(
1.32
), we define the renormalized quantities
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upvarphi
as follows:

{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\coloneqq{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\int^{1}_{t}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}=\begin{cases}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-{\upkappa}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\log t,&\text{ if }p_{i}=p_{j}\\[6.00006pt]
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-{\upkappa}_{{\underline{j}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{j}}}{\underline{i}}}\left(\displaystyle{\frac{t^{2p_{\underline{i}}-2p_{\underline{j}}}-1}{2p_{\underline{i}}-2p_{\underline{j}}}}\right)&\text{ if }p_{i}\neq p_{j}.\end{cases}

(1.33)

\upvarphi\coloneqq\upphi-\uppsi\log t.

(1.34)

Remark
.

Though (
1.33
) and (
1.34
) are convenient in that they agree with the natural Cauchy data
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upphi
at
t=1
, it will be useful to consider more general versions where
t=1
is replaced by
t=T
, i.e.

{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}={\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}},\qquad\tilde{\upvarphi}=\upphi-\uppsi\log( \frac{t}{T} ).

(1.35)

In our energy estimates, the natural choice of
T
turns out to be the frequency dependent
T=t_{\lambda*}
, with
t_{\lambda*}
as in Definition
1.1
. This indicates that a sharp scattering statement requires the use of the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}
, as we will see in Theorem
1.4
.

1.3.3
Infinitesimal gauge transformations

Before proceeding to state the scattering theorem for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
1.28
)–(
1.32
), we make one further observation regarding the choice of spatial gauge. The observation is that though our time function
t
is fixed by the CMC condition, there is remaining gauge freedom in the ADM-type gauge (
1.24
), due to diffeomorphisms from
\mathbb{T}^{D}
to itself.

At the infinitesimal level, such diffeomorphisms are associated to a vector field
\upxi^{i}
on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, since such vector fields generate a one-parameter family of diffeomorphisms via their flow. Such a vector field (which we often denote a
change of gauge
) causes the linearized quantities
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
to transform as follows:

\displaystyle{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\mapsto{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\partial_{q}\upxi^{p},

(1.36)

\displaystyle{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\mapsto{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}})\partial_{p}\upxi^{j}-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}})\partial_{i}\upxi^{p}.

(1.37)

The renormalized quantity
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
defined in (
1.33
) transforms as follows:

{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\mapsto{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}(1)\mathring{g}^{jq}(1)\partial_{q}\upxi^{p}.

(1.38)

For now, let
\upxi^{i}
be a regular vector field on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
that does not vary in time. Under the change of gauge (
1.36
)–(
1.38
), the system of equations (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) is invariant. (Such gauge invariance holds in a broader context in Section
4.2
, where we allow the vector field
\upxi^{i}
to also depend on time.)

A complete scattering theory for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system must be armed with a canonical choice of gauge. It turns out that the choice of gauge that leads to sharp estimates will be different for Cauchy data at
t=1
and for asymptotic data at
t=0
, as we explain now.

Our canonical gauge for Cauchy data is inherited from the requirement that the coordinates
x^{i}
are harmonic with respect to the Riemannian metric
\bar{g}_{ij}(1)
at
t=1
. Upon linearization, this translates to the following condition for
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
:

2\partial_{j}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=\partial_{i}(\tr\upeta)\quad\text{ at }t=1.

(1.39)

This is related to the linearized
Constant Mean Curvature Spatially Harmonic
(CMCSH) gauge that appears in Section
4.1
, the difference being that in CMCSH gauge, (
1.39
) is instead true at all times
t>0
(and for that we must allow
\upxi^{j}
to be time-dependent).
It will be shown in Lemma
4.3
that any Cauchy data for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system can be transformed into this gauge.

One may now understand one direction of the scattering problem as follows. Firstly, one prescribes Cauchy data for
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
at
t=1
which satisfies suitable constraints as well as the spatially harmonic condition (
1.39
).
One then evolves this data using (
1.28
)–(
1.32
), as a regular solution in
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
.
For (i) the existence of scattering states, we show that the solution obeys certain asymptotics as
t\to 0
, namely that the renormalized quantities
({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\upvarphi)
attain limits as
t\to 0
:

({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\upvarphi)\to({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty}).

The opposite direction involves prescribing the asymptotic data
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
at
t=0
. These also satisfy suitable constraint equations, derived the usual constraint equations, see Lemma
4.5
. More importantly, one must impose a canonical gauge for the asymptotic data. In light of our energy estimates, we make a slightly peculiar choice, which will be the following identity involving
6
6

6

We make sense of the integral in (
1.40
) in terms of the symbol calculus; indeed upon evaluation of the integral it is just a combination of constants and
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{2p_{i}-2p_{j}}
or
\log(\mathcal{T}_*)
.
the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}
.

2\partial_{j}\left({(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\int^{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{*}}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right)=2\partial_{i}\left(\tr\Upupsilon_{\infty}\right).

(1.40)

We later show that we can always transform suitable asymptotic data into this gauge, which we often call the
canonical asymptotic gauge condition
.

For (ii) asymptotic completeness, we show that for any sufficiently regular asymptotic data
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
satisfying the constraints as well as the gauge condition (
1.40
), there exists a unique regular solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
to the system (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) in the whole spacetime
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
, which achieves the prescribed asymptotic data at
t=0
.

As warned, the gauge choice corresponding to linearly spatially harmonic data at
t=1
(i.e. data satisfying (
1.39
)), will not necessarily match in evolution with asymptotic data which satisfies the canonical asymptotic gauge condition (
1.40
), and vice versa. Therefore, in the scattering theory we allow for a change of gauge vector field
\upxi^{j}
that moves from one gauge to the other.
See Section
4.2
for further comments regarding the spatial gauge.

1.3.4
The scattering theorem

We now state a slightly imprecise, but still rather detailed, version of the scattering theorem for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system around a Kasner spacetime obeying the subcriticality relation (
1.12
). We prove Theorem
1.4
in Section
7
. The proof will actually be via a scattering theorem in another gauge, Theorem
7.1
.

Theorem 1.4

(Scattering for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system in subcritical Kasner spacetimes)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
be a (generalized) Kasner spacetime whose exponents satisfy the subcriticality condition (
1.12
). Consider solutions
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
to the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations around
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
, written as the first-order elliptic-hyperbolic system (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) and satisfying suitable constraints. The scattering theory is as follows:

(i)

(Existence of the scattering operator)
Let
s
be sufficiently large. Then given Cauchy data
({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})\in H^{s+1}\times H^{s}\times H^{s+1}\times H^{s}
at
t=1
satisfying suitable constraints as well as the spatially harmonic gauge condition (
1.39
), there exists a unique (classical) solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
to the system (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) in
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
attaining the Cauchy data

{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(1,\cdot)={(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(\cdot),\quad{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(1,\cdot)={(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(\cdot),\quad\upphi(1,\cdot)=\upphi_{C}(\cdot),\quad\uppsi(1,\cdot)=\uppsi_{C}(\cdot).

Let
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upvarphi
be as in (
1.33
)–(
1.34
).
Then there exist unique
(1,1)
tensors
{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(x)
and
{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(x)
and unique functions
\uppsi_{\infty}(x)
and
\upvarphi_{\infty}(x)
such that

{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t,\cdot)\to{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(\cdot)\text{ in }C^{3},\qquad{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t,\cdot)\to{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(\cdot)\text{ in }C^{3}\quad\text{ strongly as }t\to 0,

(1.41)

\uppsi(t,\cdot)\to\uppsi_{\infty}(\cdot)\text{ in }H^{s},\qquad\upvarphi(t,\cdot)\to\upvarphi_{\infty}(\cdot)\text{ in }H^{s}\quad\text{ strongly as }t\to 0.

(1.42)

Furthermore, there exists a change of gauge vector field
\upxi_{\infty}^{j}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}
such that after applying the transformations (
1.36
)–(
1.38
), the transformed versions of
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
satisfy suitable asymptotic constraints as well as the canonical asymptotic gauge condition (
1.40
), and have the following regularity:

\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{\underline{i}}+p_{\underline{j}}}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{\underline{j}}}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{\underline{i}}+p_{\underline{j}}}\left({(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{\underline{j}}}+\int^{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{*}}\mathring{g}_{\underline{i}p}(s)\mathring{g}^{\underline{j}q}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right)\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}},

(1.43)

\uppsi_{\infty}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\upvarphi_{\infty}+\log(\mathcal{T}_*)\uppsi_{\infty}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}.

(1.44)

(ii)

(Asymptotic completeness)
Let
s
be sufficiently large, and let
{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
be
(1,1)
tensors in
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, and
\uppsi_{\infty}
,
\upvarphi_{\infty}
be functions in
\mathbb{T}^{D}
with Sobolev regularity as in (
1.43
)–(
1.44
), and satisfying suitable constraints as well as the canonical asymptotic gauge condition (
1.40
). Then there exists a unique (classical) solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
to the system (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) in
\mathcal{M}_{Kas}
, with regularity

\left({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}),

\left(\upphi,\uppsi\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}),

as well as a change of gauge vector field
\upxi^{j}\in H^{s-1}
, such that:

1.

The quantity
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
satisfies the spatially harmonic condiion (
1.39
) at
t=1
.

2.

If
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upvarphi
are defined as in (
1.33
)–(
1.34
), then after applying the transformation (
1.36
)–(
1.38
) associated to the vector field
\upxi^{j}
, the resulting transformed quantities obey the asymptotics (
1.41
) and (
1.42
) hold, strongly as
t\to 0
.

(iii)

(Scattering isomorphism)
For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, define the following Hilbert spaces together with their norms:

\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}=\left\{\left({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi\right):{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\in H^{s+1},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\in H^{s},\upphi\in H^{s+1},\uppsi\in H^{s}\right\},

(1.45)

\left\|\left({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{i,j=1}^{D}\left(\|{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\right)+\|\upphi\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\uppsi\|_{H^{s}}^{2},

(1.46)

\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\infty}=\left\{\left({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\upvarphi\right):\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}\left({\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-\int^{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{*}}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right)\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\right.,\\[3.00003pt]
\left.\uppsi\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}},\upvarphi+\log(\mathcal{T}_*)\uppsi\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

(1.47)

\left\|\left({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\upvarphi\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{i,j=1}^{D}\left(\left\|\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}\left({\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-\int^{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{*}}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right)\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\right)\\[3.00003pt]
+\|\uppsi\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\upvarphi+\log(\mathcal{T}_*)\uppsi\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

(1.48)

Further let
\mathcal{H}_{C,c}^{s}
be the subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{C}^{s}
obeying suitable constraints as well as the spatially harmonic gauge condition (
1.39
), and similarly let
\mathcal{H}_{\infty,c}^{s}
be the subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{s}
obeying the appropriate asymptotic constraints as well as the canonical asymptotic gauge condition (
1.40
).

From (
i
), let the operator
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}
map the Cauchy data
({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})
to the asymptotic quantities
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
, where the asymptotic quantities are taken
after
modification by the gauge transformation associated to the vector field
\upxi^{j}
in (
i
). Then this map can be extended to a bounded, injective homomorphism from
\mathcal{H}_{C,c}^{s}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\infty,c}^{s}
.

Conversely, from (
ii
), let the operator
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}
map the asymptotic data
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
satisfying (
1.40
) to
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(1,\cdot),{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(1,\cdot),\upphi(1,\cdot),\uppsi(1,\cdot))
, noting that
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(1,\cdot)
is chosen to satisfy (
1.39
) after modification by the change of gauge vector field
\xi^{j}
. Then this map
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}
may be extended to a bounded, injective homomorphism from
\mathcal{H}_{\infty,c}^{s}
to
\mathcal{H}_{C,c}^{s}
.

Finally,
\mathcal{H}_{\infty,c}^{s}
is the image of
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{C,c}^{s}
, and we may view
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}:\mathcal{H}_{C,c}^{s}\to\mathcal{H}_{\infty,c}^{s}
as a Hilbert space isomorphism with inverse
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}
.

Remark
.

Aside from the
H^{s}\leftrightarrow H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}
phenomenon already observed in Theorem
1.3
, there is a new phenomenon that arises in the scattering theory upon introduction of the
(1,1)
-tensors
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
. This is that due to the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}
, the anisotropy of the Kasner spacetime also affects the derivative gain and loss incurred in the scattering process, particularly in
off-diagonal
components of the tensors
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
.

In fact, as one varies across the whole range of Kasner exponents satisfying the subcriticality condition (
1.12
), the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}
can be of arbitrarily high order. Thus the degeneration of top order energy estimates – without modification by such a symbol – as observed in the nonlinear setting of say
[
35
]
or
[
33
]
, is a true phenomenon, and one expects that in any nonlinear scattering result one must allow for a large loss of derivatives in either direction.

Remark
.

The subcriticality condition (
1.12
) is necessary in order to conclude asymptotics of the type given in Theorem
1.4
(i), due to heuristics of
[
44
]
. However, even if one violates (
1.12
), it may be possible to prove a version of Theorem
1.4
if we suppress the linearly unstable directions.

For instance, if one makes the additional assumption that for any
i,j,k
such that
-p_{i}+p_{j}+p_{k}\geq 1
,

\frac{\partial_{k}{\upkappa}_{{\underline{j}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{j}}}{\underline{i}}}(t)}{p_{\underline{i}}-p_{\underline{j}}}-\frac{\partial_{j}{\upkappa}_{{\underline{k}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{k}}}{\underline{i}}}(t)}{p_{\underline{i}}-p_{\underline{k}}}=0

(1.49)

for all times
t>0
, then our proof shows that a version of Theorem
1.4
still holds. (
1.49
) is true in certain symmetry classes, for instance
\mathbb{T}^{2}
symmetry or polarized
U(1)
symmetry in
1+3
dimensions. This complements known nonlinear stability results in these classes
[
5
,
35
]
. In fact one would expect a version of Theorem
1.4
to still hold if (
1.49
) is only valid
asymptotically as
t\to 0
. We return to this issue in Section
2.3
.

1.4
Features of the proof

We illustrate several ideas involved in the proofs of Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
. For simplicity, we mainly focus on the isotropic Kasner spacetime with
p_{I}=\frac{1}{D}
for
I=1,\ldots,D
, and mention only briefly the differences in the anisotropic case. We also rescale time in such a way that the wave equation (
1.7
) can be rewritten as the following first order hyperbolic system, where
\Delta=(g_{Euc}^{-1})^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}
is the standard Laplacian on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
.

t\partial_{t}\phi=\psi,\qquad t\partial_{t}\psi=t^{2}\Delta\phi.

(1.50)

Our linear scattering results will always follow upon decomposing our PDEs in frequency space using Fourier series, and performing ODE analysis for each Fourier mode. Taking the Fourier decomposition of (
1.50
), we have for
|\lambda|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\lambda_{i}^{2}
the system

t\partial_{t}\phi_{\lambda}=\psi_{\lambda},\qquad t\partial_{t}\psi_{\lambda}=-|\lambda|^{2}\phi_{\lambda}.

(1.51)

Remarkably, (
1.51
) can be solved explicitly. This is because (
1.51
) is Bessel’s equation (of order 0) written in first order form. Using
[
31
]
as a reference regarding Bessel’s equation and the Bessel functions
J_{\nu}
and
Y_{\nu}
, solutions to (
1.51
) can be written as the following linear combinations of Bessel functions:

\phi_{\lambda}=c_{J}(\lambda)J_{0}(|\lambda|t)+c_{Y}(\lambda)Y_{0}(|\lambda|t),\qquad\psi_{\lambda}=-c_{J}(\lambda)(|\lambda|t)J_{1}(|\lambda|t)-c_{Y}(\lambda)(|\lambda|t)Y_{1}(|\lambda|t).

(1.52)

In particular, the asymptotics of Bessel functions in
[
31
, Chapter 10.7]
imply the following behaviour for
\phi_{\lambda}
and
\psi_{\lambda}
in the regimes
|\lambda|t\to 0
and
|\lambda|t\to+\infty
.

\displaystyle\phi_{\lambda}(t)=\begin{cases}\frac{2}{\pi}c_{Y}(\lambda)\log(|\lambda| t)+c_{J}(\lambda)+\cdots&\text{ as }|\lambda|t\to 0,\\
\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi|\lambda|t}}\left(c_{J}(\lambda)\cos(|\lambda|t - \frac{\pi}{4})+c_{Y}(\lambda)\sin(|\lambda|t - \frac{\pi}{4})\right)+\cdots&\text{ as }|\lambda|t\to+\infty,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(t)=\begin{cases}\frac{2}{\pi}c_{Y}(\lambda)+\cdots&\text{ as }|\lambda|t\to 0,\\
\sqrt{\frac{2|\lambda|t}{\pi}}\left(-c_{J}(\lambda)\sin(|\lambda|t - \frac{\pi}{4})+c_{Y}(\lambda)\cos(|\lambda|t - \frac{\pi}{4})\right)+\cdots&\text{ as }|\lambda|t\to+\infty.\end{cases}

One may read off from these asymptotics that as
t\to 0
, we expect an expansion
\phi=\psi_{\infty}\log t+\varphi_{\infty}+\cdots
as in Theorem
1.1
, and moreover that the Fourier coefficients of
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
can be read off from the coefficients
c_{J}(\lambda)
and
c_{Y}(\lambda)
. Using the characterization of Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
in Fourier space, we may then translate these asymptotics into statements about Sobolev regularity:

1.

Having
\phi(1,\cdot)\in H^{s+1}
and
\psi(1,\cdot)\in H^{s}
is equivalent to the sum
\sum_{\lambda}|\lambda|^{2s+1}(|c_{J}(\lambda)|^{2}+|c_{Y}(\lambda)|^{2})
being finite. This is due to the powers of
|\lambda|t
arising in the
|\lambda|t\to\infty
regime.

2.

At
t=0
, we observe that
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}=\frac{2}{\pi}c_{Y}(\lambda)
and
(\varphi_{\infty})_{\lambda}=c_{J}(\lambda)-\frac{2}{\pi}c_{Y}(\lambda)\log|\lambda|=c_{J}(\lambda)-(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}\log|\lambda|
. Therefore, the sum
\sum_{\lambda}|\lambda|^{2s+1}(|c_{J}(\lambda)|^{2}+|c_{Y}(\lambda)|^{2})
being finite is equivalent to
\psi_{\infty}
and the modified
\varphi_{\infty}+(\log|\nabla|)\psi_{\infty}
being in
H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}
.

Combining these two observations yields the scattering result of Theorem
1.3
, at least after detailed analysis of the error terms in the Bessel asymptotics.

However, since we wish to generalize to anisotropic Kasner spacetimes, and also to the study of the linearized Einstein–scalar field system, it is desirable to find a method more robust than using the explicit Bessel functions. That is, we wish to capture Bessel-like asymptotics in both the
|\lambda|t\to+\infty
and the
|\lambda|t\to 0
regimes, which we denote the high-frequency and low-frequency regimes respectively.

It turns out we can achieve this via energy estimates for each Fourier mode
(\phi_{\lambda},\psi_{\lambda})
. In our two regimes, we shall require two different energy estimates, which we explain as follows:

1.

In the high-frequency regime
|\lambda|t\gg 1
, we use a high-frequency energy which is given by

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)=\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}}{|\lambda|t}+\frac{\psi_{\lambda}\phi_{\lambda}}{|\lambda|t}+\frac{\phi_{\lambda}^{2}}{2|\lambda|t}+|\lambda|t\phi_{\lambda}^{2}.

Using (
1.51
), a computation involving crucial top order cancellations yields that for some
C>0
,

|t\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)|\leq C(\lambda t)^{-2}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t).

Therefore, Grönwall’s inequality implies that for a fixed Fourier mode
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, the high-frequency energy remains well controlled in the region
|\lambda|t\geq 1
.

2.

In the low-frequency regime
|\lambda|t\ll 1
, we first define
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}=\phi_{\lambda}-\psi_{\lambda}\log(|\lambda| t)
so that
\phi_{\lambda}=\tilde{\varphi}(|\lambda|t)
exactly when
|\lambda|t=1
. We then use a low-frequency energy quantity which is simply

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)=\psi_{\lambda}^{2}+\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{2}.

This is uniformly equivalent to the high-frequency energy
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)
at the transitional time
t=|\lambda|^{-1}
, and one may show from the system (
1.51
) that

|t\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)|\leq C(\lambda t)^{2}(1+|\log(\lambda t)|^{2})\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t).

Therefore, Grönwall’s inequality implies that for a fixed Fourier mode
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, the low-frequency energy remains well controlled in the region
0<|\lambda|t\leq 1
.

Combining these two energy estimates and translating into a Sobolev regularity statement yields a proof of Theorem
1.3
. Furthermore, this energy method is robust and may be applied more widely.

In particular, it is robust enough to deal with the linearized Einstein equations. Focusing only on the linearized metric perturbations, we write the linearized Einstein equations (in the isotropic Kasner with a rescaled time variable) in the following schematic form, where
\upeta
and
\upkappa
are our main dynamical variables:

t\partial_{t}\upeta=\upkappa+\upnu+\partial\upchi,\qquad t\partial_{t}\upkappa=t^{2}\Delta\upeta+t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta+\upnu+\partial\upchi.

(1.53)

There are three major differences from the case of the scalar wave equation. Firstly,
\upeta
and
\upkappa
are
(1,1)
-tensors rather than scalars. In particular, we see terms of the kind
t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta
, which schematically represent linear combinations of contractions of the derivatives and the tensor indices.

Secondly, the equations (
1.53
) are coupled to an elliptic equation for
\upnu
, which is schematically

(1-t^{2}\Delta)\upnu=t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta.

(1.54)

Finally, we see another variable
\upchi
, which corresponds to a gauge choice (see Section
4.2
). We single out two such gauge choices: one choice is we set
\upchi=0
, or alternatively we may choose a gauge, called the CMCSH gauge, such that
\upchi
will also solve an elliptic equation of the schematic form

t^{2}\Delta\partial\upchi=t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta.

(1.55)

The benefit is that in CMCSH gauge, the term
t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta
in (
1.53
) vanishes. Thus in CMCSH gauge (
1.53
), coupled to (
1.54
)–(
1.55
), can be viewed as a first-order elliptic-hyperbolic system.

Let us now see how the high- and low-frequency energy estimates apply to this system.

1.

At high-frequencies
|\lambda|t\gg 1
, we wish to use a similar looking energy of the form

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)=\frac{|\upkappa_{\lambda}|_{g}^{2}}{|\lambda|t}+\frac{\langle\upkappa_{\lambda},\upeta_{\lambda}\rangle_{g}}{|\lambda|t}+\frac{|\upeta_{\lambda}|^{2}_{g}}{2|\lambda|t}+|\lambda|t\cdot|\upeta_{\lambda}|^{2}_{g}.

Here the norm
|\cdot|_{g}^{2}
and the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{g}
indicate one needs a metric to define norms on tensors. As in the wave equation case, we require crucial top order cancellations in order to obtain a good high-frequency energy estimate. In order to achieve the same cancellations in this case, it is essential that we use the CMCSH gauge, in order to remove the top order term
t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta
in (
1.53
).

In this gauge, we get the desired top order cancellations from the
\upeta
and
\upkappa
terms. However, we still need to control the contributions from
\upnu
and
\partial\upchi
. The elliptic equation (
1.54
) suggests that in the
|\lambda t|\geq 1
regime,
\upnu_{\lambda}
is of the same size as
\upeta_{\lambda}
, and thus the
\upnu
appearing in the first equation in (
1.53
) seems to produce a top order contribution not conducive to using Grönwall towards
t=0
.

Fortunately, the Hamiltonian constraint means that we may rewrite the elliptic equation in a different schematic form, namely
(1-t^{2}\Delta)\upnu=\upkappa
. For
|\lambda|t\geq 1
, this tells us that
\upnu
is of the same size as
(|\lambda|t)^{-2}\upkappa
, which in our definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)
behaves better than
\upeta
, and allows us to Grönwall terms involving
\upnu
.

Similar considerations apply for
\partial\upchi
. Though the elliptic equation (
1.55
) suggests
\partial\upchi
is of the same size as
\upeta
, upon taking the derivative
t\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)
, we can apply the CMCSH gauge condition and the momentum constraint to close the energy estimate.

2.

At low-frequencies
|\lambda|t\ll 1
, we first define a quantity akin to
\tilde{\varphi}
from the wave equation case which remain boundeds as
t\to 0
. In the isotropic case, it will suffice to take
\tilde{\Upupsilon}=\upeta-\upkappa\log(\lambda t)
(in the anisotropic setting renormalized quantities are as in (
1.33
)). We employ an energy of the type

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)=|\upkappa_{\lambda}|_{g_{*}}^{2}+|\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda}|_{g_{*}}^{2}.

Note that we use a different norm
|\cdot|_{g_{*}}^{2}
to emphasize that different metrics are used to quantify tensorial objects in the high-frequency and the low-frequency estimates.

Ignoring this subtlety for now, we consider how the different terms in (
1.53
) affect our low-frequency energy estimates. It seems that
\partial\upchi
will be extremely problematic. This is since if one uses CMCSH gauge and the elliptic equation (
1.55
), we expect
\partial\upchi
to have the same size as
\upeta
. However, since it is not
\upeta
but instead
\tilde{\Upupsilon}
that is bounded, a term of order
\upeta
really has a term of order
\upkappa\log(|\lambda| t)
, even in the isotropic case. Therefore this term is not even bounded as
|\lambda|t\to 0
.

We resolve this by using a different gauge, namely the gauge where
\upchi\equiv 0
. Of course, this means that the term
t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta
in (
1.53
) will return. Fortunately, the low-frequency energy estimate did not require top order cancellations for the main terms involving
\upkappa
and
\upeta
, and thus the term
t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta
, which goes to
0
as
|\lambda|t\to 0
, is of no issue.

For terms involving
\upnu
, from (
1.54
), we see
\upnu
is also of order
t^{2}\partial^{2}*\upeta
and presents no further issue in the energy estimates. Thus at least in the isotropic case, we can derive the same estimate
t\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)\leq C(|\lambda|t)^{2}(1+|\log(\lambda t)|^{2})
, and thereby close the argument.

Translating into statements about Sobolev spaces, we can recover a scattering result for the linearized Einstein equations. Note the different gauges used in the high-frequency and low-frequency regimes, and is exactly the reason that we must allow a change of gauge (associated to a vector field
\upxi^{j}
) in Theorem
1.4
.

We briefly comment upon the additional difficulties when the background Kasner is not isotropic. Firstly, in order to obtain the correct top order cancellations at high frequency, we need to tailor the high-frequency energy in a way that sees the anisotropy. This is done by introducing frequency-dependent coefficients
\zeta=\zeta_{\lambda}(t)
into the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}
, see already Lemma
3.1
and Definition
3.1
.

Another difficulty is associated to the tensorial norms
|\cdot|_{g}
and
|\cdot|_{g_{*}}
introduced in the high-frequency and low-frequency energies above. In order to close the estimates,
|\cdot|_{g}
must be associated to the time-dependent metric
\mathring{g}_{ij}(t)
, where
\mathring{g}_{ij}
represents the background Kasner metric. On the other hand
|\cdot|_{g_{*}}
is associated to the metric
\mathring{g}_{ij}(t_{\lambda*})
where
t_{\lambda*}
is as in Definition
1.1
. This is the source of the symbols
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}
appearing in Theorem
1.4
.

Yet another difficulty arises from the fact that our renormalized quantity
\tilde{\Upupsilon}
is not just
\upeta-\upkappa\log(|\lambda| t)
, but a more complicated expression involving powers of
t
. Therefore, the low-frequency energy estimate involves carefully tracking the powers of
t
that arise, in order to ensure that
\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)
is integrable towards
t=0
; this is exactly where we require the subcriticality condition (
1.12
). This concludes our expository discussion of the proof.

1.5
Outline of the paper

•

In Section
2
, we provide further background regarding the Einstein–scalar field system in an ADM-type gauge, and discuss the role played by Kasner and Kasner-like spacetimes in this context. We also provide a review of the literature and briefly discuss the nonlinear scattering problem. Readers only interested in the wave equation (
1.7
) may skip to Section
3
.

•

In Section
3
, we provide a complete proof of our scattering result for the scalar wave equation in (non-degenerate) Kasner spacetimes, Theorem
1.3
. As outlined in Section
1.4
, the main ingredients will be a high-frequency energy estimate and a low-frequency energy estimate for each Fourier mode.

•

In Section
4
, we introduce the linearized Einstein–scalar field system to which our scattering result Theorem
1.4
applies. We derive the full system (including the linearized shift vector
\upchi^{j}
) in Proposition
4.2
, then address several key issues including linearized gauge transformations, well-posedness, and appropriate renormalized variables near
t=0
.

•

In Section
5
, we derive the high-frequency energy estimate which applies to our linearized Einstein–scalar field system. As mentioned in Section
1.4
, this energy estimate uses the CMCSH gauge.

•

In Section
6
, we derive the low-frequency energy estimate which applies to our linearized Einstein–scalar field system. As mentioned in Section
1.4
, this energy estimate instead uses the CMCTC gauge.

•

In Section
7
, we patch together our high-frequency energy estimate and low-frequency energy estimate to complete the proof of Theorem
1.4
. As is turns out, Theorem
1.4
will be derived from an alternative version of our scattering result, Theorem
7.1
, which follows more naturally from our energy estimates.

Notation

Indices:
Lower case Roman letters
a,b,i,j,p,q,
etc. will always denote spatial indices, and vary between
1
and
D
. We use Einstein summation convention with one index up and one index down, and specify cases where there is no summation by underlining the indices, e.g.
t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=-p_{\underline{i}}{\delta}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{j}}
.

Tensors:
From Section
2
onwards, tensors will always be on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, and written with respect to standard coordinates. We generally clarify the rank of tensors by denoting them with indices. For instance,
{\upalpha}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is a
(1,1)
tensor on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, while
\upbeta^{j}
is a vector field.

Math accents:
We often denote variables with accents to signify their features. Expressions with an overhead circle, e.g.
\mathring{g}^{ij}
or
t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
will always refer to background Kasner variables. A hat e.g. on
{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
or
{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
indicates the usage of the CMCSH gauge. A tilde e.g. on
\tilde{\upvarphi}
or
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
indicates the usage of renormalized quantities where the “renormalization time” is not chosen to be
T=1
, see for instance (
4.24
).

Fourier modes:
The letter
\lambda
is reserved for Fourier modes
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
. As in Definition
A.1
, the Fourier series coefficients of a function
f
or a tensor
{\upalpha}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
will be denoted by
f_{\lambda}
or
{(\upalpha_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
respectively.

Constants:
The letter
C
will always be used to denote constants which are dependent only on the exponents of the background Kasner spacetime (and thus independent of the data, as well as the Fourier mode
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
); as per usual we allow
C
to vary between lines.
We write
F\lesssim G
to denote that
F\leq CG
, while
F\asymp G
means that both
F\lesssim G
and
G\lesssim F
.

2
Kasner spacetimes and the ADM equations

In this second expository section, we further detail the ADM-type gauge used to describe the Einstein–scalar field system, then explain the role of the Kasner metrics (
1.1
) and the related Kasner-like metrics.

2.1
The ADM decomposition

To study the linearized Einstein–scalar field system in the generality we require, and to simply fix notation, we shall introduce the ADM-type gauge in more detail than was discussed in Section
1.1
. In this section we introduce also a
shift vector

X^{i}
.

For a spacetime
\mathcal{M}\cong(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{D}
endowed with a timelike function
t
, the standard
(1+D)
-decomposition of the spacetime metric is as follows, where
x^{i}
are standard coordinates on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
.

g=-n^{2}dt^{2}+\bar{g}_{ij}(dx^{i}+X^{i}dt)(dx^{j}+X^{j}dt).

(2.1)

We call this an
ADM-type
gauge, with
n:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
the
lapse function
and
X=X^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}
a vector field tangent to the surfaces
\Sigma_{t}
of constant
t
called the
shift vector field
. The Riemannian metric
\bar{g}_{ij}=\bar{g}_{ij}(t)
on
\Sigma_{t}
is often called the
first fundamental form
.

While the lapse
n=\left(-g(\nabla t,\nabla t)\right)^{1/2}
is determined purely by the choice of time function
t
, the shift vector field
X^{i}
is determined by the choice of coordinates
x^{i}
on each spatial slice
\Sigma_{t}
. The (future-directed) unit normal to the foliation of
(\mathcal{M},g)
by the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
is given by:

\mathbf{N}\coloneqq n^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-X^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\right).

(2.2)

The following definitions are standard for the ADM-type gauge, see e,g,
[
54
, Chapter 2]
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
second fundamental form

k_{ij}
of the foliation of
\mathcal{M}
by
\Sigma_{t}
is a symmetric
(0,2)
-tensor tangent to
\Sigma_{t}
, given by the following:

k(\xi,\eta)=-g(\xi,\mathbf{D}_{\eta}\mathbf{N}),\quad\forall\,\xi,\eta\text{ tangent to }\Sigma_{t}.

The
mean curvature

\tr k=\tr_{\bar{g}}k
of the foliation is then given by
\tr k=(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}k_{ij}
.

Definition 2.2
.

One says that the ADM-type gauge (
2.1
) is
Constant Mean Curvature
(CMC) if the mean curvature of the foliation is constant on each slice
\Sigma_{t}
, with mean curvature
\tr k=-t^{-1}
.

Definition 2.3
.

We say that the gauge (
2.1
) is
Constant Mean Curvature Transported Coordinates
(CMCTC) if it is CMC and also satisfies
X^{i}\equiv 0
. In this case, the coordinates
x^{i}
are transported by the normal vector field
\mathbf{N}
, i.e.
\mathbf{N}x^{i}\equiv 0
for
i=1,\ldots,D
.

On the other hand, we say that the gauge (
2.1
) is
Constant Mean Curvature Spatially Harmonic
(CMCSH) if it is CMC and is such that the coordinate map
(\mathbb{T}^{D},\bar{g}_{ij}(t))\to(\mathbb{T}^{D},\bar{g}_{Euc})
is a harmonic map for all
t\in(0,T)
, where
\bar{g}_{Euc}
represents the standard Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
. In other words,

(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}\left(\Gamma^{i}_{jk}[\bar{g}(t)]-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}[\bar{g}_{Euc}]\right)=0,

(2.3)

where
\Gamma^{i}_{jk}[\bar{g}]
are the Christoffel symbols of the Levi-Civita connection associated to
\bar{g}
, and similarly for
\bar{g}_{Euc}
. (With respect to standard coordinates,
\Gamma^{i}_{jk}[\bar{g}_{Euc}]=0
, but we choose to write (
2.3
) in this form to clarify that it is actually a geometric equation.)

In Proposition
2.1
, we present the (nonlinear) Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) with respect to the ADM-type gauge. The variables
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},n,X^{i})
are used to describe the metric, while the variables
(\phi,\mathbf{N}\phi)
are used to describe the scalar field
\phi
. In Proposition
2.1
, the symbol
\nabla_{i}
will always represent the Levi-Civita connection with respect to the Riemannian metric
\bar{g}_{ij}=\bar{g}_{ij}(t)
. We also raise and lower indices with respect to this metric. In particular,
{k}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
will always mean
(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}
.

Proposition 2.1

(Einstein–scalar field in ADM-type gauge)
.

In a spacetime described by a CMC ADM-type gauge with
k=-t^{-1}
, the Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) are as follows:

1.

The metric variables
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij})
obey the system

\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{ij}=-2nk_{ij}+\bar{g}_{ik}\nabla_{j}X^{k}+\bar{g}_{jk}\nabla_{i}X^{k},

(2.4)

\partial_{t}k_{ij}=-\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}n+n\left(\textup{Ric}_{ij}[\bar{g}]-2\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{j}\phi-2{k}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{\ell}}k_{\ell j}-\frac{1}{t}k_{ij}\right)+X^{k}\nabla_{k}k_{ij}+k_{jk}\nabla_{i}X^{k}+k_{ik}\nabla_{j}X^{k}.

(2.5)

Here
\textup{Ric}_{ij}[\bar{g}]
denotes the Ricci tensor of the spatial metric
\bar{g}
, and may be expressed as

\textup{Ric}_{ij}[\bar{g}]=-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\bar{g}_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\bar{g}_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{i}\bar{g}_{bj}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{j}\bar{g}_{bi}+\mathcal{N}(\bar{g}^{-1},\partial\bar{g}),

(2.6)

where the expression
\mathcal{N}(\bar{g}^{-1},\partial\bar{g})
is a nonlinear term quadratic in the term
\partial\bar{g}
.

If the gauge is moreover spatially harmonic (i.e. satisfies (
2.3
)), then

\textup{Ric}_{ij}[\bar{g}]=-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\bar{g}_{ij}+\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{g}^{-1},\partial\bar{g}),

(2.7)

where
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{g}^{-1},\partial\bar{g})
is a modified nonlinear term, still quadratic in
\partial\bar{g}
.

2.

The matter variables
(\phi,\mathbf{N}\phi)
evolve as follows:

\partial_{t}\phi=n\left(\mathbf{N}\phi+X^{i}\nabla_{i}\phi\right),

(2.8)

\partial_{t}(\mathbf{N}\phi)=n\left((\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\phi-\frac{1}{t}\mathbf{N}\phi+X^{i}\nabla_{i}(\mathbf{N}\phi)\right)+(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\nabla_{i}n\nabla_{j}\phi.

(2.9)

3.

The lapse
n
obeys the following elliptic equation:

-(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}n+\frac{1}{t^{2}}(n-1)=-n\left(\textup{R}[\bar{g}]-2(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{j}\phi\right).

(2.10)

Here
\textup{R}[\bar{g}]=\tr\textup{Ric}_{ij}[\bar{g}]
is the scalar curvature of
\bar{g}
. If the gauge is moreover chosen to be spatially harmonic, then the shift vector
X^{i}
also obeys an elliptic equation as follows.

(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}X^{i}+(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\textup{Ric}_{jk}[\bar{g}]X^{k}=(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\left(2{k}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{a}}\nabla_{a}n+\frac{1}{t}\nabla_{j}n-4n\,\mathbf{N}\phi\,\nabla_{j}\phi\right)\\
-2(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\left(n{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}}+\nabla_{a}X^{c}\right)\left(\Gamma^{i}_{bc}[\bar{g}]-\Gamma^{i}_{bc}[g_{Euc}]\right).

(2.11)

4.

Finally, the following Hamiltonian and momentum constraint equations are satisfied:

\textup{R}[\bar{g}]-{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}{k}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}+\frac{1}{t^{2}}=2(\mathbf{N}\phi)^{2}+2(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{j}\phi,

(2.12)

\nabla_{b}\,{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}=-2\,\mathbf{N}\phi\,\nabla_{a}\phi.

(2.13)

Proof.

The ADM evolution equations (
2.4
)–(
2.5
) and the constraints (
2.12
)–(
2.13
) are standard and follow from a higher dimensional analogue of
[
54
, Section 2.3]
, upon substitution of the energy momentum tensor (
1.3
) and the gauge condition
\tr k=-t^{-1}
. One obtains the elliptic equation (
2.10
) by taking the trace of (
2.5
) and again using the CMC condition. The equation (
2.8
) is a consequence of the expression (
2.2
) for the normal vector field
\mathbf{N}
, while (
2.9
) follows from the wave equation (
1.7
) written in the gauge (
2.1
).

In the case of CMCSH gauge, the relevant equations can be found in
[
8
]
, at least in the vacuum case. See also
[
7
,
32
]
for the addition of matter described by the scalar field
\phi
with energy momentum tensor (
1.3
).
∎

Remark
.

From the PDE viewpoint, it is important to have a local well-posedness theory for the equations of Proposition
2.1
. There are several ways to do this, each of which involves picking a specific spatial gauge. One of these is the CMCSH gauge, where local well-posedness is shown in
[
8
]
. For a more general overview we refer the reader to
[
36
]
.

2.2
Kasner and Kasner-like spacetimes

Let us revisit the Kasner spacetimes in light of the discussion of Section
2.1
. The (generalized) Kasner spacetimes
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
with (
1.1
), (
1.5
), and the Kasner relations (
1.6
), are naturally written in the ADM-type gauge, with:

\displaystyle\mathring{n}\equiv 1,\quad\mathring{X}^{i}\equiv 0,\quad\mathring{\bar{g}}_{ij}=t^{2p_{\underline{i}}}\delta_{\underline{i}j},\quad\mathring{\phi}=p_{\phi}\log t,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\mathring{k}_{ij}=-\frac{p_{\underline{i}}}{t}\mathring{\bar{g}}_{\underline{i}j},\quad\textup{Ric}_{ij}[\mathring{g}]\equiv 0,\quad\mathring{\mathbf{N}}\mathring{\phi}=\partial_{t}\mathring{\phi}=\frac{p_{\phi}}{t}.

(2.15)

The CMC condition
\tr\mathring{k}=-t^{-1}
is a consequence of the first Kasner relation in (
1.6
), while the second Kasner relation in (
1.6
) is due to the Hamiltonian constraint (
2.12
).

The Kasner spacetimes are spatially homogeneous, in the sense that the coordinate vector fields
\partial_{x^{i}}
are Killing fields of
g_{Kas}
, and solve to the Einstein–scalar field equations as stated in Proposition
2.1
. With
\mathring{n}
and
\mathring{X}^{i}
as above, the gauge is moreover both CMCTC and CMCSH. Thus in studying perturbations of the Kasner spacetimes, it is permissible to use either of these gauges.

One should then ask: what do
O(\varepsilon)
-perturbations of Kasner spacetimes to look like? One possibility is that one perturbs a Kasner spacetime into a nearby exact Kasner spacetime with Kasner exponents
q_{i}=p_{i}+O(\varepsilon)
and scalar field coefficient
q_{\phi}=p_{\phi}+O(\varepsilon)
.
However, when the perturbation is not exactly spatially homogeneous one should expect the Kasner exponents
q_{i}
to also depend on the spatial variable
x\in\mathbb{T}^{D}
.

Furthermore, one expects spatial variation in the frame that diagonalizes the second fundamental form. That is, one expects
Kasner-like
metrics
7
7

7

Due to setting
n^{2}=1
in (
2.16
), this ansatz will not generally be CMC. Nevertheless, it suffices to consider such an ansatz for the heuristic discussion below. One notes, for instance, that heuristically the metric is asymptotically CMC in the sense that
\tr k=-t^{-1}+O(t^{-1})
.
of the following form:

g=-dt^{2}+\sum_{I=1}^{D}t^{2q_{I}(x)}\omega_{i}^{I}(x)dx^{i}\otimes\omega^{I}_{j}(x)dx^{j}+l.o.t.

(2.16)

Here the Kasner exponents
q_{I}:\mathbb{T}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
are now functions, while
\omega^{I}(x)=\omega^{I}_{i}(x)dx^{i}
are now
1
-forms on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
which diagonalize
k_{ij}
near
t=0
. Finally,
l.o.t.
represents lower order terms that arise since (
2.16
) without the lower order correction cannot be expected to be an exact solution to the Einstein–scalar field system.

Assuming moreover that the scalar field
\phi
takes the form
\phi=q_{\phi}(x)\log t+r_{\phi}(x)+l.o.t.
for functions
q_{\phi},r_{\phi}:\mathbb{T}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, one initially observes that the CMC condition and the Hamiltonian constraint impose the following generalized Kasner relations on the functions
q_{I}(x)
and
q_{\phi}(x)
:

\sum_{I=1}^{D}q_{I}(x)\equiv 1,\qquad\sum_{I=1}^{D}q_{I}^{2}(x)+2q_{\phi}^{2}(x)=1.

(2.17)

There are a further
D
equations relating the quantities
q_{I}(x)
,
\omega^{I}_{i}(x)
,
q_{\phi}(x)
and
r_{\phi}(x)
which arise from the momentum constraints.

We return to size
O(\varepsilon)
perturbations of Kasner spacetimes. Assuming we have stability, one expects the new exponents
q_{I}(x)
, frame coefficients
\omega^{I}_{i}(x)
, and scalar field functions
q_{\phi}(x)
and
r_{\phi}(x)
, to be
\varepsilon
-close to their analogous values in the background Kasner spacetime, that is,

q_{I}(x)=p_{I}+O(\varepsilon),\qquad\omega^{I}_{i}(x)=\delta^{I}_{i}+O(\varepsilon),\qquad q_{\phi}(x)=p_{\phi}+O(\varepsilon),\qquad r_{\phi}(x)=O(\varepsilon).

(2.18)

In other words, assuming stability,
O(\varepsilon)
-perturbations of the initial data
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
at
t=1
, should lead to Kasner-like spacetimes which are
O(\varepsilon)
close to the background in the above sense.

In such situations, one senses that there might be a one-to-one correspondence between the Kasner-like metrics (
2.16
) and initial data
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
, at least in a neighborhood of exact Kasner spacetimes. One naïve justification is the function counting argument of
[
44
]
: note that there are
D(D+1)+2=D+D^{2}+2
‘‘functional degrees of freedom’’ for initial data
8
8

8

Recall that
\bar{g}_{ij}
and
k_{ij}
are symmetric and therefore have
\frac{D(D+1)}{2}
independent components.

(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
, while amongst the quantities
