(q_{I},\omega^{I}_{i},q_{\phi},r_{\phi})
appearing in the final Kasner-like state, there are also
D+D^{2}+2
“functional degrees of freedom”.

One must also take into account constraint equations and gauge choices in justifying this correspondence. Vaguely speaking, there are
D+2
functional constraints in both cases: at
t=1
one has the Hamiltonian constraint, the
D
momentum constraints, and the constraint arising from the wish to “synchronise the singularity” at
t=0
, while at
t=0
we have the
2
Kasner relations and the
D
momentum constraints.

Supposing we believe this heuristic function counting argument, this provides another interpretation of the scattering problem. Using the scattering terminology of Section
1.1
, this interpretation is as follows:

(i)

(
Existence of the scattering operator
):
For what class of Cauchy data
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
at
t=1
does the corresponding solution to the Einstein–scalar field system attain Kasner-like aymptotics near
t=0
in the above sense, with corresponding Kasner-like quantities
(q_{I},\omega^{I}_{i},q_{\phi},r_{\phi}
)?

(ii)

(
Asymptotic completeness
):
If instead we are given a Kasner-like end state at
t=0
with quantities
(q_{I},\omega^{I}_{i},q_{\phi},r_{\phi})
, when is it possible to find Cauchy data
(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
at
t=1
such that the corresponding solution to the Einstein–scalar field system has the required Kasner-like asymptotics?

(iii)

(
Scattering isomorphism
):
What are the properties of the maps in (i) and (ii)? Can we make sense of it as a map between Banach spaces? In the linearized setting, do we have a Hilbert space isomorphism?

Remark
.

For linearized gravity, the above description of the Kasner-like end state differs somewhat from the asymptotic scattering data that we refer to in Theorem
1.4
, i.e. the renormalized variables
{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
\upphi_{\infty}
, and
\uppsi_{\infty}
.
The reason we use the latter quantities
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})
in place of
(q_{I},\omega^{I}_{i})
, is that
q_{I}
and
\omega^{I}
are secretly eigenvalues and eigenvectors of matrices related to
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})
, and there are losses of regularity
9
9

9

For instance, the map
E:\mbox{Sym}_{D\times D}\to\mathbb{C}^{D}
sending a symmetric
D\times D
matrix to its
D
ordered eigenvalues is known to be only Lipschitz continuous in the neighborhood of matrices with eigenvalues having multiplicity greater than 1.
incurred in this diagonalization process.

2.3
Velocity dominated asymptotics and the BKL conjecture

In claiming the existence of a Kasner-like end state, the discussion of Section
2.2
posits that the background Kasner spacetime around which we are perturbing is in some sense stable. In this section, we outline several heuristics from the physics literature detailing when we expect this to be the case.

Before one asks the question of stability, an more fundamental question is to ask whether a metric of form (
2.16
), complemented by a scalar field of the form
\phi=q_{\phi}\log t+r_{\phi}+l.o.t.
, can be considered as a legitimate approximate solution to the Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.4
).

This metric and scalar field, together with the Kasner relations (
2.17
), solve the so-called
velocity dominated
equations, at least if we ignore terms denoted
l.o.t.
. We explain briefly what we mean by the velocity dominated equations. These are the ADM evolution equations (
2.4
)–(
2.11
), except where we discard any terms involving a spatial derivative of any sort – this includes discarding the term involving the spatial Ricci tensor
\mathrm{Ric}[\bar{g}]
.
In other words, inserting also
n=1
and
X^{i}=0
, we solve the equations

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{ij}=-2k_{ij},\qquad\partial_{t}k_{ij}=-2k_{i}^{\phantom{i}\ell}k_{lj}-\frac{1}{t}k_{ij},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi=\mathbf{N}\phi,\qquad\partial_{t}(\mathbf{N}\phi)=-\frac{1}{t}\mathbf{N}\phi.

Therefore, a reasonable consistency check for the approximation (
2.16
) is that all the discarded terms involving spatial derivatives are indeed suitably small.

This heuristic is reflected in work of Belinskii, Khalatnikov and Lifschitz from the latter half of the 20th century. In
[
44
]
(see also
[
12
,
29
]
), the authors suggest that the required smallness for the most important discarded term, namely the spatial Ricci tensor
\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}[\bar{g}]
, is that for some
\delta>0
, the following bounds hold as
t\to 0
.

|\mathrm{Ric}_{IJ}[\bar{g}]|\lesssim t^{q_{I}+q_{J}-2+\delta}

(2.19)

Here
\mathrm{Ric}_{IJ}
is the projection of the Ricci tensor onto the frame
e_{I}
dual to the
1
-forms
\omega^{I}
.

On the other hand, in the same paper
[
44
]
, the authors use the (approximately) orthonormal frame
e_{I}(x)
to compute the leading order term in
\mathrm{Ric}_{IJ}
. Denoting
\lambda_{IJ}^{K}=\langle[e_{I},e_{J}],e_{K}\rangle
to be structure coefficients of the frame, the resulting computation yields

t^{-p_{I}-p_{J}+2}\cdot\mbox{Ric}_{IJ}[\bar{g}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq K,L,M\leq D\\
L\neq M\end{subarray}}\pm(\lambda^{K}_{LM})^{2}\cdot t^{2(1+q_{K}-q_{L}-q_{M})}\cdot\delta_{IJ}+O(t^{\delta}),

(2.20)

where we remain imprecise about the values of
\pm
. This computation still requires a weaker notion of
asymptotically velocity dominated behaviour
, in assuming that the functions
q_{I}(x)
and
\omega_{i}^{I}(x)
are regular as functions on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, and thus taking derivatives in spatial directions does not add any singular behaviour.

Now compare (
2.20
) to (
2.19
). The key is that, at least in the expected-to-be-generic case where the structure coefficients
\lambda^{K}_{LM}
are non-vanishing, the asymptotics (
2.20
) agree with the smallness in (
2.19
) only if the following
subcriticality condition
holds for some
\delta>0
:

\min_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{D}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq K,L,M\leq D\\
L\neq M\end{subarray}}(1+q_{K}(x)-q_{L}(x)-q_{M}(x))\geq\delta>0.

(2.21)

Therefore, in the absence of any symmetries that would lead to the vanishing of certain structure coefficients, the approximate metric (
2.16
) is an admissible Kasner-like metric if and only if (
2.21
) holds.

We return now to stability of exact Kasner spacetimes with metric (
2.14
) and scalar field (
1.5
). In order to expect a form of stability (outside of symmetry) where perturbations give rise to
admissible
Kasner-like metrics near
t=0
, then in light of (
2.18
) and (
2.21
), one expects to require that for some
\updelta>0
, the background Kasner exponents satisfy the following inequality:

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j,k\leq D\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}(1+p_{i}-p_{j}-p_{k})\geq\updelta>0.

(2.22)

We call this the
subcriticality condition
on the background Kasner exponents, and say that Kasner exponents satisfying (
2.22
) lie in the
subcritical regime
. At the heuristic level, the subcriticality condition seems to be a necessary and sufficient condition for the stability of the Kasner spacetime.

Indeed, the remarkable work of Fournodavlos, Rodnianski and Speck
[
35
]
shows that the subcriticality condition (
2.22
) is sufficient to prove the
nonlinear
past stability of the Kasner spacetimes. To go beyond heuristics to a rigorous proof, a key step of
[
35
]
is to show that the quantities akin to the variables
({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\upvarphi)
to be defined in Section
4
, are regular enough to obey the asymptotically velocity dominated behaviour assumed in the heuristics.

In
[
35
]
, this is done via a carefully chosen hierarchy of energy and pointwise estimates, where the hierarchy is such that high–order derivatives of these quantities may blow up as
t\to 0
, but that low-order derivatives remain bounded in
L^{\infty}
, and remain close to their background Kasner values. Such pointwise control at low order turns out to be enough to verify the BKL heuristics and hence close the argument.

One key new insight of the scattering result of the present article, in comparison to
[
35
]
, is that correctly chosen top-order energies need not blow up as
t\to 0
, at least at the level of linearized gravity. Note, however, that our top-order energies depend sensitively on both time and frequency.

Further remarks concerning the subcriticality condition (
2.22
)

One should comment upon the existence of Kasner spacetimes satisfying (
2.22
) for some
\updelta>0
. For
D=3
, it has been known since
[
44
]
that for
vacuum
Kasner solutions, the subcriticality condition (
2.22
)
never
holds. This remains true for
3\leq D\leq 9
, see
[
29
]
. Thus in these dimensions our result applies only if we introduce also a scalar field
\phi
, as observed in
[
12
]
. For instance, our result applies to the isotropic spacetimes with
p_{I}=\frac{1}{D}
for all
I
and
p_{\phi}=\sqrt{\frac{D-1}{2D}}
. In high dimension
D\geq 10
,
[
29
]
observes there are also vacuum Kasner spacetimes satisfying (
2.22
), and therefore to which our results indeed apply.

In the case that (
2.22
) fails, i.e. if there are
i
and
j\neq k
such that
1+p_{i}-p_{j}-p_{k}\leq 0
, then the Kasner spacetime is expected to be unstable to the past. In this case, the BKL heuristics
[
13
]
predict that generically the spacetime will transition to another Kasner-like regime with different exponents, and these transitions will cascade either indefinitely, or until an Kasner-like end state satisfying (
2.21
) is reached. We refer the reader to
[
11
, Chapter 3]
for more details regarding such BKL transitions.

Velocity dominated behaviour outside the subcritical regime

It remains of interest to study backgrounds where (
2.22
) fails, particularly since this is true for
1+3
-dimensional vacuum spacetimes. In light of (
2.20
), we can only expect asymptotically velocity dominated behaviour in the perturbed spacetime (and in particular no BKL transitions as above), if for any Kasner exponents
q_{I},q_{J},q_{K}
of the perturbed spacetime violating (
2.21
), we have that the corresponding connection coefficient
\lambda^{I}_{JK}
vanishes.

At the linearized level, one can show that the vanishing of
\lambda^{I}_{JK}
corresponds to saying that for any
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
violating (
2.22
), the following limit holds for the linearized Weingarten map
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
:

\lim_{t\to 0}\left(\frac{\partial_{k}{\upkappa}_{{\underline{j}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{j}}}{\underline{i}}}(t)}{p_{\underline{i}}-p_{\underline{j}}}-\frac{\partial_{j}{\upkappa}_{{\underline{k}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{k}}}{\underline{i}}}(t)}{p_{\underline{i}}-p_{\underline{k}}}\right)=0.

(2.23)

This suggests that some version of our scattering result Theorem
1.4
should hold in cases where the above limit holds. We could interpret this hope as a codimensional
10
10

10

Since (
2.23
) must hold at every spatial point
x\in\mathbb{T}^{D}
, this is an infinite codimension statement.
stability and scattering statement for linearized gravity around Kasner spacetimes violating (
2.22
).

In our proof, the only stage where we use (
1.12
) is in the low-frequency energy estimate, in particular the derivative estimate of Propostition
6.4
. A more careful study of this proof shows that our result still holds in the case that (
2.23
) holds
without the limit
i.e. if the expression vanishes for all
t>0
. This is related to known results in polarized
\mathbb{T}^{2}
symmetry or polarized
U(1)
symmetry.

In fact, one could hope to prove a version of Proposition
6.4
where one only assumes (
2.23
), which would involve a detailed analyis of the rate at which the expression in (
2.23
) tends to
0
. We formulate this resolution of codimensional stability and scattering in the following conjecture.

Conjecture 2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
be a (generalized) non-degenerate Kasner spacetime.
not necessarily
satisfying the subcriticality relation (
2.22
). Consider solutions
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
to the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations around
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
, written as the first-order elliptic-hyperbolic system (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) and satisfying suitable constraints. Then we have the following:

(i)

(Existence of the scattering operator)
Let
s
be sufficiently large. Suppose that the Cauchy data
({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})
, satisfying suitable constraints and the spatially harmonic gauge condition (
1.39
), launches a solution to (
1.28
)–(
1.32
) such that for any Kasner exponents
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
violating (
2.22
), the limit (
2.23
) holds.

Then the conclusions of Theorem
1.4
(
i
) still hold, and the limiting quantity
{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is such that for any Kasner exponents
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
violating (
2.22
),

\frac{\partial_{k}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{\underline{j}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{j}}}{\underline{i}}}(t)}{p_{\underline{i}}-p_{\underline{j}}}-\frac{\partial_{j}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{\underline{k}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{k}}}{\underline{i}}}(t)}{p_{\underline{i}}-p_{\underline{k}}}=0.

(2.24)

(ii)

(Asymptotic completeness)
Let
s
be sufficiently large, and let and let
{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
be
(1,1)
tensors in
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, and
\uppsi_{\infty}
,
\upvarphi_{\infty}
be functions in
\mathbb{T}^{D}
with Sobolev regularity as in (
1.43
)–(
1.44
), and satisfying suitable constraints as well as the canonical asymptotic gauge condition (
1.40
).

Suppose furthermore that
{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is such that for any Kasner exponents
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
violating (
2.22
), the identity (
2.24
) holds. Then the conclusions of Theorem
1.4
(
ii
) still hold.

(iii)

(Scattering isomorphism)
For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, recall the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{C,c}^{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\infty,c}^{s}
from Theorem
1.4
(
iii
). Further define
\mathcal{H}_{C,AVTD}^{s}\subset\mathcal{H}_{C,c}^{s}
be the subspace such that the corresponding solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system satisfies (
2.23
)
11
11

11

For
s
small, the convergence in (
2.23
) may hold in a distributional sense.
for Kasner exponents
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
violating (
2.22
). Also define the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\infty,AVTD}^{s}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\infty,C}^{s}
to be the subspace such that for Kasner exponents
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
violating (
2.22
), the identity (
2.24
) holds.

In light of (i) and (ii), define
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}
as in Theorem
1.4
(
iii
). Then
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}:\mathcal{H}_{C,AVTD}^{s}\to\mathcal{H}_{\infty,AVTD}^{s}
is a Hilbert space isomorphism with inverse
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}
.

2.4
Relationship to previous literature

There is a rich mathematical literature regarding problems in general relativity related to the discussions of Sections
1
and
2
. Below, we classify the literature into four broad categories.

Model hyperbolic systems in cosmological spacetimes

The scalar wave equation (
1.7
) in Kasner spacetimes has been studied in
[
2
,
3
,
53
,
56
]
; most of these works show that
\phi
blows up towards
t=0
, as in Theorem
1.1
. In fact similar conclusions may be reached in a wider class of spacetimes which have Kasner-like asymptotics in the sense of the BKL ansatz. See also
[
34
]
for related results in the case of the interior of the Schwarzschild black hole.

The work
[
56
]
in particular provides a comprehensive study of a wide array of linear systems of hyperbolic equations in such backgrounds. The related
[
38
]
provides detailed asymptotics of all orders, and addresses, as an example, Maxwell’s equations on a fixed Kasner background. Note that
[
38
,
56
]
address solving the equations both towards and away from the singularity, though their results are not sharp in terms of regularity.

There is also a vast literature on linear and nonlinear hyperbolic systems in expanding cosmological spacetimes, for instance (asymptotically) de Sitter spacetimes
[
23
,
40
,
64
]
and expanding FLRW cosmologies
[
25
,
24
,
47
]
. As explained in
[
56
]
, these can be likened to the study of spacelike singularities if the analysis is with respect to a conformal time variable. We mention in particular
[
23
]
, which proves a sharp linear scattering theory in de Sitter, and in particular features similar mechanisms as in our proof.

The Einstein equations: from Cauchy data to spacelike singularities

In
D=3
dimensions, heuristics of
[
44
]
suggest solutions the Einstein
vacuum
equations possessing spacelike singularities with convergent Kasner-like asymptotics are expected to be non-generic, as the subcriticality condition (
2.22
) is violated. This remains true for most matter models (although not the scalar field). Therefore, most results regarding the formation of spacelike singularities from Cauchy data occur in symmetry classes where instabilities of
[
44
]
are suppressed.

This includes work on Gowdy symmetric spacetimes
[
22
,
55
]
, where various notions of the strong cosmic censorship conjecture may be proved, as well as work addressing polarized
U(1)
perturbations of the Schwarzschild singularity
[
1
]
. We also mention the study of the Einstein–scalar field system in spherical symmetry, pioneered by Christodoulou
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
, and continued by many other authors
[
26
,
49
,
6
,
50
]
. Our previous result
[
50
]
provides a Kasner-like description of spacelike singularities arising in spherically symmetric spacetimes.

More relevant to our result are the bodies of work addressing stability of singular solutions to the Einstein–scalar field system
outside of symmetry
. The breakthrough results were those of Rodnianski–Speck
[
57
,
58
,
59
]
, who address the linear and nonlinear stability of generalized Kasner spacetimes which are near-isotropic. This was extended to all Kasner spacetimes obeying the subcriticality condition (
1.12
) in
[
35
]
; see also the recent
[
39
]
. Note that in these works the authors use a gauge involving a Fermi-propagated frame in order to capture the subcriticality condition (
2.21
). See also
[
61
,
32
,
14
]
for extensions to other topologies and gauges.

The linearized Einstein–scalar field system we study is essentially the same as that studied in
[
57
]
. Even upon restricting our scattering result to the study of evolution of Cauchy data at
t=1
towards
t=0
, our result improves upon
[
57
]
in that our linear stability result applies to the whole subcritical range (
1.12
), as our energy estimates do not rely on “monotonicity formulae” that apply only to their mildly anisotropic regime.

In all of
[
32
,
35
,
57
,
58
,
59
,
61
]
, the top-order energy estimates used blow-up towards
t=0
; we interpret this as a loss of derivatives in going from Cauchy data to the Kasner-like asymptotics. Theorem
1.4
confirms that this is expected even in the linearized setting, at least when one considers the full range of Kasner exponents obeying the subcriticality condition (
1.12
).

The Einstein equations: Evolving from asymptotic data on spacelike singulatities

The converse direction of our scattering theory addresses prescribing suitable asymptotic data at singularity and solving a “singular initial value problem”. There is a history of such constructions in general relativity, which typically evolves solving so-called Fuchsian PDEs with singular coefficients at
t=0
.

The original results addressed solving from singular initial data in the real analytic class
[
9
,
28
,
46
]
, where a Fuchsian analogue of the Cauchy-Kovalevskaya theorem may be applied. Here
[
9
]
addresses the (nonlinear) Einstein–scalar field system in
D=3
dimensions, while
[
28
]
addresses more general subcritical regimes including higher dimensional spacetimes.

To go beyond real analyticity, one must combine the Fuchsian analysis with energy estimates: as before such energy estimates often allow for a mild rate of blow-up at top-order. In the context of Kasner-like spacetimes with asymptotically velocity dominated asymptotics, there are many examples of such results in various symmetry classes
[
4
,
15
,
18
]
.

We also mention the remarkable
[
33
]
, where the authors evolve from asymptotic data for the Einstein vacuum equations in
D=3
spatial dimensions. They are able to perform this procedure whenever the structure coefficient
\lambda^{K}_{LM}
responsible for the inconsistency in (
2.20
) is made to vanish by a suitable choice of the frame
\omega^{I}(x)
. We consider this a construction of the “non-generic” class of spacetimes in
[
44
]
.

Scattering constructions in general relativity

Scattering theory is ubiquitous in the study of linear and nonlinear evolutionary PDE, see the classical text
[
60
]
, or
[
62
]
for a modern treatment in the context of nonlinear dispersive PDE. The scattering terminology used throughout the article is taken from these texts.

In general relativity, there are many results concerning scattering theory for wave equations in the exterior of black holes, see
[
17
,
37
]
for analysis at the level of modes, and
[
30
,
27
]
for an energy-method approach. There are also scattering results for linearized gravity in the Schwarzschild exterior
[
51
,
52
]
. Going into the black hole interior, there are results
[
43
]
regarding scattering for the scalar wave equation between the event horizon and the Cauchy horizon of subextremal Reissner-Nordström. All of these results address scattering between data on
null
rather than spacelike hypersurfaces.

To our knowledge, our result is the first to determine a sharp scattering isomorphism (of Sobolev spaces) between Cauchy data and asymptotic data in the context of spacelike singularities. However, there is some relation to scattering in (asymptotically) de Sitter spacetimes, where past and future null infinities
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
appear as spacelike boundaries after Penrose compactification, see
[
23
,
63
,
64
]
. The result
[
23
, Theorem 1.2]
in particular features a symbol
\log\nabla
which plays the same role as
\log\mathcal{T}_{*}
in Theorem
1.3
.

2.5
Applications to the nonlinear Einstein–scalar field system

We conclude this section with comments on how our ideas could potentially be applied to the study of Kasner-like singularities for the fully nonlinear Einstein–scalar field system (
1.2
)–(
1.4
). For instance, it would be desirable to formulate a scattering theory relating Cauchy data and asymptotic data in the context of Kasner-like spacetimes. In light of the nonlinear stability result
[
35
]
, one could conjecture that there exists a scattering map between Cauchy data that is a small perturbation Kasner data at
t=1
, and suitable asymptotic data close to the associated Kasner data at
t=0
.

Many additional difficulties arise in addressing the nonlinear problem. In fact, some of these difficulties arise already in
linear
hyperbolic problems where the background Kasner-like spacetime is not exactly
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
, but a non-homogeneous perturbation thereof, since our proof is reliant on a Fourier decomposition on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, and outside of the homogeneous setting one must what consider carefully what operators such as
\log\mathcal{T}_{*}
and
\mathcal{T}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}_{*}
mean. It is possible we must consider microlocal operators with both physical space and Fourier space dependence.

We now comment single out several features of our proof to discuss how they could be adapted to the nonlinear problem. (Or in some cases, linear problems on non-homogeneous spacetimes.)

Renormalized quantities in the nonlinear problem

In Theorem
1.4
, the Cauchy data at
t=1
uses the variables
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi)
while our asymptotic data at
t=0
uses instead the renormalized variables
({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\upvarphi)
, since it is these renormalized variables that remain bounded (and convergent) as
t\to 0
.

In the nonlinear problem, one must determine the correct renormalized variables at
t=0
, or in other words the variables we prescribe as “scattering data”. One proposal is the following:

\displaystyle{K}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t)=(\bar{g}^{-1}(t))^{jk}(tk_{ik}(t)),\quad M_{ij}(t)=\bar{g}_{jk}(t)\cdot{\exp( 2 K(t) \ln t )}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{k}},

\displaystyle\psi(t)=t\mathbf{N}\phi(t),\quad\varphi(t)=\phi(t)-\psi(t)\log t.

Here
\exp
denotes matrix exponentiation. Note it is a consequence of
[
35
, Theorem 6.1]
that these quantities are bounded and convergent as
t\to 0
for perturbations of Kasner spacetimes obeying the subcriticality condition (
2.22
). Thus a candidate nonlinear scattering map could be a map between Cauchy data
(\bar{g}_{ij}(1),k_{ij}(1),\phi(1),\psi(1))
and the asymptotic quantities
({(K_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},(M_{\infty})_{ij},\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})=\lim_{t\to 0}({K}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),M_{ij}(t),\psi(t),\varphi(t))
.

Another subtlety regarding how we use renormalized quantities in our energy estimates is that our low-frequency energy estimates really apply to
({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\tilde{\upvarphi})
, with the tildes signifying that we use
T=\mathcal{T}_{*}
in the definitions (
1.35
).
Thus in a background which is not exactly Kasner, one must also understand how to adapt the renormalized variables in a manner akin to choosing
T=\mathcal{T}_{*}
.

Gauge in the nonlinear problem

In contrast to
[
35
]
where the authors use Fermi-propagated frames, our scattering result Theorem
1.4
employs a transported coordinate ADM-type gauge. In reality, the proof does not really use a transported coordinate gauge but instead a
frequency adapted
gauge, see already Section
7
and Theorem
7.1
.

A quick way of describing this gauge is that it is CMCSH at high-frequency and essentially CMCTC at low-frequency, see Section
1.4
for why this is the case. Both the CMCSH gauge and the CMCTC gauge exist at the nonlinear level, and our method suggests it is reasonable to use a mixture of the two gauges in the nonlinear setting, so long as one makes sense of the frequency-dependent transition between the gauges.

In particular, this has the effect that the spatial Ricci tensor
\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}[g]
can be viewed as an elliptic operator acting on
\bar{g}_{ij}
at high-frequency (see (
2.7
)), but that there is no shift vector
X^{i}
at low-frequency and thus the equation for
\partial_{t}{K}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=\partial_{t}((\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}(t{k}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}))
will remain suitably integrable towards
t=0
.

Norms and energies in the nonlinear problem

In Theorem
1.4
, the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{s}
associated to the scattering data
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\upvarphi_{\infty})
also features the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}
extensively; this arises due to the way that one compares the high-frequency energy and the low-frequency energy and the relevant metrics used to control the tensorial norms. The idea is that high-frequencies use the metric
\mathring{g}_{ij}(t)
, while low-frequencies use
\mathring{g}_{ij}(t_{\lambda*})
.

As a result, the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}
appears, especially when acting on off-diagonal components of tensors. The dependence of
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}
on the Kasner exponents means that if one were instead to consider perturbations of Kasner have spatially dependent exponents, one must be careful about the norms and energies used to control the asymptotic data at
t=0
.

This issue is perhaps easier to resolve in the direction where one solves “forwards” from asymptotic data at
t=0
, since in this context the perturbed Kasner exponents, and thus potentially the nonlinear analogues of
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}
, are given. In the other direction, one has to understand how we can generate the correct such operators in evolution.

The nonlinear terms in the evolution equations

If one can resolve the previous issues (which already play a role in studying linearized gravity around perturbations of Kasner), it remains to control the ‘semilinear’ nonlinear terms in the evolution equations (
2.4
)–(
2.9
).
For instance, there are the
\mathcal{N}(g^{-1},\partial g)
terms appearing in (
2.7
), and terms involving the shift vector
X^{i}
.

Given our time-dependent frequency decomposition, this would involve an extremely careful analysis of the different types of interaction (i.e. high-high, high-low, and low-low) both in terms of regularity and in terms of behaviour in
t
. Similarly, one would have to understand the interaction of commutator terms that arise, including commutation with the suggested microlocal operator
\mathcal{T}_{*}
.

3
Scattering for the scalar wave equation

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.3
, concerning scattering between Cauchy data and asymptotic data for solutions of the wave equation (
1.7
) in a non-degenerate Kasner spacetime
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
. As outlined in Section
1.4
, the proof relies on a detailed ODE analysis for each Fourier mode
(\phi_{\lambda},\psi_{\lambda})
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

3.1
Fourier decomposition and preliminary lemmas

Projecting the first order system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) onto each Fourier mode
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
, we find the following first order system of ODEs for the Fourier coefficients
\phi_{\lambda}
and
\psi_{\lambda}
.

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\phi_{\lambda}=\psi_{\lambda},

(3.1)

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\psi_{\lambda}=-\tau^{2}\phi_{\lambda},.

(3.2)

Here the expression
\tau^{2}=\tau^{2}_{\lambda}(t)
is the following function
12
12

12

When clear from context, we often denote
\tau
without its subscript
\tau_{\lambda}
, primarily to shorten the exposition, and also to make it clear that
\tau_{\lambda}
is not a Fourier coefficient. Similarly for
\zeta
and
\zeta_{\lambda}
.
of
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
and
t>0
.

\tau^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}.

(3.3)

Our aim is to find energy estimates for
\phi_{\lambda}
,
\psi_{\lambda}
and
\varphi_{\lambda}=\phi_{\lambda}-\psi_{\lambda}\log t
which are uniform in frequency
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
. The energies appearing in such energy estimates will often feature
\tau
and derivatives thereof, and thus much of our analysis boils down to understanding properties of
\tau
. We often appeal to the following two lemmas:

Lemma 3.1
.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, let
\zeta=\zeta_{\lambda}(t)
be the following logarithmic
\partial_{t}
-derivative of
\tau
:

\zeta=\zeta_{\lambda}(t)\coloneqq\left(t\frac{d}{dt}\log\tau\right)^{-1}=\frac{\tau}{t}\cdot\left(\frac{d\tau}{dt}\right).

(3.4)

Then the following upper and lower bounds for
\zeta=\zeta_{\lambda}(t)
hold, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
t>0
:

\min_{1\leq i\leq D}\frac{1}{1-p_{i}}\leq\zeta_{\lambda}(t)\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq D}\frac{1}{1-p_{i}}.

(3.5)

Furthermore,
\zeta_{\lambda}(t)
is nonincreasing in
t
, and one has the integral estimate

\int_{0}^{1}|\zeta_{\lambda}^{\prime}(s)|\,ds\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq D}\frac{1}{1-p_{i}}-\min_{1\leq i\leq D}\frac{1}{1-p_{i}}.

(3.6)

Proof.

We differentiate the expression (
3.3
) for
\tau
with respect to
t
. This yields

2t\tau\cdot\frac{d\tau}{dt}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}.

We then use the definition (
3.4
) and rearrange to find

\zeta(t)=\frac{2\tau^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}}=\frac{2\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}}.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, at least one summand appearing in both the numerator and denominator is positive, and from this the bounds (
3.5
) are immediate.

We now show that
\zeta(t)
is nonincreasing. Using the above, we compute the following logarithmic derivative

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}\log(\zeta(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})^{2}t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})^{2}t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}\right)}{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{D}t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{D}(2-2p_{i})t^{2-2p_{i}}\lambda_{i}^{2}\right)}.

The result now follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, i.e. that

\left(\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}^{D}}x_{i}^{2}\right)\left(\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}^{D}}y_{i}\right)^{2}\geq\left(\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}^{D}}x_{i}y_{i}\right)^{2},

with
x_{i}=t^{1-p_{i}}\lambda_{i}
and
y_{i}=(1-p_{i})t^{1-p_{i}}\lambda_{i}
. This shows that the numerator above is nonpositive, and thus that
\zeta(t)
is nonincreasing. This monotonicity and the upper and lower bounds (
3.5
) for
\zeta(t)
immediately yields (
3.6
).
∎

We use Lemma
3.1
, particularly (
3.5
), to estimate integrals involving powers of
\tau
.

Lemma 3.2
.

As in Definition
1.1
, for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, let
t_{\lambda*}\in(0,1]
be such that
\tau_{\lambda}^{2}(t_{\lambda*})=1
. Then for
\alpha>0
, we have the following integral estimate corresponding to
\tau\geq 1
:

\int_{t_{\lambda*}}^{1}\tau^{-\alpha}(s)\,\frac{ds}{s}\lesssim\alpha^{-1}.

(3.7)

One also has the following integral estimate corresponding to
\tau\leq 1
. For
0<t\leq t_{\lambda_{*}}
,

\int_{0}^{t}\tau^{2}(s)\left(1+\log(\frac{t_{\lambda*}}{s})^{2}\right)\,\frac{ds}{s}\lesssim\tau^{2}(t)\left(1+\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t})^{2}\right)\lesssim 1.

(3.8)

Proof.

The key to these integral estimates is that we may perform a change of variables
t\mapsto\tau
, and that

\frac{dt}{t}=\zeta\frac{d\tau}{\tau}.

For instance, for (
3.7
), upon applying this change of variables the upper bound in (
3.5
) yields

\int_{t_{\lambda*}}^{1}\tau^{-\alpha}(s)\,\frac{ds}{s}\leq\int_{1}^{+\infty}\tau^{-\alpha}\,\zeta\frac{d\tau}{\tau}\leq\alpha^{-1}\cdot\max_{1\leq i\leq D}\frac{1}{1-p_{i}}.

For (
3.8
) we start by expressing
\log(\frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t})
in terms of
\tau
. Using the same change of variables, we find

\log(\frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t})=\int^{t_{\lambda*}}_{t}\frac{dt}{t}=\int^{1}_{\tau(t)}\zeta\frac{d\tau}{\tau}.

Using again the upper and lower bounds (
3.5
) on
\zeta(t)
, we thus have that for
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
,

\log\left(\frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t}\right)\asymp\log\tau^{-1}.

(3.9)

Therefore, the integral in (
3.8
) is estimated as follows.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\tau^{2}(s)\left(1+\log(\frac{t_{\lambda*}}{s})^{2}\right)\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\tau(t)}\tau^{2}\left(1+(\log\tau^{-1})^{2}\right)\,\frac{d\tau}{\tau}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau^{2}\left(1+(\log\tau^{-1})^{2}\right).

The estimate (
3.8
) then follows from using (
3.9
) again and that
\tau^{2}\left(1+(\log\tau^{-1})^{2}\right)\lesssim 1
for
\tau\leq 1
.
∎

3.2
The high-frequency energy estimate

We commence the study of the first order ODE system (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
. In the high-frequency regime
\tau=\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq 1
, we make use of the following energy quantity.

Definition 3.1
.

The
high-frequency energy quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)
, used for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq 1
, is given by

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)=\zeta^{2}\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}+\zeta\frac{\psi_{\lambda}\phi_{\lambda}}{\tau}+\frac{\phi_{\lambda}^{2}}{2\tau}+\zeta^{2}\tau\phi_{\lambda}^{2}.

(3.10)

The bounds for
\zeta=\zeta_{\lambda}(t)
obtained in Lemma
3.1
immediately yield the following coercivity of the energy, which will be especially useful to compare
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(\cdot)
to Sobolev norms at
\Sigma_{1}
.

Corollary 3.3
.

One has that uniformly in frequency
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
t\geq t_{\lambda*}
,

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)\asymp\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)}{\langle\tau(t)\rangle}+\langle\tau(t)\rangle\phi_{\lambda}^{2}(t).

In particular, at
t=1
one has
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1)\asymp\langle\lambda\rangle^{-1}\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(1)+\langle\lambda\rangle\,\phi_{\lambda}^{2}(1)
.

The main result of the current section is the following high-frequency energy estimate.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(\phi_{\lambda}(t),\psi_{\lambda}(t))
be a solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.2
). Then the
\partial_{t}
-derivative of the high-frequency energy quantity obeys the following bound, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
t\geq t_{\lambda*}
:

\left|t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)\right|\lesssim\left(t|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t).

(3.11)

There exists some
C_{high}>0
, depending only on the background Kasner spacetime, such that for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
t_{\lambda*}\leq t\leq 1
, one has

C_{high}^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1)\leq C_{high}\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t).

(3.12)

Proof.

We take the
t\frac{d}{dt}
derivative of each expression appearing in (
3.10
), using the equations (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) as well as
t\frac{d}{dt}\tau=\zeta^{-1}\tau
. Isolating the terms where the derivative hits
\zeta(t)
, one has

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}

\displaystyle=\zeta^{2}\frac{2\psi_{\lambda}(-\tau^{2}\phi_{\lambda})}{\tau}-\zeta\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}

(3.13a)

\displaystyle\qquad+\zeta\frac{\phi_{\lambda}(-\tau^{2}\phi_{\lambda})}{\tau}+\zeta\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}-\frac{\psi_{\lambda}\phi_{\lambda}}{\tau}

(3.13b)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\phi_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}}{\tau}-\zeta^{-1}\frac{\phi_{\lambda}^{2}}{2\tau}

(3.13c)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\zeta^{2}\tau\phi_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}+\zeta\tau\phi_{\lambda}^{2}

(3.13d)

\displaystyle\qquad+t\zeta^{\prime}(t)\left(2\zeta\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}+\frac{\psi_{\lambda}\phi_{\lambda}}{\tau}+2\zeta\tau\phi_{\lambda}^{2}\right).

(3.13e)

We shall exploit crucial cancellations in the above expression. We see that

•

The first term in (
3.13a
) cancels the first term in (
3.13d
).

•

The second term in (
3.13a
) cancels the second term in (
3.13b
).

•

The first term in (
3.13b
) cancels the second term in (
3.13d
).

•

The third term in (
3.13b
) cancels the first term in (
3.13c
).

Upon applying these cancellations, we are left with the following:

t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}=-\zeta^{-1}\frac{\phi_{\lambda}^{2}}{2\tau}+\text{\eqref{eq:wave_high_freq_e}}.

The proof of the derivative bound (
3.11
) then follows straightforwardly, after applying Corollary
3.3
and the upper and lower bounds on
\zeta(t)
from (
3.5
).

We shall deduce (
3.12
) by applying Grönwall argument to (
3.11
). For any
t_{\lambda_{*}}\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq 1
and
C
the implied constant in (
3.11
), Grönwall’s inequality yields that

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{2})\leq\exp\left(C\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|\,dt+C\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\tau^{-2}(t)\,\frac{dt}{t}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{1}).

In fact, by applying Grönwall in the opposite direction, the reverse inequality also holds:

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{1})\leq\exp\left(C\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|\,dt+C\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\tau^{-2}(t)\,\frac{dt}{t}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{2}).

Now, from Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
, the exponent

C\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|\,dt+C\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\tau^{-2}(t)\,\frac{dt}{t}\leq C\int_{t_{\lambda*}}^{1}|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|\,dt+C\int_{t_{\lambda*}}^{1}\tau^{-2}(t)\,\frac{dt}{t}

is bounded, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
. The energy estimate (
3.12
) then follows upon taking
t_{2}=1
.
∎

3.3
The low-frequency energy estimates

In the low-frequency regime where
\tau=\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq 1
, we need to use a different energy estimate. Since
\phi_{\lambda}(t)
generally blows up as
t\to 0
, we first introduce a renormalized scalar field:

\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)=\phi_{\lambda}(t)-\psi_{\lambda}(t)\log( \frac{t}{t_{\lambda*}} ).

(3.14)

Note that
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)
is chosen to coincide with
\phi_{\lambda}(t)
exactly at
t=t_{\lambda*}
, and differs from the (Fourier coefficients) of the quantity
\varphi(t)
defined in (
1.19
) by a frequency dependent object. Nevertheless, it will be the correct renormalized quantity for our energy estimates. We use the following low-frequency energy quantity.

Definition 3.2
.

The
low-frequency energy quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)
, used for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
0<\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq 1
, is given by

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)=\psi_{\lambda}^{2}+\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{2}.

(3.15)

Using Corollary
3.3
, the low-frequency energy quantity is such that
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})\asymp\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{\lambda*})
.
The main result of this section is the following low-frequency energy estimate.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
(\phi_{\lambda}(t),\psi_{\lambda}(t))
be a solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.2
). Then the
\partial_{t}
-derivative of the low-frequency energy quantity obeys the following bound, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
:

\left|t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)\right|\lesssim\tau^{2}\left(1+\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t})^{2}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t).

(3.16)

There exists some
C_{low}>0
, depending only on the background Kasner spacetime, such that for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
, one has

C_{low}^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})\leq C_{low}\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t).

(3.17)

Proof.

In the low-frequency regime, our fundamental quantities are now
\psi_{\lambda}(t)
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)
. We start by deriving appropriate evolution equations for these quantities. From (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) and (
3.15
) above we have

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\psi_{\lambda}=-\tau^{2}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}+\tau^{2}\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )\psi_{\lambda},

(3.18)

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}=-\tau^{2}\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}+\tau^{2}\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )^{2}\psi_{\lambda}.

(3.19)

From these equations, we easily compute

t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)=2\tau^{2}\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )\psi_{\lambda}^{2}+2\tau^{2}\left(\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t}^2 )-1\right)\psi_{\lambda}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}-2\tau^{2}\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{2}.

Therefore the derivative bound (
3.16
) is immediate.

The energy estimate (
3.17
) follows as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. Letting
C
be the implied constant in (
3.16
), Grönwall’s inequality tells us that for any
0<t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{\lambda*}
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{2})\leq\exp( C \int_{t_1}^{t_2} \tau^2(t) \left( 1 + \log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )^2 \right) \frac{dt}{t} )\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{1}),

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{1})\leq\exp( C \int_{t_1}^{t_2} \tau^2(t) \left( 1 + \log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )^2 \right) \frac{dt}{t} )\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{2}).

By Lemma
3.2
, the integral in the exponent is bounded, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
0<t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{\lambda*}
. This completes the proof of the energy estimate.
∎

In order to justify the existence of the scattering states
(\psi_{\infty},\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})
, one must also show that the functions
\psi_{\lambda}(t)
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)
attain a limit as
t\to 0
. Furthermore, for the purpose of asymptotic completeness we need to show that we can prescribe the limits
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}
and
(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}
to launch a solution to the ODEs. This is achieved in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.6
.

For some fixed
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, and
C_{low}>0
as in Proposition
3.5
, the following hold.

(i)

Let
(\phi_{\lambda}(t),\psi_{\lambda}(t))
be a solution to the system (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) for
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
. Then there exist real numbers
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}
and
(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}
such that
\psi_{\lambda}(t)
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)
attain these as limits as
t\to 0
, at the following rate

|\psi_{\lambda}(t)-(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}|+|\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)-(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}|\lesssim\tau^{2}\left(1+\log(\frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t})^{2}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}^{1/2}(t_{\lambda*})\lesssim\tau\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}^{1/2}(t_{\lambda*}).

(3.20)

Furthermore, one has that
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}+(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\leq C_{low}\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})
.

(ii)

Conversely, let
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}
and
(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}
be any real numbers. Then there exists a unique solution to
(\phi_{\lambda}(t),\psi_{\lambda}(t))
to the system (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) for
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
such that
\psi_{\lambda}(t)
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)
obey the asymptotics (
3.20
).

For this solution, one has that
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})\leq C_{low}\left((\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}+(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\right)
.

Proof.

We first prove (i). By Proposition
3.5
, we have that the low-frequency energy
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)
is uniformly bounded for
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
by its value at
t=t_{\lambda*}
. Therefore, one sees from the equations (
3.18
)–(
3.19
) that the following derivative bounds hold:

\left|t\frac{d}{dt}\psi_{\lambda}(t)\right|+\left|t\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)\right|\lesssim\tau^{2}\left(1+\log(\frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )^{2}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}^{1/2}(t_{\lambda*}).

This derivative estimate, along with Lemma
3.2
, shows that
\partial_{t}\psi_{\lambda}(t)
and
\partial_{t}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)
are integrable towards
t=0
. Therefore the limits
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}
and
(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}
exist, and moreover satisfy the bounds (
3.20
).
The final estimate
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}+(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\leq C_{low}\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})
follows from the upper bound in (
3.17
) and taking the limit
t\to 0
.

For the converse statement (ii), we require a local existence statement for the ODE system (
3.18
)–(
3.19
) given initial data at
t=0
. Since these ODEs are singular at
t=0
, we cannot apply the usual Picard–Lindelöf theorem. Nonetheless, local existence follows, using the Fuchsian theory of first-order ODEs in Lemma
B.1
.

We apply Lemma
B.1
(ii) with
\mathbf{V}=(\psi_{\lambda},\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda})
and
A=0
. The derivative bounds above imply that we can apply this lemma, with initial data
\mathbf{v}=((\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda},(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda})
, and thereby generate a local solution
(\psi_{\lambda}(t),\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t))
to these ODEs such that
(\psi_{\lambda}(t),\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t))\to((\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda},(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda})
as
t\to 0
, and that this solution is the unique solution attaining the desired limit.

Since (
3.18
)–(
3.19
) are regular for
t>0
are linear in
(\psi_{\lambda},\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda})
, we may extend the solution to
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
, indeed one may extend to the whole spacetime
t>0
. The asymptotics (
3.20
) follow as in (i), and the final estimate
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})\leq C_{low}\left((\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}+(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\right)
follows from the lower bound in (
3.17
)
∎

3.4
Construction of the scattering isomorphism

We now complete the proof of Theorem
1.3
using our energy estimates in Sections
3.2
and
3.3
. We shall first assume smoothness for the solution
(\psi(t),\phi(t))
and derive a priori estimates relating Sobolev spaces defined at
\Sigma_{1}
and Sobolev spaces defined at the
t=0
singularity. From these a priori estimates, the density of smooth functions in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{D})
may be used recover the full scattering theory.

There are two directions for our scattering theory. First we show that in Section
3.4.1
that smooth Cauchy data
(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})
at
\Sigma_{1}
gives rise to a smooth solution to (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) for
t>0
, achieving suitable (smooth) asymptotics at
t=0
with Sobolev bounds, and conversely that one may prescribe smooth asymptotic data
(\phi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
at
t=0
which launches a smooth solution to (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) in the same region, and whose restriction to
\Sigma_{1}
is smooth and satisfies good Sobolev bounds.

Before proceeding, note that our results in Sections
3.2
and
3.3
do not apply to the zero frequency
\lambda=0
, since
\tau(t)
defined in (
3.3
) vanishes. However, the projection of (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) on zero frequency is trivial, as evident from the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
be a solution of the equations (
1.17
)–(
1.18
). Then defining
\varphi(t)
as in (
1.19
), the
0
-frequency Fourier coefficients
\psi_{0}(t)
and
\varphi_{0}(t)
are constant, in particular for all
t>0
:

\psi_{0}(t)=\psi_{0}(1),\qquad\varphi_{0}(t)=\varphi_{0}(1)=\phi_{0}(1).

(3.21)

Proof.

This is immediate from (
1.17
) and (
1.18
) in the case that
\lambda=0
, since in this case
\tau_{0}(t)=0
also.
∎

3.4.1
From Cauchy data to asymptotic data

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\phi_{C}
and
\psi_{C}
be smooth functions on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
. Then there exists a unique smooth solution
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
to the system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) achieving the initial data in the sense

\phi(1,\cdot)=\phi_{C}(\cdot),\qquad\psi(1,\cdot)=\psi_{C}(\cdot).

Moreover, there exist smooth functions
\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{D})
such that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi(t)
as in (
1.19
), the following (strong) convergence holds:

\psi(t)\to\psi_{\infty}\text{ in }H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{D}),\quad\varphi(t)\to\varphi_{\infty}\text{ in }H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{D})\quad\text{ as }t\to 0.

(3.22)

Finally, for the symbol
\log(\mathcal{T}_*)
defined in Definition
1.1
, one has

\|\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\varphi_{\infty}+(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\lesssim\|\phi_{C}\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi_{C}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}.

(3.23)

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of a smooth solution
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
for
t>0
, which attains the initial data
\phi(1,\cdot)=\phi_{C}(\cdot),\psi(1,\cdot)=\psi_{C}(\cdot)
, follows from standard theory for first-order linear hyperbolic systems. We then take the Fourier decomposition.

Since
\phi_{C}
and
\psi_{C}
are smooth, it follows from the definition of the high-frequency energy quantity (
3.10
) and Corollary
3.3
that the energy quantities and Sobolev norms can be related in the following way:

\phi_{0}^{2}+\psi_{0}^{2}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+1}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1)\lesssim\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}\left(\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+2}\phi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)+\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)\right)=\|\phi_{C}\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi_{C}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}.

(3.24)

The next step is to produce the functions
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
. For this, we consider the Fourier coefficients
\phi_{\lambda}(t)
and
\psi_{\lambda}(t)
, which evolve according to (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) when
\lambda\neq 0
. By Proposition
3.4
and Corollary
3.3
, we know that for
t_{\lambda*}\leq t<1
, one has

\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)}{\tau_{\lambda}(t)}+\tau_{\lambda}(t)\phi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1).

(3.25)

Combining this with Proposition
3.5
and the fact that
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{\lambda*}))\asymp\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})
, we find that for
0<t<t_{\lambda*}
, one has

\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)+\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{2}(t)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1).

(3.26)

Here
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)
is defined in (
3.15
), and is related to Fourier coefficients of
\varphi(t)
via
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)=\varphi_{\lambda}(t)+\psi_{\lambda}(t)\log t_{\lambda*}
.

Recall now that Proposition
3.6
(i) asserts the existence of limiting coefficients
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}
and
(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}
such that
\psi_{\lambda}(t)\to(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}
and
\varphi_{\lambda}(t)+\psi_{\lambda}(t)\log t_{\lambda*}=\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(t)\to(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}
as
t\to 0
. Recalling also Lemma
3.7
, we thus define the functions
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
as follows:

\psi_{\infty}(x)=\psi_{0}(1)+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}\,e^{i\lambda\cdot x},\qquad\varphi_{\infty}(x)=\varphi_{0}(1)+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\left((\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}-(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}\log t_{\lambda*}\right)e^{i\lambda\cdot x}.

(3.27)

Note that by definition, the function
\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty}(x)=\varphi_{\infty}(x)+(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}(x)
has Fourier decomposition

\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty}(x)=\varphi_{0}(1)+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}\,e^{i\lambda\cdot x}.

(3.28)

We next prove the regularity of
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
. For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, letting
t\to 0
in (
3.26
), multiplying by
\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+1}
and summing over
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
yields

\left(\psi_{0}^{2}(1)+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+1}(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\right)+\left(\varphi_{0}^{2}(1)+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+1}(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\right)\\[3.00003pt]
\lesssim\phi_{0}^{2}(1)+\psi_{0}^{2}(1)+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+1}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1).

By (
3.24
), this tells us that
\psi_{\infty}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty}=\varphi_{\infty}+(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}
are both in
H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}
, and in fact (
3.23
) holds. Since this is true for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\log\mathcal{T}_{*}:H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\to H^{s}
is bounded for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
(see Lemma
A.1
), we have that
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
are smooth, as required.

The final step is to show the convergence (
3.22
). We write the
H^{s}
-norm of
\psi(t)-\psi_{\infty}
in the following manner:

\|\psi(t)-\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)>1\end{subarray}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\left|\psi_{\lambda}(t)-(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}\right|^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq 1\end{subarray}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\left|\psi_{\lambda}(t)-(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}\right|^{2}.

We estimate the first sum on the right hand side by expanding crudely and using (
3.25
), while for the second sum we use Proposition
3.6
, particularly the estimate (
3.20
). We find the following:

\|\psi(t)-\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)>1\end{subarray}}\left(\tau_{\lambda}(t)\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1)+\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq 1\end{subarray}}\tau_{\lambda}^{2}(t)\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*}).

Using again Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
to deduce
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1)\lesssim\langle\lambda\rangle^{-1}(\psi_{C})_{\lambda}^{2}+\langle\lambda\rangle(\phi_{C})_{\lambda}^{2}
, as well as the fact that
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq\langle\lambda\rangle t^{1-p}
for
p=\max\{p_{i}\}
, we therefore have that

\|\psi(t)-\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\lesssim t^{1-p}\left(\|\phi_{C}\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi_{C}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)>1\end{subarray}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}.

(3.29)

Since we have
\psi_{\infty}\in H^{s}
and
\min\{|\lambda|:\tau_{\lambda}(t)<1\}\to\infty
as
t\to 0
, we conclude that
\psi_{\infty}(t)\to\psi_{\infty}
in
H^{s}
as
t\to 0
.

The convergence of
\varphi(t)
is more complicated due to the additional log factors. Nevertheless, a similar but slightly more involved computation will yield that

\displaystyle\|\varphi(t)-\varphi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)>1\end{subarray}}\left(\tau_{\lambda}(t)(1+(\log t^{-1})^{2})\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1)+\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}(\varphi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 56.9055pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq 1\end{subarray}}\tau_{\lambda}^{2}(t)(1+(\log t_{\lambda*}^{-1})^{2})\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*}).

Using the additional fact (
3.9
), one may show that when
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq 1
, one has
\tau_{\lambda}(1+(\log t_{\lambda^{*}}^{-1})^{2})\lesssim(1+(\log t^{-1})^{2})
. Therefore the analogue of (
3.29
) is that

\|\varphi(t)-\varphi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\lesssim t^{1-p}\,(1+(\log t^{-1})^{2})\left(\|\phi_{C}\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi_{C}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}\\
\tau_{\lambda}(t)>1\end{subarray}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}(\varphi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}.

(3.30)

Since
\varphi_{\infty}
also lies in
H^{s}
, the right hand side converges to
0
as
t\to 0
. This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

3.4.2
From asymptotic data to Cauchy data

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\psi_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
be smooth functions on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
. Then there exists a unique smooth solution
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
to the system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) for
t>0
, which achieves the asymptotic data in the sense that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, one has the (strong) convergence:

\psi(t)\to\psi_{\infty}\text{ in }H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{D}),\quad\varphi(t)\to\varphi_{\infty}\text{ in }H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{D})\quad\text{ as }t\to 0.

(3.31)

Moreover, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
one has the following bound between Sobolev spaces:

\|\phi(1,\cdot)\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi(1,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\varphi_{\infty}+(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

(3.32)

Proof.

For existence, we first decompose
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
into their Fourier modes, and define also for
\lambda\neq 0
,

(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}=(\varphi_{\infty})_{\lambda}+(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}\log t_{\lambda*}.

By definition of the Sobolev norm and the operator
\log\mathcal{T}_{*}
, one has

(\psi_{\infty})_{0}^{2}+(\varphi_{\infty})_{0}^{2}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+1}\left((\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}+(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}\right)=\|\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\varphi_{\infty}+(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

(3.33)

We now appeal to Proposition
3.6
(ii), which states that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, we can find a unique solution to the ODE system (
3.18
)–(
3.19
) which achieves the limits
(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}
and
(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}
as
t\to 0
, at the rate (
3.20
).
Transforming back to
(\phi_{\lambda}(t),\psi_{\lambda}(t))
, we can find a solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) in the whole domain
t>0
. Furthermore, Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
imply that for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
0<t\leq t_{\lambda*}
, one has

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)\lesssim(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}+(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}.

(3.34)

Combining this with Proposition
3.4
, Corollary
3.3
and that
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{\lambda*})\asymp\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t_{\lambda*})
, one has for
t_{\lambda*}\leq t\leq 1
the estimate

\frac{\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)}{\tau_{\lambda}(t)}+\tau_{\lambda}(t)\phi_{\lambda}^{2}(t)\lesssim(\psi_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}+(\tilde{\varphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}^{2}.

(3.35)

We now define our solution
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
to the equations (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) as the sum.

\phi(t,x)=(\varphi_{\infty})_{0}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\phi_{\lambda}(t)e^{i\lambda\cdot x},\qquad\psi(t,x)=(\psi_{\infty})_{0}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\psi_{\lambda}(t)e^{i\lambda\cdot x}.

(3.36)

Let us verify the smoothness of
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
at
t=1
. Using (
3.35
),(
3.33
) and
\tau_{\lambda}(1)\asymp\langle\lambda\rangle
, we get

(\varphi_{\infty})_{0}^{2}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s+2}\phi_{\lambda}^{2}(1)+(\psi_{\infty})_{0}^{2}+\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(1)\lesssim\|\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\varphi_{\infty}+(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

Therefore
\phi(1,\cdot)\in H^{s+1}
,
\psi(1,\cdot)\in H^{s}
, and the estimate (
3.32
) holds. Since this is true for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, these functions are smooth. Furthermore, since the Fourier modes
(\phi_{\lambda}(t),\psi_{\lambda}(t))
satisfy the ODEs (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) for
\lambda\neq 0
, and we may appeal to Lemma
3.7
for the zero mode,
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
is a smooth solution to the first order system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
), as required.

By the construction of
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
and the proof of Proposition
3.9
,
\psi(t)
and
\varphi(t)
also obey the asymptotics (
3.31
). Finally, the fact that
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
is the
unique
smooth solution to (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) obeying these asymptotics follows by studying each Fourier mode for
\phi_{\lambda}(t),\psi_{\lambda}(t)
and appealing to the uniqueness statement in Proposition
3.6
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
, and Lemma
3.7
for
\lambda=0
.
∎

3.4.3
Completion of the proof of Theorem
1.3

All that remains is to extend Proposition
3.8
and Proposition
3.9
to the setting where the functions
\phi(t)
and
\psi(t)
have finite regularity. For the sake of completeness, we provide the standard density argument here:

(i)

(Existence of the scattering operator)
By Proposition
3.8
, we are already done in the case that
(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})
are smooth. Now, for
s>\frac{D}{2}+1
suppose instead that
(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})\in H^{s+1}\times H^{s}
. By the density of smooth functions in Sobolev spaces, there exist smooth functions
(\phi^{(n)}_{C},\psi^{(n)}_{C})
such that

(\phi^{(n)}_{C},\psi^{(n)}_{C})\to(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})\text{ in }H^{s+1}\times H^{s}\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Applying Proposition
3.8
to each of
(\phi^{(n)}_{C},\psi^{(n)}_{C})
, we construct a sequence of solutions
(\phi^{(n)}(t),\phi^{(n)}(t))
to the system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) and a sequence of functions
(\psi_{\infty}^{(n)},\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)})
such that

\|\psi_{\infty}^{(n)}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)}-(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}^{(n)}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\lesssim\|\phi_{C}^{(n)}\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi_{C}^{(n)}\|_{H^{s}}^{2},

and we have the (strong convergence)

\psi^{(n)}(t)\to\psi_{\infty}^{(n)}\text{ in }H^{s},\quad\varphi^{(n)}(t)\to\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)}\text{ in }H^{s}\quad\text{ as }t\to 0.

Furthermore, since (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) is linear we also have the difference estimates of the type

\|\psi_{\infty}^{(n)}-\psi_{\infty}^{(m)}\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|(\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)}-(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}^{(n)})-(\varphi_{\infty}^{(m)}-(\log\mathcal{T}_{*})\psi_{\infty}^{(m)})\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\\[3.99994pt]
\lesssim\|\phi_{C}^{(n)}-\phi_{C}^{(m)}\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi_{C}^{(n)}-\psi_{C}^{(m)}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}.

Therefore, by construction of the sequence
(\phi_{C}^{(n)},\psi_{C}^{(n)})
, it is clear we can obtain
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
such that
(\psi_{\infty}^{(n)},\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)})\to(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
in the space
\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\infty}
defined in (
1.23
), and by similar difference estimates applied to the proof of Proposition
3.8
we obtain a limiting solution

(\phi(t),\psi(t))\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}),

which attain the asymptotics (
1.20
). Since
s>\frac{D}{2}+1
, Sobolev embedding implies that
H^{s-1}\subset C^{0}
and therefore this solution is classical.

(ii)

(Asymptotic completeness)
By Proposition
3.9
, we are done in the case that
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
are smooth. If instead we only have
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
with regularity as in (
1.21
), i.e. we have
(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})\in\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{s}
, then first pick a sequence
(\psi_{\infty}^{(n)},\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)})
of smooth functions such that

(\psi_{\infty}^{(n)},\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)})\to(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})\text{ in }\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{s}\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Now apply Proposition
3.9
to each of
(\psi_{\infty}^{(n)},\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)})
. We obtain a sequence
(\phi^{(n)}(t),\psi^{(n)}(t))
of solutions of (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) which moreover obey the difference estimate

\|\phi^{(n)}(1,\cdot)-\phi^{(m)}(1,\cdot)\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\|\psi^{(n)}(1,\cdot)-\psi^{(m)}(1,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|(\psi_{\infty}^{(n)}-\psi_{\infty}^{(m)},\varphi_{\infty}^{(n)}-\varphi_{\infty}^{(m)})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\infty}}^{2}.

Therefore, by construction of the sequence
(\phi_{\infty}^{(n)},\psi_{\infty}^{(n)})
, we construct firstly a limit
(\phi^{(n)}(1,\cdot),\psi^{(n)}(1,\cdot))\to(\phi(1,\cdot),\psi(1,\cdot))
in the space
\mathcal{H}^{s}_{C}=H^{s+1}\times H^{s}
, and then by applying (i) again, we get a limiting solution
(\phi(t),\psi(t))
to the system (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) as desired.

(iii)

(Scattering isomorphism)
By Proposition
3.8
and Proposition
3.9
, we know that the two maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}:(\phi_{C},\psi_{C})\mapsto(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})\text{ from (i)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}:(\psi_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})\mapsto(\phi(1,\cdot),\psi(1,\cdot))\text{ from (ii)},

upon restriction to the space of smooth functions, are well-defined and are such that both
\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\uparrow}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\downarrow}
are the identity map. Furthermore, the bounds (
3.23
) and (
3.32
), together with the density of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{D})^{2}
in both the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{C}^{\infty}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{s}
, imply that these maps may indeed be extended to be Hilbert space isomorphisms, as required. ∎

4
The Linearized Einstein–scalar field system around Kasner

In this section, we derive the linearized Einstein–scalar field system around Kasner, to which Theorem
1.4
applies. This system, to be presented in full detail in Proposition
4.2
, will include a linearized shift vector, and is thus more general than the setup discussed in Section
1.3
.
After stating the equations, we then comment upon several features of this system, including gauge invariance (see Lemma
4.3
) and well posedness (see Proposition
4.4
), and remark upon how to interpret the system near
t=0
in Section
4.3
.

After finishing this discussion, we project this system in frequency space using Fourier series (see Appendix
A
), in preparation for the energy estimates of Section
5
and Section
6
. Finally, we explain how different gauges and energy estimates will be applied in our proof, which will be completed in Section
7
.

4.1
The linearized Einstein equations

Our aim is to present the linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
1.10
)–(
1.11
) around a fixed Kasner background
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas},\phi_{Kas})
. To make this concrete, we first define several variables which we deem
linearly small
in Definition
4.1
. We then derive the linearized system by Taylor expanding (to first order) the system (
2.4
)–(
2.13
) around a fixed Kasner background with

(\bar{g}_{ij},k_{ij},\phi,\mathbf{N}\phi,n,X^{i})=(\mathring{\bar{g}}_{ij},\mathring{k}_{ij},p_{\phi}\log t,p_{\phi},1,0),

and removing all terms which are quadratic or higher order in the linearly small quantities.

Since the Kasner solution is itself a solution to the nonlinear system (
2.4
)–(
2.13
), the outcome of the procedure described above is a homogeneous linear system of PDEs where the unknowns are the linearly small quantities and the coefficients are related to the background Kasner variables. This system of PDEs is what is presented in Proposition
4.2
.

For a further justification of the linearization procedure, we refer the reader to Section 3 of
[
57
]
. We now define our linearly small variables, which differ slightly from those of
[
57
]
.

Definition 4.1
.

We define the following
linearly small
quantities (see also (
2.15
)):

\displaystyle{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\bar{g}}^{-1})^{jk}(\bar{g}_{ik}-\mathring{\bar{g}}_{ik}),\quad{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\coloneqq t({\bar{g}}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}-t(\mathring{\bar{g}}^{-1})^{jk}\mathring{k}_{ik}=t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}+{p_{\underline{i}}}{\delta}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{j}}.

(4.1)

\displaystyle\upphi\coloneqq\phi-\mathring{\phi},\quad\uppsi\coloneqq t(\mathbf{N}\phi-\mathring{\mathbf{N}}\mathring{\phi})=t\,\mathbf{N}\phi-p_{\phi},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\upnu\coloneqq n-\mathring{n}=n-1,\quad\upchi^{i}\coloneqq tX^{i}.

(4.3)

We also let
\tr\upeta={\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{i}}
and
\tr\upkappa={\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{i}}
be the usual traces.

Lemma 4.1
.

A consequence of the CMC gauge condition
\tr_{\bar{g}}k=-t^{-1}
is that:

\tr\upkappa=0.

(4.4)

On the other hand, the linearized version of the spatially harmonic condition (
1.39
), which we often denote the linearized CMCSH gauge
13
13

13

We always demarcate when we are using a CMCSH gauge by writing the
(1,1)
tensors
{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
with a hat.
, is the following:

2\partial_{j}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=\partial_{i}\tr\hat{\upeta}.

(4.5)

Proof.

The equation (
4.4
) follows immediately by taking the trace of (
4.1
) and using that
\tr_{\bar{g}}k=\tr_{\mathring{\bar{g}}}\mathring{k}=-t^{-1}
. In the case of spatially harmonic gauge, note that in the usual Euclidean coordinates, the condition (
2.3
) may be written as
(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\Gamma^{i}_{ab}[\bar{g}]=0
, or as

\frac{1}{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\left(\partial_{a}\bar{g}_{jb}+\partial_{b}\bar{g}_{ja}-\partial_{j}\bar{g}_{ab}\right)=0.

As the background
\mathring{\bar{g}}_{ij}
is spatially homogeneous,
\partial_{i}\bar{g}_{jk}=\partial_{i}(\bar{g}_{jk}-\mathring{\bar{g}}_{jk})
is linearly small. Writing
\bar{h}_{jk}=\bar{g}_{jk}-\mathring{\bar{g}}_{jk}
, one may then Taylor expand the inverse metric
(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}
to write the above equation as

\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ij}\left(\partial_{a}\bar{h}_{jb}+\partial_{b}\bar{h}_{ja}-\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{ab}\right)=\mathcal{Q}(h,\partial h),

where
\mathcal{Q}(h,\partial h)
is quadratic and higher order in the linearly small quantity
h
. Ignoring this term, and using
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\bar{h}_{ik}
, this above equation becomes

-2(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ij}\partial_{a}{\upeta}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{a}}+(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ij}\partial_{j}{\upeta}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{a}}=0.

Up to multiplication by
\mathring{g}_{ik}
and relabelling indices, this is exactly (
1.39
).
∎

Proposition 4.2

(Linearized Einstein–scalar field system)
.

The linear system of PDEs for the variables
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi,\upnu,\upchi^{j})
obtained by linearizing the ADM system (
2.4
)–(
2.13
) around a background Kasner solution, where the linearization procedure is as described at the start of Section
4.1
, is as follows.

1.

The metric variables
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})
obey the evolution equations

t\partial_{t}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}={\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}){\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}){\upeta}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upchi^{j}-\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\partial_{q}\upchi^{p},

(4.6)

t\partial_{t}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=t^{2}\,{\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}[\upeta]-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\upnu-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}})\partial_{i}\upchi^{a}-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{a}})\partial_{a}\upchi^{j}.

(4.7)

Here, the expression
\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}[\upeta]
is the linearized Ricci tensor, and is defined as

{\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}[\upeta]\coloneqq\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\mathring{g}^{ja}\partial_{i}\partial_{a}(\tr\upeta)-\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{i}\partial_{b}{\upeta}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}}-\mathring{g}^{ja}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{b}}.

(4.8)

In particular, if
{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
moreover satisfies the spatially harmonic condition (
4.5
), then

{\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}[\hat{\upeta}]=\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}.

(4.9)

2.

The matter variables
(\upphi,\uppsi)
obey

t\partial_{t}\upphi=\uppsi+p_{\phi}\upnu,

(4.10)

t\partial_{t}\uppsi=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upphi-p_{\phi}\upnu.

(4.11)

3.

The linearized lapse
\upnu
obeys the elliptic equation

\left(1-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\right)\upnu=-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(\tr\upeta)+2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{i}\partial_{a}{\upeta}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}.

(4.12)

If
{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
moreover satisfies the spatially harmonic condition (
4.5
), this equation simplifies to

\left(1-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\right)\upnu=-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(\tr\hat{\upeta}).

(4.13)

Furthermore, if (
4.5
) holds we have an additional elliptic equation for the shift:

-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upchi^{j}=-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}\upnu-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{k}})\partial_{k}\upnu-4t^{2}\mathring{g}^{pq}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{i}})\partial_{q}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{\hat{\upeta}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi].

(4.14)

4.

As well as the linearized CMC condition (
4.4
), the following constraints are satisfied:

\displaystyle-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(\tr\upeta)+2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{i}\partial_{a}{\upeta}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}+2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{\upkappa}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+4p_{\phi}\uppsi=0.

(4.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{j}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\partial_{i}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{\upeta}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi]-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\partial_{j}(\tr\upeta)=0.

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}{\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}+\mathring{g}^{cd}\partial_{d}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{\upeta}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi]-2\mathring{g}^{ab}\,t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,\partial_{a}{\upeta}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}=0.

(4.17)

As mentioned the solution is in CMCSH gauge if (
4.5
) is also satisfied. In this case, (
4.15
) simplifies to

\displaystyle-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(\tr\hat{\upeta})+2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+4p_{\phi}\uppsi=0.

(4.18)

5.

Finally, there are linearized symmetry conditions for
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
:

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}{\upeta}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}=\mathring{g}^{cb}{\upeta}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}},

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}\left({\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{\upeta}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}\right)=\mathring{g}^{cb}\left({\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{\upeta}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{a}}\right).

(4.20)

Remark
.

We outline some of the main differences from the linearized system derived in
[
57
, Proposition 3.2]
. The first difference is that we use the linearly small quantity
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
, while
[
57
]
directly uses the metric perturbation
\bar{h}_{ij}=\bar{g}_{ij}-\mathring{g}_{ij}
. Secondly, we do not insist on transported coordinates, and therefore allow a linearized shift vector
\upchi^{j}
. Thirdly, we use
\uppsi=\mathbf{N}\phi-p_{\phi}
as our linearly small quantity, in place of
\partial_{t}\phi-\frac{p_{\phi}}{t}
. (
[
57
]
do mention our
\uppsi
as an ‘alternative approach’.)

Proof.

We shall provide detailed proofs of four of the equations in Proposition
4.2
, namely the evolution equations (
4.6
) and (
4.7
), the elliptic equation (
4.14
) for
\upchi^{j}
in CMCSH gauge, and the symmetry condition (
4.20
). The remaining equations may be derived by similar methods and are left to the reader.

As in
[
57
, Proposition 3.2]
, the overall strategy will be to consider the equations in Proposition
2.1
and Taylor expand them around the background Kasner solution, eventually discarding terms which are quadratic or higher in the linearly small quantities of Definition
4.1
. We also make use of
\bar{h}_{ij}=\bar{g}_{ij}-\mathring{g}_{ij}
, and as in
[
57
]
, at least within this proof we shall write
(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}
and
(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}
to denote the inverse metrics.

We start with the derivation of (
4.6
). For this, the first step is to multiply (
2.4
) by
t(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}
. Using the fact that
t\partial_{t}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}=2t{\mathring{k}}_{{\ell}}^{\phantom{{\ell}}{j}}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{k\ell}
, and relabelling indices, we deduce that

-\frac{1}{2}t\partial_{t}((\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\bar{g}_{ik})=-t{\mathring{k}}_{{\ell}}^{\phantom{{\ell}}{j}}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{k\ell}\bar{g}_{ik}+n(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}tk_{ik}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\bar{g}_{i\ell}\nabla_{k}(tX^{\ell})-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\bar{g}_{k\ell}\nabla_{i}(tX^{\ell}).

We now begin to Taylor expand. Writing
\bar{g}_{ab}=\mathring{g}_{ab}+\bar{h}_{ab}
in the first, third and fourth terms of the right hand side, while using
n=1+\upnu
and
(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}=(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}+(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ja}\bar{h}_{ab}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{bk}
in the second term, we have

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\left(-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\bar{g}_{ik}\right)
