\displaystyle=-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-t{\mathring{k}}_{{\ell}}^{\phantom{{\ell}}{j}}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{k\ell}\bar{h}_{ik}+(1+\upnu)\left(t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}+(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ja}\bar{h}_{ab}\,t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{bk}\right)k_{ik}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}(\mathring{g}_{i\ell}+\bar{h}_{i\ell})\nabla_{k}\upchi^{\ell}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}(\mathring{g}_{k\ell}+\bar{h}_{k\ell})\nabla_{i}\upchi^{\ell}.

We now simply use that
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\bar{h}_{ik}
, as well as the fact that modulo quadratic error terms,
\nabla_{a}
can be replaced by
\partial_{a}
since the Christoffel symbols and the renormalized shift
\upchi^{\ell}
are both linearly small. Therefore for
\mathcal{Q}_{\upeta}=\mathcal{Q}_{\upeta}(\bar{h},\partial\bar{h},\upnu,\upchi,\partial\upchi)
a quadratic and higher order nonlinearity,

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}

\displaystyle=t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+2t{\mathring{k}}_{{\ell}}^{\phantom{{\ell}}{j}}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{\ell}}-2t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{bk}k_{ik}{\upeta}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{j}}+t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}\upnu

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 113.81102pt-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\mathring{g}_{i\ell}\partial_{k}\upchi^{\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upchi^{j}+\mathcal{Q}_{\upeta}.

Thereby upon inserting the definition of the linearly small quantity
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
, and removing all quadratic or higher order terms in the linearly small quantities, the equation (
4.6
) follows.

The next equation we derive is (
4.7
). For this equation, we firstly combine the two equations (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) to deduce

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\left(t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}\right)

\displaystyle=-t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}n+nt^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}\mathrm{Ric}_{ik}[\bar{g}]-2nt^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{k}\phi-(n-1)t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}

\displaystyle\qquad+t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}(tX^{\ell})\nabla_{\ell}k_{ik}+t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{k\ell}\nabla_{k}(tX^{\ell})-t(\bar{g}^{-1})^{k\ell}k_{ik}\nabla_{\ell}(tX^{j}).

We now simply insert the definitions of all the linearly small quantities, and replace all
\nabla_{a}
derivatives with
\partial_{a}
derivatives modulo quadratic error terms, to deduce that for some term
\mathcal{Q}_{\upkappa}
quadratic in the linearly small quantities
\upeta
,
\partial\upeta
,
\partial^{2}\upeta
,
\upnu
,
\partial\upnu
,
\partial^{2}\upnu
,
\upkappa
,
\partial\upkappa
,
\upchi
,
\partial\upchi
and
\partial\upphi
,

t\partial_{t}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\mathrm{Ric}_{ik}[\bar{g}]-t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{jk}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\upnu-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\upnu+t{\mathring{k}}_{{\ell}}^{\phantom{{\ell}}{j}}\partial_{k}\upchi^{\ell}-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{\ell}}\partial_{\ell}\upchi^{j}+{\mathcal{Q}}_{\upkappa}.

Upon using (
2.6
) to express
\mathrm{Ric}_{ik}[\bar{g}]
and removing all quadratic or higher order error terms, the equation (
4.7
) follows. (If instead we used (
2.7
) we get the desired result in the case of CMCSH gauge.)

The next order of business is the elliptic equation (
4.14
), assuming the CMCSH gauge condition (
1.39
). We shall derive this equation by using (
2.11
), which we multiply by
t^{3}
and rearrange to get

t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}(tX^{i})=t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\left(2t{k}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{a}}\nabla_{a}n+\nabla_{j}n-4np_{\phi}\nabla_{j}\phi\right)-2nt^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\Gamma^{i}_{bc}[\bar{g}]\\
-2t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}\nabla_{a}(tX^{c})\Gamma^{i}_{bc}[\bar{g}]-t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}\textup{Ric}_{jk}[\bar{g}]tX^{k}-4nt^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}(t\mathbf{N}\phi-p_{\phi})\nabla_{j}\phi.

Now, we observe that the second line of this expression is already quadratic or higher order in our linearly small variables
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi,\upnu,\upchi^{j})
and their derivatives, and we therefore discard it. Similarly, we may Taylor expand all but the final term on the first line, and we deduce that for some
\mathcal{Q}_{\upchi}
which is quadratic or higher order in the linearly small quantities, one has:

t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upchi^{i}=t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ij}(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{a}}\partial_{a}\upnu+\partial_{j}\upnu-4p_{\phi}\partial_{j}\upphi)-2t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\Gamma_{bc}^{i}[\bar{g}]+\mathcal{Q}_{\upchi}.

It remains to expand the expression
t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\Gamma^{i}_{bc}[\bar{g}]
. Using the local coordinate form of Christoffel symbols, and the fact that
\partial_{i}\bar{g}_{jk}=\partial_{i}\bar{h}_{jk}
, we have

-2t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\Gamma^{i}_{bc}[\bar{g}]=-t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}(t{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\left(\partial_{b}\bar{h}_{jc}+\partial_{c}\bar{h}_{jb}-\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{cb}\right).

Since the final parenthesized expression is linearly small, we may replace
(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ij}
and
(t{k}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})
by their background Kasner analogues up to a quadratic error. Using also that
t{k}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is self-adjoint with respect to
\bar{g}_{ij}
, we find that up to a quadratic or higher order error we call
\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma}
, one has

\displaystyle-2t^{2}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{k}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\Gamma^{i}_{bc}[\bar{g}]

\displaystyle=-2t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\partial_{b}((\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ij}\bar{h}_{jc})+\partial_{a}\bar{h}_{jb}+t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ij}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\partial_{j}((\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}\bar{h}_{bc})+\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=4t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\partial_{b}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{i}}-2t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ij}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}})\partial_{j}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{a}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma}.

Combining this with the expression for
t^{2}(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upchi^{i}
above, and discarding the nonlinearities
\mathcal{Q}_{\upchi}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma}
, we get the elliptic equation (
4.14
), as required.

The final equation we derive in detail is the symmetry constraint (
4.20
) for
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
. This arises from the symmetry of the second fundamental form
k_{ij}
, which we write in the following way (as in the elliptic equation for shift, the
(1,1)
-tensor
{k}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
means
(\bar{g}^{-1})^{jk}k_{ik}
.)

(\bar{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{k}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}})=(\bar{g}^{-1})^{cb}(t{k}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}).

Expanding the various terms, we therefore have

((\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}-(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ad}\bar{h}_{de}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{eb})(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}+{\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}})=((\mathring{g}^{-1})^{cb}-(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{cd}\bar{h}_{de}(\bar{g}^{-1})^{eb})(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}+{\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}).

The equation (
4.20
) then follows by inserting the definition of
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
, rearranging, cancelling
(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{ab}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}})=(\mathring{g}^{-1})^{cb}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}})
from both sides, and finally removing quadratic and higher order terms.
∎

4.2
Spatial gauge and well-posedness

Though we have fixed our foliation via the CMC condition
\tr_{\bar{g}}k=-t^{-1}
, there is still gauge freedom in the choice of linearized quantities
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upchi^{j})
in Definition
4.1
and in Proposition
4.2
due to freedom in the choice of spatial coordinates, which we may view as diffeomorphisms of the spatial manifold
\mathbb{T}^{D}
that we allow to be time-dependent.

While one way of removing this freedom by imposing some gauge on initial data as well as in evolution, for instance by specifying a shift vector
\upchi^{j}
or alternatively by imposing the linearized CMCSH condition (
4.5
), it will be useful in our context of this article to allow all such gauge choices, as well as how to change between them. At the infinitesimal or linearized level, this is achieved via a time-dependent spatial vector field
\upxi^{j}
on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, as in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi,\upnu,\upchi^{j})
be a solution to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system in Proposition
4.2
(
not necessarily
obeying the linearized CMCSH condition (
4.5
)). Let
\upxi^{j}
be a possibly time-dependent vector field on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
. Then the system (
4.6
)–(
4.20
) is invariant under the gauge transformation:

\displaystyle{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\mapsto{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\partial_{q}\upxi^{p},

(4.21)

\displaystyle{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\mapsto{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}})\partial_{p}\upxi^{j}-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}})\partial_{i}\upxi^{p},

(4.22)

\displaystyle\upchi^{j}\mapsto\upchi^{j}-t\partial_{t}\upxi^{j},

(4.23)

while the scalar variables
\upphi,\uppsi,\upnu
are left unchanged. In particular,

(i)

For a solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi,\upnu,\upchi^{j})
as above, then applying the above transformation using the choice of vector field
\upxi^{j}
given by inverting the elliptic equation

t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upxi^{j}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\left(\partial_{k}\tr\upeta-2\partial_{\ell}{{\upeta}}_{{k}}^{\phantom{{k}}{\ell}}\right)

yields a solution in linearized CMCSH gauge, via the transformation (
4.21
)–(
4.23
).

(ii)

Let
\upchi^{j}_{\circ}
be a fixed (possibly time-dependent) vector field. For any solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi,\upnu,\upchi^{j})
as above, then for any choice of
T>0
choose the vector field
\upxi^{j}
to satisfy

\upxi^{j}=\int^{t}_{T}\left(\upchi^{j}(s)-\upchi^{j}_{\circ}(s)\right)\frac{ds}{s}.

Then after applying the transformation (
4.21
)–(
4.23
) to our solution, the transformed solution has
\upchi^{j}\equiv\upchi^{j}_{\circ}
. In particular, by choosing
\upchi^{j}_{\circ}\equiv 0
, we can always transform to a solution with zero shift. We say that such solutions are in
linearized CMCTC gauge
.

(Sketch) Proof.

We leave the proof of invariance of the system (
4.6
)–(
4.20
) under the transformation (
4.21
)–(
4.23
) to the reader, and only mention at a heuristic level how the transformation (
4.21
)–(
4.23
) is derived. Essentially, we consider coordinate transformations of the form
\tilde{x}^{j}=x^{j}+\upxi^{j}
. Then the ADM-type metric (
2.1
) may be rewritten as

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=-n^{2}dt^{2}+\bar{g}_{ij}(dx^{i}+X^{i}dt)(dx^{j}+X^{j}dt)

\displaystyle=-n^{2}dt^{2}+\bar{g}_{ij}\left(d\tilde{x}^{i}-\frac{\partial\upxi^{i}}{\partial x^{k}}dx^{k}-\frac{\partial\upxi^{i}}{\partial t}dt+X^{i}dt\right)\left(d\tilde{x}^{j}-\frac{\partial\upxi^{j}}{\partial x^{\ell}}dx^{\ell}-\frac{\partial\upxi^{j}}{\partial t}dt+X^{i}dt\right)

\displaystyle=-n^{2}+\tilde{\bar{g}}_{ij}(d\tilde{x}^{i}+\tilde{X}^{i}dt)(d\tilde{x}^{j}+\tilde{X}^{j})+\mathcal{Q}_{\upxi}(\upxi,\partial\upxi).

In this final line,
\mathcal{Q}_{\upchi}(\upxi,\partial\upxi)
is a quadratic nonlinearity, while the modified first fundamental form
\tilde{\bar{g}}_{ij}
and shift vector
\tilde{X}^{j}
are given by

\tilde{\bar{g}}_{ij}=\bar{g}_{ij}-\bar{g}_{ik}\frac{\partial\upxi^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}-\bar{g}_{jk}\frac{\partial\upxi^{k}}{\partial x^{i}},\qquad\tilde{X}^{j}=X^{j}-\frac{\partial\upxi^{j}}{\partial t}.

One now inserts
\tilde{\bar{g}}_{ij}
and
\tilde{X}^{j}
in place of
\bar{g}_{ij}
and
X^{j}
in the expressions of the linearly small quantities of Definition
4.1
. (One can also suitably modify the second fundamental form). Discarding terms which are quadratic or higher in the original linearly small quantities and
\upxi^{j}
, we are left with (
4.21
)–(
4.23
).

Leaving the remaining details to the reader, we move on to (i) and (ii). For (i), suppose that the change of gauge vector field
\upxi^{j}
satisfies the elliptic equation
t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upxi^{j}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}(\partial_{k}\tr\upeta-2\partial_{\ell}{\upeta}_{{k}}^{\phantom{{k}}{l}})
, and consider the transformed version of
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
, which from (
4.21
) we know to be

{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}={\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\partial_{q}\upxi^{p}.

To check this is now in CMCSH gauge, we want to check that
2\partial_{j}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=\partial_{i}\tr\hat{\upeta}
. For this we compute:

\displaystyle t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}(2\partial_{k}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{k}}-\partial_{i}\tr\hat{\upeta})

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}(2\partial_{k}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{k}}-\partial_{i}\tr\upeta)+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{k}\partial_{i}\upxi^{k}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ik}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\upxi^{j}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\upxi^{k}

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}(2\partial_{k}{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{k}}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ik}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\upxi^{j}=0.

The final equality followed exactly due to the choice of
\upxi^{j}
. This completes the proof of (i). The proof of (ii) is more straightforward; one simply uses the transformation formula for the shift (
4.23
).
∎

Remark
.

In Lemma
4.3
, one may in particular choose a vector field
\upxi^{j}
which is
time-independent
. This has the effect of transforming
{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
but leaving
\upchi^{j}
unchanged. In particular, even in the class of solutions with
\upchi^{j}=0
such gauge changes are still permitted. Gauge changes of this type were discussed previously in Section
1.3.3
and were used in the statements of Theorem
1.4
.

Upon fixing the spatial gauge, by either fixing the shift vector
\upchi^{j}
or by insisting upon the linearized CMCSH condition (
4.5
), we can appeal to the following well-posedness result.

Proposition 4.4

(Well-posedness for the linearized Einstein–scalar field system)
.

Let
({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})
be Cauchy data (for instance at
t=1
) that verifies the constraint equations (
4.4
) and (
4.15
)–(
4.20
), with regularity

({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})\in H^{s+1}\times H^{s}\times H^{s+1}\times H^{s}.

Then both of the following well-posedness statements hold.

(i)

Suppose furthermore that
{(\hat{\upeta}_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
verifies the linearized CMCSH condition (
4.5
). Then there exists a unique CMCSH solution
({\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi(t),\uppsi(t),\upnu(t),\upchi^{j}(t))
to the system (
4.6
)–(
4.20
) for
t\in(0,+\infty)
, where
\upchi^{j}
has zero average
14
14

14

In this paper, the vector fields
\upchi^{j}
and
\upxi^{j}
will always have zero average, meaning their
\lambda=0
Fourier coefficients
\upchi_{0}^{j}
and
\upxi_{0}^{j}
always vanish. Note the zero modes have no affect on how
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
transform.
, and with regularity:

\displaystyle\left({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}),

\displaystyle\left(\upphi,\uppsi\right)\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+1}\times H^{s})\cap C^{1}((0,+\infty),H^{s}\times H^{s-1}),

\displaystyle\upnu\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+2}),\qquad\upchi^{j}\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+2}).

(ii)

Alternatively, let
\upchi^{j}_{\circ}
be any (possibly time-dependent) vector field on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
, with
\upchi^{j}_{\circ}\in C^{0}((0,+\infty),H^{s+2})
. Then there exists a unique solution
({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi(t),\uppsi(t),\upnu(t))
to the system (
4.6
)–(
4.20
) for
t\in(0,+\infty)
, such that
\upchi^{j}\equiv\upchi^{j}_{\circ}
at all times, with the same regularity as above.

Proof.

Let us start with (i). We first use the equations (
4.6
), (
4.7
), (
4.9
), (
4.10
), (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.14
), which is a linear elliptic-hyperbolic system in the background
(\mathcal{M}_{Kas},g_{Kas})
. (Note it is crucial that we used (
4.9
) in place of (
4.8
) since only then does
\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}[\hat{\upeta}]
appearing in
4.7
become a top order term of hyperbolic character.)
Therefore we may apply standard elliptic-hyperbolic theory, e.g. that of
[
8
]
, to deduce that there is a solution to these equations (
4.6
), (
4.7
), (
4.9
), (
4.10
), (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.14
) for
t\in(0,+\infty)
. Moreover, this solution has the regularity claimed in (i).

It remains to check that the constraint equations (
4.15
)–(
4.20
) and the gauge conditions (
4.4
) and (
1.39
) also remain true for
t\in(0,+\infty)
. The propagation of constraints is left to Proposition
C.1
. Finally, uniqueness follows from the elliptic-hyperbolic theory, so long as we fix
\upchi^{j}
to have zero average in order to have uniqueness for solutions of the elliptic equation (
4.14
).

We now move on to (ii). In order to show existence, the first step will be to apply a gauge transformation to the Cauchy data
({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})
using a vector field
\upxi^{j}_{C}
which solves the elliptic equation

\mathring{g}^{ab}(1)\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upxi^{j}_{C}=\mathring{g}^{jk}(1)(\partial_{k}\tr\upeta_{C}-2\partial_{\ell}{(\upeta_{C})}_{{k}}^{\phantom{{k}}{\ell}}).

As in Lemma
4.3
, this has the effect of putting the transformed
{(\hat{\upeta}_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}={(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}(1)\mathring{g}^{jq}(1)\partial_{q}\upxi^{p}
in CMCSH gauge, at least at
t=1
. This allows us to use the existence result in (i), and we generate a CMCSH solution
({\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi(t),\uppsi(t),\upnu(t),\upchi^{j}(t))
for
t>0
which achieves the Cauchy data
({(\hat{\upeta}_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\hat{\upkappa}_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})
.

We now perform another change of gauge to recover the solution we desire. Note that the shift vector
\upchi^{j}(t)
was generated in evolution in CMCSH gauge, and thus cannot be expected to equal
\upchi^{j}_{\circ}
, and also that our CMCSH solution does not match our initial choice of Cauchy data
({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})
. We resolve both these issues by using yet another gauge transformation vector field coming from Lemma
4.3
(ii), namely

\upxi^{j}=-\upxi^{j}_{C}+\int^{t}_{1}\left(\upchi^{j}(s)-\upchi^{j}_{\circ}(s)\right)\frac{ds}{s}.

After applying this gauge transformation to our CMCSH solution
({\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi(t),\uppsi(t),\upnu(t),\upchi^{j}(t))
, it is immediate to see that we obtain a solution satisfying the requirements of (ii).

The regularity is also immediate, and we finally turn to uniqueness. By taking the difference of two such solutions, uniqueness is equivalent to the statement that the only solution to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system with trivial data
({(\upeta_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{C})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{C},\uppsi_{C})=(0,0,0,0)
and vanishing shift
\upchi^{j}(t)=0
being the zero solution.
Suppose, on the contrary, that there exists a non-trivial such solution
({(\upeta_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{(\upkappa_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi_{\circ}(t),\uppsi_{\circ}(t))
.

By uniqueness of CMCSH solutions, we know that this solution must not be CMCSH, hence there exists some time
T>0
such that
2\partial_{j}{(\upeta_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(T)\neq\partial_{i}\tr\upeta_{\circ}(T)
.
Now we apply Lemma
4.3
(i) to this solution – this generates, a new CMCSH solution
({(\hat{\upeta}_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi_{\circ}(t),\uppsi_{\circ}(t),\upnu_{\circ}(t),\upchi^{j}_{\circ}(t))
, via a transformation associated to the (zero average) vector field
\upxi^{j}_{\circ}
which solves the elliptic equation

t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upxi^{j}_{\circ}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}(\partial_{j}\tr\upeta_{\circ}-2\partial_{\ell}({(\upeta_{\circ})}_{{k}}^{\phantom{{k}}{\ell}}).

Since
{(\upeta_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=0
at
t=1
, we must have
\upxi^{j}_{\circ}(1)=0
. On the other hand, by definition of
T>0
, we must have
\upxi^{j}_{\circ}(T)\neq 0
. As a result, there must exist some
\hat{T}>0
such that
\partial_{t}\upxi^{j}_{\circ}(\hat{T})\neq 0
, and therefore by the transformation rule (
4.23
) and the fact that we initially had vanishing shift, we must have
\upchi^{j}_{\circ}(\hat{T})\neq 0
.

However, by
\upxi^{j}_{\circ}(1)=0
the solution
({(\hat{\upeta}_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\circ})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi_{\circ}(t),\uppsi_{\circ}(t),\upnu_{\circ}(t),\upchi^{j}_{\circ}(t))
is a CMCSH solution with trivial data, and hence by uniqueness in the CMCSH gauge we must have
\upchi^{j}_{\circ}(t)=0
for all
t>0
. This contradicts the existence of a nontrivial solution with
\upchi^{j}=0
, and completes the proof of (ii).
∎

4.3
Scattering data at
t=0

The linearly small quantities
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upphi
generically blow up towards
t=0
, and are thus unsuitable choices for scattering data at
t=0
itself. Therefore, in Definition
1.2
, we introduced the renormalized quantities
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upvarphi
, which we recall to be:

{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\coloneqq{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\int^{1}_{t}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}},\qquad\upvarphi\coloneqq\upphi-\uppsi\log t.

In fact, we often entertain a more general form of
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upvarphi
, where the time
t=1
is replaced by
t=T>0
. That is, we introduce the quantities
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\tilde{\upvarphi}
as

{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\coloneqq{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\,\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}},\qquad\tilde{\upvarphi}\coloneqq\upphi-\uppsi\log( \frac{t}{T} ).

(4.24)

These more generally defined quantities will play a role in the following two lemmas.

Part of Theorem
1.4
is that
{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upvarphi
remain bounded near
t=0
, and in fact tend to limits
{(\Upupsilon_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upvarphi_{\infty}
as
t\to 0
. This will also be true for
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\uppsi
, which will tend to limits
{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\uppsi_{\infty}
as
t\to 0
. In this section, we prove two important properties of these renormalized quantities, or more generally the corresponding quantities
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})
for any
T>0
.

Firstly, in Lemma
4.5
, we show that these limiting quantities
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})
also obey constraint equations, analogous to those of (
4.15
)–(
4.20
), which we often denote the
asymptotic constraint equations
.
Then in Lemma
4.6
, we determine how
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
transforms under the (linearized) change of gauge discussed in Section
4.2
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
s
be sufficiently large, and let
({{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi,\uppsi,\upnu,\upchi^{j})
be a solution to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system in Proposition
4.2
, with regularity as in Proposition
4.4
, where the Kasner exponents satisfy the subcriticality condition (
2.22
). For arbitrary
T>0
, suppose furthermore that the quantities
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
\uppsi
and
\tilde{\upvarphi}
remain bounded towards
t=0
, and moreover tend to a limit:

({\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi,\tilde{\upvarphi})\to({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})\quad\text{ in }C^{3}\text{ as }t\to 0.

Then the following
asymptotic constraint equations
hold:

\displaystyle t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\uppsi_{\infty}=0,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\partial_{j}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\partial_{i}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{({\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{\infty})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty}]-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\partial_{j}(\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})=0,

(4.26)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\partial_{a}{(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}+\mathring{g}^{cd}(T)\partial_{d}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty}]-2\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\,t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,\partial_{a}{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}=0,

(4.27)

as well as the following symmetry conditions:

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T){(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}=\mathring{g}^{cb}(T){(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}},

(4.28)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\left({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}\right)=\mathring{g}^{cb}(T)\left({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{a}}\right).

(4.29)

Finally, we have the linearized CMC condition
\tr\upkappa_{\infty}=0
.

Proof.

For (
4.25
) and (
4.26
), the idea is to simply take the usual constraints (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), rewrite them in terms of the renormalized variables
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
\tilde{\upvarphi}
, and then finally take the limit
t\to 0
. For instance, we rewrite the Hamiltonian constraint (
4.15
) using (
1.33
) as

-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}+2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{i}\partial_{a}{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{bp}(s)\mathring{g}^{iq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot\partial_{i}\partial_{a}{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}+2(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}){\upkappa}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+4p_{\phi}\uppsi=0.

We now take the limit of this equation as
t\to 0
. The last two terms will give us the desired (
4.25
), so it suffices to show that the remaining terms tend to
0
.

As
\partial_{a}\partial_{b}{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\partial_{a}\partial_{b}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
are bounded towards
t\to 0
by assumption, it suffices to show that as
t\to 0
,

t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\to 0\quad\text{ and }\quad t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\int^{1}_{t}\mathring{g}_{bp}(s)\mathring{g}^{iq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}=0.

In fact both of these will follow by the non-degeneracy of the Kasner exponents; using a modification of Lemma
6.3
(with
t_{\lambda*}
replaced by
1
in the lemma) to estimate the integral above, one shows these terms are bounded by
\max_{i}t^{2-2p_{i}}(1+|\log t|)
and thus tend to
0
as
t\to 0
.

Moving to the momentum constraint, we rewrite (
4.16
) as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\partial_{i}[(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}){\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\tilde{\upvarphi}]-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\partial_{j}\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}=-\partial_{i}[(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}){\upkappa}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\uppsi]\log t.

Note that we used the fact that
t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}
is non-zero only when
a=b
, hence the integral appearing in (
1.33
) ends up being
\log t
. The right hand side of this rewritten momentum constraint appears exactly in the rewritten Hamiltonian constraint above, and (given that third derivatives of
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
are also bounded) is therefore bounded by
\max_{i}t^{2-2p_{i}}(1+|\log t|^{2})
. Thus taking the limit
t\to 0
yields (
4.26
).

We next move to the symmetry conditions (
4.28
) and (
4.29
). We shall actually prove that for all times
t>0
, we have the symmetry conditions

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T){\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}(t)=\mathring{g}^{cb}(T){\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}(t),

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\left({\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}(t)-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}(t)\right)=\mathring{g}^{cb}(T)\left({\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}(t)-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{a}}(t)\right),

(4.31)

and simply take the limit of these as
t\to 0
.
Note that (
4.30
) is equivalent to
\mathring{g}^{ac}(T)\mathring{g}_{bd}(T){\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}={\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}
, or

\mathring{g}^{ac}(T)\mathring{g}_{bd}(T)\,{\upeta}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}+\mathring{g}^{ac}(T)\mathring{g}_{bd}(T)\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{cp}(s)\mathring{g}^{dq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}={\upeta}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}+\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{bp}(s)\mathring{g}^{aq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}.

(4.32)

We now make the following observation. Using the explicit form of
\mathring{g}_{ij}
in (
2.14
), one has the identity:

\mathring{g}^{ac}(T)\mathring{g}_{bd}(T)\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{cp}(s)\mathring{g}^{dq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}=\mathring{g}_{cp}(t)\mathring{g}^{dq}(t)\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}^{ac}(s)\mathring{g}_{bd}(s)\frac{ds}{s}.

One obtains this identity using a substitution of the form
s\mapsto\tilde{s}=\frac{Tt}{s}
in the integral.

Using this identity, one computes the second term on the left hand side of (
4.32
) to be

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ac}(T)\mathring{g}_{bd}(T)\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{cp}(s)\mathring{g}^{dq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}

\displaystyle=\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}^{ac}(s)\mathring{g}_{bd}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot\mathring{g}_{cp}(t)\mathring{g}^{dq}(t)\,{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}^{ac}(s)\mathring{g}_{bd}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot\left({\upkappa}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{d}}){\upeta}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{p}}){\upeta}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{d}}\right),

where we used the symmetry condition (
4.20
) in the second step. We next observe that we can identify the latter two terms on the right hand side in the following way:

\mathring{g}^{ac}(T)\mathring{g}_{bd}(T)\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{cp}(s)\mathring{g}^{dq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}=\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}^{ac}(s)\mathring{g}_{bd}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}-\int^{T}_{t}s\partial_{s}(\mathring{g}^{ac}\mathring{g}_{bd})\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upeta}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}.

Therefore we obtain (
4.32
) by using the fundamental theorem of calculus, and simply relabelling indices. This completes the proof of (
4.30
). For (
4.31
), we firstly observe that

{\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}){\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}={\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}){\upeta}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}+\int^{T}_{t}s\partial_{s}(\mathring{g}_{bp}(s))\mathring{g}^{cq}\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}.

Therefore integrating by parts and using (
4.20
) in a similar way to before, one eventually finds that

{\upkappa}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}){\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}=\mathring{g}_{bp}(T)\mathring{g}^{cq}(T)\,{\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}){\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}.

From this identity, (
4.31
) follows from (
4.30
). We also deduce (
4.28
) and (
4.29
) by taking
t\to 0
.

Finally, the second momentum constraint (
4.27
) follows from combining (
4.26
) with (
4.28
) and (
4.29
).
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Consider the renormalized quantity
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
defined in (
4.24
), for some
T>0
.
Then the change of gauge associated the vector field
\upxi^{j}
, which transforms
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
as in (
4.21
) and (
4.22
), then transforms the expression
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
as follows:

{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\mapsto{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}(T)\mathring{g}^{jq}(T)\partial_{q}\upxi^{p}.

(4.33)

In particular, choosing
\upxi^{j}
to be a solution of the elliptic equation

\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\upxi^{j}=\mathring{g}^{jk}(T)\left(\partial_{k}\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}-2\partial_{\ell}{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{k}}^{\phantom{{k}}{\ell}}\right),

(4.34)

one can transform
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
in such a way that the transformed version satisfies the differential relation

\partial_{i}\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}-2\partial_{j}{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=0.

Proof.

From the definition (
4.24
), the transformation associated to
\upxi^{j}
transforms
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
as follows:

\displaystyle{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left({\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}(t)\mathring{g}^{jq}(t)\partial_{q}\upxi^{p}\right)+\int^{T}_{t}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot\left({\upkappa}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{s}})\partial_{s}\upxi^{p}-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{s}}^{\phantom{{s}}{p}})\partial_{q}\upxi^{s}\right)

\displaystyle={\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\upxi^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}(t)+\mathring{g}^{jq}(t)\partial_{q}\upxi^{p}+\int^{T}_{t}s\partial_{s}(\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s))\frac{ds}{s}\cdot\partial_{q}\upxi^{p}.

The transformation formula (
4.33
) then follows by applying the fundamental theorem of calculus.

The second part of the lemma, concerning the change of gauge associated to the vector field
\upxi^{j}
solving the elliptic equation (
4.34
), follows from the same argument as Lemma
4.3
(i).
∎

4.4
Fourier decomposition

The proof of the scattering results will involve a Fourier decomposition and detailed ODE analysis for each Fourier mode. For this purpose, we will project the whole linearized Einstein–scalar field system of Proposition
4.2
onto the Fourier mode corresponding to
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
, as below.

Proposition 4.7

(Linearized Einstein–scalar field in Fourier space)
.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
, suppose that
({(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{\lambda},\uppsi_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda},\upxi^{j}_{\lambda})
is the projection of a solution to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
4.6
)–(
4.20
) onto the
\lambda
-Fourier mode.

Our solutions will always be CMC in the sense that
\tr\upkappa=0
, and we say that the
\lambda
-Fourier mode is (linearly) spatially harmonic if the following holds (again the hat denotes CMCSH):

2\lambda_{j}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=\lambda_{i}\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}.

(4.35)

As before, we demarcate the occasions when we assume the CMCSH gauge (
4.35
) using a hat. Then the projected variables
({(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{\lambda},\uppsi_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda},\upxi^{j}_{\lambda})
obey the following equations.

1.

The metric variables
({(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})
obey the evolution equations:

t\partial_{t}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}={(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}){(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}){(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu_{\lambda}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\lambda_{q}\upchi_{\lambda}^{p},

(4.36)

t\partial_{t}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=t^{2}\,{(\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}[\upeta_{\lambda}]+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}\upnu_{\lambda}-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu_{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}})\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{a}+\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{a}})\lambda_{a}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}.

(4.37)

Here, the expression
(\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}_{\lambda})[\upeta_{\lambda}]
is defined by

t^{2}{\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}[(\upeta_{\lambda})]\coloneqq-\tau^{2}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ja}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}(\tr\upeta_{\lambda})+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{b}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ja}\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{b}}.

(4.38)

In particular, if
{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
satisfies the spatially harmonic condition (
4.5
), then

t^{2}{(\widecheck{\textup{Ric}}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}[\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}]=-\tau^{2}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}.

(4.39)

2.

The matter variables
(\upphi_{\lambda},\uppsi_{\lambda})
obey

t\partial_{t}\upphi_{\lambda}=\uppsi_{\lambda}+p_{\phi}\upnu_{\lambda},

(4.40)

t\partial_{t}\uppsi_{\lambda}=-\tau^{2}\upphi_{\lambda}-p_{\phi}\upnu_{\lambda}.

(4.41)

3.

The linearized lapse
\upnu_{\lambda}
obeys the elliptic equation

\left(1+\tau^{2}\right)\upnu_{\lambda}=2\tau^{2}(\tr\upeta_{\lambda})-2t^{2}\tilde{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}.

(4.42)

If
{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
moreover satisfies the spatially harmonic condition (
4.5
), this equation simplifies to

\left(1+\tau^{2}\right)\upnu_{\lambda}=\tau^{2}(\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}).

(4.43)

Furthermore, if (
4.35
) holds we have an additional elliptic equation for the shift:

\tau^{2}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}=\mathrm{i}t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\lambda_{i}\upnu_{\lambda}+2\mathrm{i}t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{k}})\lambda_{k}\upnu_{\lambda}+4\mathrm{i}t^{2}\mathring{g}^{pq}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{i}})\lambda_{q}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-2\mathrm{i}t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\lambda_{i}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi_{\lambda}].

(4.44)

4.

As well as the linearized CMC condition
\tr\upkappa_{\lambda}=0
, the following constraints are satisfied:

\displaystyle 2\tau^{2}(\tr\upeta_{\lambda})-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}+2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+4p_{\phi}\uppsi_{\lambda}=0.

(4.45)

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\lambda_{i}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi_{\lambda}]-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\lambda_{j}(\tr\upeta_{\lambda})=0.

(4.46)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{a}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}+\mathring{g}^{cd}\lambda_{d}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi_{\lambda}]-2\mathring{g}^{ab}\,t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,\lambda_{a}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}=0.

(4.47)

As mentioned the solution is in CMCSH gauge if (
4.35
) is also satisfied. In this case, (
4.45
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\tau^{2}(\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})+2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+4p_{\phi}\uppsi_{\lambda}=0.

(4.48)

5.

Finally, there are linearized symmetry conditions for
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
, which are also propagated:

\mathring{g}^{ab}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}=\mathring{g}^{cb}{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}},

(4.49)

\mathring{g}^{ab}\left({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}\right)=\mathring{g}^{cb}\left({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{a}}\right).

(4.50)

Proof.

The proof follows immediately simply by replacing any spatial derivative
\partial_{i}
appearing in the systen (
4.6
)–(
4.20
) by the symbol
-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}
, and simplifying accordingly.
∎

It is also possible to translate Lemma
4.3
, Lemma
4.5
and Lemma
4.6
into frequency space. To keep things brief, of these we only write the Fourier projections of the asymptotic constraints (
4.25
)–(
4.29
).

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
({(\upkappa_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\infty},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})
be as in Lemma
4.5
, for some
T>0
. Then for
\lambda>0
, the following asymptotic constraints hold between the
\lambda
-Fourier projections
({((\upkappa_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},(\uppsi_{\infty})_{\lambda},(\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda})
:

\displaystyle t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{((\upkappa_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}(\uppsi_{\infty})_{\lambda}=0,

(4.51)

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}{((\upkappa_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\lambda_{i}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{(({\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}(\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}]-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\lambda_{j}(\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda}=0,

(4.52)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\lambda_{a}{((\upkappa_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}+\mathring{g}^{cd}(T)\lambda_{d}[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}\,{((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}(\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda}]-2\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\,t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,\lambda_{a}{((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}=0,

(4.53)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T){((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}=\mathring{g}^{cb}(T){((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}},

(4.54)

\displaystyle\mathring{g}^{ab}(T)\left({((\upkappa_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}}\right)=\mathring{g}^{cb}(T)\left({((\upkappa_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}-2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}\,{((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{a}}\right).

(4.55)

Proof.

Again just replace
\partial_{i}
by
-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}
in Lemma
4.5
.
∎

5
Linearized Einstein–scalar field: the high-frequency energy estimate

In this section, we derive a high-frequency energy estimate similar to that of Proposition
3.4
, for the Fourier modes of the linearized Einstein–scalar field system given in Proposition
4.7
. The high-frequency energy estimates of this section will
always use the CMCSH gauge
, in particular the condition (
4.35
), and we make this clear by denoting
{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
with a hat.

5.1
The high-frequency energy quantity

Definition 5.1
.

The
geometric high-frequency energy quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)
, used for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq 1
large, is given by the following:

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)\coloneqq\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}+\left(\frac{1}{2\tau}+\zeta^{2}\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}.

(5.1)

We also define the
matter high-frequency energy quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)
as follows:

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)\coloneqq\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\tau}\uppsi_{\lambda}^{2}+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}\uppsi_{\lambda}\upphi_{\lambda}+\left(\frac{1}{2\tau}+\zeta^{2}\tau\right)\upphi_{\lambda}^{2}.

(5.2)

Finally, define the
total high-frequency energy quantity
as the sum
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)
.

Remark
.

Since
{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is not self-adjoint with respect to
\mathring{g}_{ij}(t)
, it is not clear a priori that
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\hat{\upeta},high}(t)
is positive definite. The following lemma shows that
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)
is still uniformly coercive, as required for the energy estimates, at least for
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
, where
\mathring{\tau}\geq 1
is a constant independent of
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)
as defined in (
5.1
), and suppose that
{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
satisfy the symmetry properties (
4.49
)–(
4.50
). Then there exists some
\mathring{\tau}\geq 1
, depending only on the Kasner exponents
p_{i}
, such that for
\tau(t)=\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
, one has the following energy coercivity, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
:

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)\asymp\frac{1}{\langle\tau\rangle}\mathring{g}^{ac}(t)\mathring{g}_{bd}(t)\,{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}+\langle\tau\rangle\mathring{g}^{ac}(t)\mathring{g}_{bd}(t)\,{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}.

(5.3)

Similarly, for
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)
, one has the following energy coercivity, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
:

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)\asymp\frac{1}{\langle\tau\rangle}\uppsi_{\lambda}^{2}+\langle\tau\rangle\upphi_{\lambda}^{2}.

(5.4)

Proof.

Using the (Fourier projections) of the symmetry constraints (
4.19
)–(
4.20
), one may instead write the geometric high-frequency energy quantity
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\tau}\mathring{g}^{ab}\mathring{g}_{cd}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}+\left(\frac{1}{2\tau}+\zeta^{2}\tau\right)\mathring{g}^{ab}\mathring{g}_{cd}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}\left[\mathring{g}^{ab}\mathring{g}_{cd}+\zeta\,\mathring{g}^{ab}\mathring{g}_{ce}(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{e}})-\zeta\,\mathring{g}^{ae}\mathring{g}_{cd}(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{e}}^{\phantom{{e}}{b}})\right]{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{c}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}.

Using that
t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is simply the diagonal matrix
-p_{\underline{i}}{\delta}_{{\underline{i}}}^{\phantom{{\underline{i}}}{j}}
, we thus have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{D}\mathring{g}^{ii}\mathring{g}_{jj}\left[\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\tau}\left({(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{2\tau}+\zeta^{2}\tau\right)\left({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)^{2}+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}\big(1+(2p_{i}-2p_{j})\zeta\big)\left({(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)\left({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)\right].

Using Young’s inequality, and the upper bound for
\zeta
in (
3.5
), we find that there exists some
\mathring{\tau}\geq 1
such that for
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
, we have the inequality:

\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2\tau}\left({(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\zeta^{2}\tau\left({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)^{2}\geq\left|\frac{\zeta}{\tau}\big(1+(2p_{i}-2p_{j})\zeta\big)\left({(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)\left({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right)\right|.

Combining all these inequalities as well as (
3.5
) then gives the lower bound in (
5.3
). (We use also that
\tau\asymp\langle\tau\rangle
for
\lambda\neq 0
.) The upper bound in (
3.5
) is more straightforward, as is the coercivity (
5.4
) for
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)
.
∎

The aim of this section will be to prove the following high-frequency energy estimate. As we see shortly, the energy estimate in Proposition
5.2
will follow by summing two derivative estimates for
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)
and
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)
which will be obtained in Proposition
5.7
and Proposition
5.6
respectively.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{\lambda},\uppsi_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda},\upchi_{\lambda}^{j})
be a solution to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
4.36
)–(
4.50
) in
CMCSH gauge
. Then the
\partial_{t}
-derivative of the total high-frequency energy quantity obeys the following bound, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
:

\left|t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)\right|\lesssim\left(t|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|+\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t).

(5.5)

There exists some
\mathring{C}_{high}
, depending only on the background Kasner spacetime, such that for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
obeying
15
15

15

\tau^{2}_{\lambda}(1)=\langle\lambda\rangle^{2}
is greater than
\mathring{\tau}^{2}
for sufficiently large
|\lambda|
. For smaller
\lambda
see the mid-frequency energy estimate of Section
6.4
.

\tau_{\lambda}(1)\geq\mathring{\tau}
, it is true for any
t
with
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
that

\mathring{C}_{high}^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(1)\leq\mathring{C}_{high}\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t).

(5.6)

Proof (assuming Propositions
5.6
and
5.7
).

The derivative estimate (
5.5
) will follow from summing (
5.9
) and (
5.11
) from Propositions
5.6
and
5.7
respectively. We deduce (
5.6
) using a Grönwall argument similar to that of the proof of Proposition
3.12
.
By Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
(in particular (
3.7
) with
\alpha=1
), we have

\int^{1}_{t_{\lambda*}}\left(s|\zeta^{\prime}(s)|+\frac{1}{\tau(s)}\right)\frac{ds}{s}\lesssim 1.

Therefore, applying Grönwall’s inequality to (
5.5
), in both the forwards and backwards directions, yields that there exists some
\mathring{C}_{high}>0
such that for any
t_{1},t_{2}
with
\mathring{\tau}\leq\tau(t_{1})\leq\tau(t_{2})\leq\tau(1)
, one has

\mathring{C}_{high}^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{1})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{2})\leq\mathring{C}_{high}\,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t_{1}).

To deduce (
5.6
), simply set
t_{2}=1
.
∎

The remainder of Section
5
is dedicated to the Propositions
5.6
and
5.7
.

5.2
The
\mathring{g}
-norm and elliptic estimates

For our energy estimates, due to the coercivity in Lemma
5.1
, it will simplify the exposition somewhat to define an inner product on (constant-valued) tensors adapted to the Cauchy slices
(\Sigma_{t},\mathring{g}_{ij}(t))
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\upalpha=\upalpha_{ij\cdots k}^{\phantom{ij\cdots k}pq\cdots r}
be a (constant complex valued) tensor on
\Sigma_{t}
. We define the
\mathring{g}
-norm,
|\cdot|_{\mathring{g}}
, of
\alpha
as follows:

|\upalpha|_{\mathring{g}}^{2}=|\upalpha_{ij\cdots k}^{\phantom{ij\cdots k}pq\cdots r}|_{\mathring{g}}^{2}\coloneqq\mathring{g}^{i_{1}i_{2}}\cdots\mathring{g}^{k_{1}k_{2}}\;\mathring{g}_{p_{1}p_{2}}\cdots\mathring{g}_{r_{1}r_{2}}\;\upalpha_{i_{1}j_{1}\cdots k_{1}}^{\phantom{i_{1}j_{1}\cdots k_{1}}p_{1}q_{1}\cdots r_{1}}\overline{\upalpha}_{i_{2}j_{2}\cdots k_{2}}^{\phantom{i_{2}j_{2}\cdots k_{2}}p_{2}q_{2}\cdots r_{2}},

where
\mathring{g}_{ij}=\mathring{g}_{ij}(t)
and its inverse
\mathring{g}^{ij}
have
t
-dependence as in (
2.14
). If
\upalpha
is a scalar, then
|\upalpha|_{\mathring{g}}=|\upalpha|
.

The following lemma is standard from the theory of tensors in inner product spaces.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\upalpha=\upalpha_{ij\cdots k}^{\phantom{ij\cdots k}pq\cdots r}
and
\upbeta=\upbeta_{lm\cdots n}^{\phantom{lm\cdots n}st\cdots u}
be (constant) tensors on
\Sigma_{t}
. We define the tensor product
\upalpha\otimes\upbeta
and the contraction
\tr\upalpha
as the tensors:

(\upalpha\otimes\upbeta)_{ij\cdots klm\cdots n}^{\phantom{ij\cdots klm\cdots n}pq\cdots rst\dots u}=\upalpha_{ij\cdots k}^{\phantom{ij\cdots k}pq\cdots r}\upbeta_{lm\cdots n}^{\phantom{lm\cdots n}st\cdots u},\quad(\tr\upalpha)_{j\cdots k}^{\phantom{j\cdots k}q\cdots r}=\upalpha_{aj\cdots k}^{\phantom{aj\cdots k}aq\cdots r}.

Then the following inequalities hold regarding the
\mathring{g}
-norms:

|\upalpha\otimes\upbeta|_{\mathring{g}}\leq|\upalpha|_{\mathring{g}}|\upbeta|_{\mathring{g}},\quad|\tr\upalpha|_{\mathring{g}}\leq\sqrt{D}|\upalpha|_{\mathring{g}}.

On the other hand, this next lemma follows from the definition (
3.3
) of
\tau
and (
2.15
):

Lemma 5.4
.

Consider
\lambda_{i}
as a covector in
\Sigma_{t}
. Then the
\mathring{g}
-norms of
\lambda_{i}
and
t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
are as follows:

|\lambda_{i}|_{\mathring{g}}=\frac{\tau}{t},\qquad|t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}|_{\mathring{g}}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{D}p_{i}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq 1.

The
\mathring{g}
-norm notation will prove useful in following elliptic estimates for
\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}
,
\upnu_{\lambda}
and
\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
\tau(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
, the following bounds hold for
\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}
and
\upnu_{\lambda}
.

|\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|+|\upnu_{\lambda}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}^{1/2}(t).

(5.7)

For the shift
\upchi^{j}_{\lambda}
, we have the following:

|\upchi^{j}_{\lambda}|_{\mathring{g}}\lesssim\frac{t}{\tau^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}^{1/2}(t).

(5.8)

Proof.

We start with the
\tr\hat{\upeta_{\lambda}}
estimate, for which we use the linearized Hamiltonian constraint (
4.45
). To illustrate how we use the
\mathring{g}
-norm estimates above, one applies the linearized CMCSH gauge (
4.35
) and rewrites this equation tensorially as

\tau^{2}(\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})=-2\tr( t \mathring{k} \otimes\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda} )-4p_{\phi}\uppsi_{\lambda}.

Therefore, upon application of Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
one has

\tau^{2}|\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|=\tau^{2}|\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|_{\mathring{g}}\lesssim|\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda}|_{\mathring{g}}+|\uppsi_{\lambda}|_{\mathring{g}}.

By the definition of the
\mathring{g}
-norm and the energy coercivity in Lemma
5.1
, one has
|\hat{\upkappa}|_{\mathring{g}}+|\uppsi|_{\mathring{g}}|\lesssim\tau^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}^{1/2}(t)
for
\tau\geq\mathring{\tau}
, thus the bound
|\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|\lesssim\tau^{-\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}^{1/2}(t)
follows.

The same estimate for
|\upnu_{\lambda}|
follows from the elliptic equation for the lapse (
4.42
); in the
\tau\geq\mathring{\tau}
regime this equation tells us that
|\upnu_{\lambda}|\leq|\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|
. This completes the proof of (
5.7
).

For (
5.8
), we write (
4.14
) in the tensorial form.

\tau^{2}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\left[\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}+2\mathrm{i}(t\mathring{k}\otimes\lambda)_{i}\right]\upnu_{\lambda}+4t^{2}\mathring{g}^{pq}(\mathrm{i}\lambda)_{q}{(t\mathring{k}\otimes\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}-2t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}(\mathrm{i}\lambda)_{i}\cdot\left[\tr( t \mathring{k} \otimes\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda} )+2p_{\phi}\upphi_{\lambda}\right].

Using Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
, we therefore find that

\tau^{2}|\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}|_{\mathring{g}}\lesssim\tau t\left(|\upnu_{\lambda}|+|\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|_{\mathring{g}}+|\upphi_{\lambda}|\right).

Using (
5.7
) to estimate
|\upnu_{\lambda}|
and Lemma
5.1
to get
|\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|_{\mathring{g}}+|\uppsi_{\lambda}|\lesssim\tau^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}^{1/2}
, the bound (
5.8
) follows. This completes the high-frequency elliptic estimates.
∎

5.3
Estimates for the matter terms

Here, we prove the high-frequency energy estimate for the matter, which is analogous to the proof of Proposition
3.4
, except for an argument to deal with the lapse
\upnu_{\lambda}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

For
({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{\lambda},\uppsi_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda},\upchi_{\lambda})
as in Proposition
5.2
, the following derivative estimate holds for
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)
, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
and
\tau(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
:

\left|t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}(t)\right|\lesssim\left(t|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|+\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t).

(5.9)

Proof.

Following the proof of Proposition
3.4
, we compute
t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}
using (
4.36
)–(
4.50
) as follows:

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,high}

\displaystyle=\zeta^{2}\frac{2\uppsi_{\lambda}(-\tau^{2}\upphi_{\lambda})}{\tau}-\zeta^{2}p_{\phi}\frac{2\uppsi_{\lambda}\upnu_{\lambda}}{\tau}-\zeta\frac{\uppsi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}

(5.10a)

\displaystyle\qquad+\zeta\frac{\upphi_{\lambda}(-\tau^{2}\upphi_{\lambda})}{\tau}+\zeta\frac{\uppsi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}+\zeta p_{\phi}\frac{(\uppsi_{\lambda}-\upphi_{\lambda})\upnu_{\lambda}}{\tau}-\frac{\uppsi_{\lambda}\upphi_{\lambda}}{\tau}

(5.10b)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\upphi_{\lambda}\uppsi_{\lambda}}{\tau}+p_{\phi}\frac{\upphi_{\lambda}\upnu_{\lambda}}{\tau}-\zeta^{-1}\frac{\upphi_{\lambda}^{2}}{2\tau}

(5.10c)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\zeta^{2}\tau\upphi_{\lambda}\uppsi_{\lambda}+2\zeta^{2}p_{\phi}\tau\upphi_{\lambda}\upnu_{\lambda}+\zeta\tau\upphi_{\lambda}^{2}

(5.10d)

\displaystyle\qquad+t\zeta^{\prime}(t)\left(2\zeta\frac{\uppsi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}+\frac{\uppsi_{\lambda}\upphi_{\lambda}}{\tau}+2\zeta\tau\upphi_{\lambda}^{2}\right).

(5.10e)

We now exploit the same cancellations as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. The difference is that we now have some additional terms related to the linearized lapse
\upnu_{\lambda}
. After the cancellations, one has

t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}=-\zeta^{-1}\frac{\upphi_{\lambda}^{2}}{2\tau}+\text{\eqref{eq:matter_high_freq_e}}+\mathfrak{E}_{\upnu},\qquad|\mathfrak{E}_{\upnu}|\lesssim\frac{|\uppsi_{\lambda}\upnu_{\lambda}|}{\tau}+\tau|\upphi_{\lambda}\upnu_{\lambda}|.

As before, it is straightforward to bound (
5.10e
) by
t|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}
, while Proposition
5.5
and Lemma
5.1
allow us to estimate the final term
\mathfrak{E}_{\upnu}
by

|\mathfrak{E}_{\upnu}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\left(\frac{\uppsi_{\lambda}^{2}}{\tau}+\tau\upphi_{\lambda}^{2}\right)+\tau^{2}\upnu_{\lambda}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}.

Combining all of these, we deduce the derivative estimate (
5.9
).
∎

5.4
Estimates for the metric terms

Next, we prove the high frequency energy estimate for the metric quantities
({\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})
, which is similar to Proposition
5.2
but crucially uses the CMCSH gauge and cancellations due to symmetry via (
4.49
)–(
4.50
).

Proposition 5.7
.

For
({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{\lambda},\uppsi_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda},\upchi_{\lambda})
as in Proposition
5.2
, the following derivative estimate holds for
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)
, uniformly in
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
and
\tau(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}
:

\left|t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}(t)\right|\lesssim\left(t|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|+\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}(t).

(5.11)

Proof.

Using the system (
4.36
)–(
4.50
), we calculate the derivative
t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}
as follows, where we group separately the main terms involving
({(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})
, the terms involving the lapse
\upnu_{\lambda}
and the shift
\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}
, and finally the terms where the derivative falls on
\zeta
.

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\zeta^{2}}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}(-\tau^{2}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})-\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}

(5.12a)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2\zeta^{2}}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right]\upnu_{\lambda}+\frac{2\zeta^{2}}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[-\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}})\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{a}+\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{a}})\lambda_{a}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}\right]

(5.12b)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}(-\tau^{2}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})-\frac{1}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}

(5.12c)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}-t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right]\upnu_{\lambda}+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[-\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}})\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{a}+\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{a}})\lambda_{a}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}\right]

(5.12d)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}\right]

(5.12e)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu_{\lambda}+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\lambda_{q}\upchi_{\lambda}^{p}\right]

(5.12f)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}\right]

(5.12g)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(-\frac{1}{2\zeta\tau}+\zeta\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}

(5.12h)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu_{\lambda}+\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\lambda_{q}\upchi_{\lambda}^{p}\right]

(5.12i)

\displaystyle\qquad+t\zeta^{\prime}\left[\frac{2\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{1}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+2\zeta\tau{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right]

(5.12j)

The terms (
5.12a
), (
5.12c
), (
5.12e
), (
5.12g
) and (
5.12h
) are what we deem the
main terms
. They feature the following crucial cancellations:

•

The first term in (
5.12a
) cancels the part of the first term in (
5.12g
) featuring
2\zeta^{2}\tau
.

•

The second term in (
5.12a
) cancels the first term in (
5.12e
).

•

The first term in (
5.12c
) cancels the part of (
5.12h
) featuring
2\zeta^{2}\tau
.

•

The second term in (
5.12c
) cancels the part of the first term in (
5.12g
) featuring
\frac{1}{\tau}
.

•

We leave the remaining term in (
5.12e
) alone, while we notice upon relabelling indices, the remaining term in (
5.12g
) cancels itself. In other words,

{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}\right]=\tr( \hat{\upeta}_{\lambda} \cdot 2 t \mathring{k} \cdot\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda} )-\tr( 2 t \mathring{k} \cdot\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda} \cdot\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda} )=0

Upon realizing these cancellations, we rewrite the sum of the main terms as follows:

\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_a}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_c}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_e}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_g}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_h}}=-\frac{1}{2\zeta\tau}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\frac{\zeta}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{p}}){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{p}}^{\phantom{{p}}{j}}\right]

and hence using Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
as well as the energy coercivity in Lemma
5.1
, one has

|\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_a}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_c}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_e}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_g}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_h}}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}.

(5.13)

We now move onto the terms (
5.12b
), (
5.12d
), (
5.12f
) and (
5.12i
), which are the
terms involving the lapse and the shift
. In order to estimate these terms, we will have to use the linearized momentum constraint (
4.46
) as well as the linearized CMCSH gauge condition (
4.35
).

•

We start with the first term of (
5.12b
). Using the linearized momentum constraint (
4.46
),

\frac{1}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}\upnu_{\lambda}=-\tau[t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi_{\lambda}]\upnu_{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\tau}t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}(\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})\upnu_{\lambda}.

We can now estimate this using Lemmas
5.3
,
5.4
and the elliptic estimates in Proposition
5.5
. The result is the following:

\left|\frac{2\zeta^{2}}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}\upnu_{\lambda}\right|\lesssim\tau\cdot(|{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}|_{\mathring{g}}+|\upphi|)\cdot|\upnu_{\lambda}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}.

The remaining terms of (
5.12b
) can be estimated immediately using the
\mathring{g}
-norm and the elliptic estimates of Proposition
5.5
. As an illustrative example, we have

\left|\frac{2\zeta^{2}}{\tau}{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}})\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{a}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\cdot|{(\hat{\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}|_{\mathring{g}}\cdot|\lambda_{i}|_{\mathring{g}}\cdot|\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}|_{\mathring{g}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}.

At the end, we find the estimate
|\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_b}}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}
.

•

The expressions (
5.12d
) and (
5.12f
) can be estimated immediately using the
\mathring{g}
-norm lemmas and Proposition
5.5
. Omitting the details, one finds that

|\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_d}}|+|\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_f}}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}.

•

The first term in (
5.12i
) can be estimated immediately using

\left|\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu_{\lambda}\right|\lesssim\tau\cdot|{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}|_{\mathring{g}}\cdot|\upnu_{\lambda}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}.

For the second term in (
5.12i
), we use the symmetry property (
4.49
) and the linearized CMCSH condition (
4.35
) to rewrite this term as

\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right){(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{j}}^{\phantom{{j}}{i}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lambda_{i}\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathring{g}_{ip}\mathring{g}^{jq}\lambda_{q}\upchi_{\lambda}^{p}\right]=\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right)\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lambda_{j}(\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}.

The insight is that from (
5.7
), the trace
\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}
, behaves better than
|{(\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}|_{\mathring{g}}
, indeed

\left|\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+2\zeta^{2}\tau\right)\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lambda_{j}(\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda})\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}\right|\lesssim\tau\cdot|\lambda_{i}|_{\mathring{g}}\cdot|\upchi_{\lambda}^{j}|_{\mathring{g}}\cdot|\tr\hat{\upeta}_{\lambda}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}.

The conclusion is that
|\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_i}}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}
.

Combining all of the above, we estimate the lapse and shift terms using

|\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_b}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_d}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_f}}+\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_i}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,high}.

(5.14)

Finally, the final term (
5.12j
) is estimated trivially using the bounds (
3.5
) and Lemma
5.1
:

|\text{\eqref{eq:metric_high_freq_i}}|\lesssim t|\zeta^{\prime}(t)|\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,high}.

(5.15)

To complete the proof, simply combine the 3 estimates (
5.13
), (
5.14
) and (
5.15
).
∎

6
Linearized Einstein–scalar field: the low-frequency energy estimate

In this section, we prove a low-frequency energy estimate analogous to that derived in Proposition
3.5
for the Fourier modes of the linearized Einstein–scalar field system derived in Proposition
4.7
. Since
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upphi
generically blow up as
t\to 0
, at low frequency we instead consider (Fourier modes of) renormalized quantities
\tilde{{\Upupsilon}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}}
and
\tilde{\upvarphi}
, as defined in (
4.24
).

To be more precise, we pick
T=t_{\lambda*}
in (
4.24
), and thus define the renormalized quantities
{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda}
in the following way:

\displaystyle{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t)={(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+G_{ip}^{\phantom{ip}jq}(t;t_{\lambda*})\cdot{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}},\quad G_{ip}^{\phantom{ip}jq}(t;t_{\lambda*})\coloneqq\int^{t_{\lambda*}}_{t}\mathring{g}_{ip}(s)\mathring{g}^{jq}(s)\frac{ds}{s},

(6.1)

\displaystyle\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda}=\upphi_{\lambda}+\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )\cdot\uppsi_{\lambda}.

(6.2)

Moreover, in contrast to the CMCSH gauge used in the high-frequency energy estimates of Section
5
, for the low-frequency estimates we shall
use a CMCTC
gauge, i.e. a gauge where the linearized shift
\upchi^{j}
vanishes. We firstly rewrite the evolution equations (
4.36
)–(
4.41
) with respect to our renormalized
{\tilde{\Upupsilon}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
,
\tilde{\upvarphi}
and assuming the gauge.

Lemma 6.1
.

In a CMCTC gauge with
\upchi^{j}=0
, the
t\partial_{t}
-derivatives of the quantities
({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\lambda},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda})
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}t\partial_{t}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b}\left({(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-G_{ip}^{\phantom{ip}jq}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right)-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ja}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}(\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{jk}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}\upnu_{\lambda}-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu_{\lambda}\\
+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{b}\left({(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{j}}-G_{ap}^{\phantom{ap}jq}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right)+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ja}\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b}\left({(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{b}}-G_{ip}^{\phantom{ip}bq}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right),\end{split}

(6.3)

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=G_{ip}^{\phantom{ip}jq}\cdot t\partial_{t}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\upnu_{\lambda},

(6.4)

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\uppsi_{\lambda}=-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b}\left(\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda}-\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )\uppsi_{\lambda}\right)-p_{\phi}\upnu_{\lambda},

(6.5)

\displaystyle t\partial_{t}\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda}=\log( \frac{t_{\lambda*}}{t} )\cdot t\partial_{t}\uppsi_{\lambda}+p_{\phi}\upnu_{\lambda}.

(6.6)

Proof.

This follows from direct computation using the equations (
4.36
)–(
4.41
), as well as the symmetry conditions (
4.49
)–(
4.50
).
∎

6.1
The low-frequency energy quantity

Definition 6.1
.

The
geometric low-frequency energy quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,low}(t)
, used for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus\{0\}
and
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq 1
, is given by the following:

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,low}(t)\coloneqq\mathring{g}^{ac}(t_{\lambda*})\mathring{g}_{bd}(t_{\lambda*})\,{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}+\mathring{g}^{ac}(t_{\lambda*})\mathring{g}_{bd}(t_{\lambda*})\,{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{c}}^{\phantom{{c}}{d}}.

(6.7)

We also define the
matter low-frequency energy quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,low}(t)
, as follows:

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,low}(t)\coloneqq\uppsi_{\lambda}^{2}+\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda}^{2}.

(6.8)

Finally, define the
total low-frequency energy quantity
as the sum
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,low}(t)\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upeta,low}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{\lambda,\upphi,low}(t)
.

Remark
.

Unlike the geometric high-frequency energy quantity defined in (
5.1
), the metric
\mathring{g}_{ij}
used as the inner product on the tensors
{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
is
\mathring{g}_{ij}(t_{\lambda*})
, rather than
\mathring{g}_{ij}(t)
. The fact that this inner product is no longer
t
-dependent is crucial in order to show that the tensors
{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
are indeed bounded towards
t=0
. This also indicates the operators
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-p_{i}+p_{j}}
arise in Theorem
1.4
.

Another remark is that though the high-frequency energy is defined for
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq 1
, the relevant energy estimates are only valid for
\tau_{\lambda}(t)\geq\mathring{\tau}\geq 1
, see Lemma
5.1
and Proposition
5.2
. In order to eventually compare the high-frequency energy to the low-frequency energy of Definition
6.1
, we will require
mid-frequency energy estimates
, valid for
1\leq\tau_{\lambda}(t)\leq\mathring{\tau}
, see Section
6.4
.

The aim of this section will be to prove the following low-frequency energy estimate, see the analogous Proposition
3.5
in the case of the wave equation. Unlike the high-frequency energy estimate Proposition
5.2
, the following will depend crucially on the subcriticality condition (
2.22
).

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
({({\upeta}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{({\upkappa}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\upphi_{\lambda},\uppsi_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda})
be a solution to the linearized Einstein–scalar field system (
4.36
)–(
4.50
) in CMCTC gauge. Suppose the background Kasner exponents
p_{i}
