has Fourier decomposition:

{\left(A_{\lambda}\right)}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{D}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}{A}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(x)e^{-i\lambda\cdot x}\,dx.

Remark
.

For sufficiently regular functions
f
, the Fourier inversion formula holds:

f(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}f_{\lambda}\,e^{i\lambda\cdot x},

(A.2)

hence one is able to recover a function
f
on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
from its Fourier series, and similarly for tensors on
\mathbb{T}^{D}
.

Definition A.2
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, a function
f:\mathbb{T}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
lies in the
Sobolev space

H^{s}=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{D})
if the following squared norm is finite, where
\langle\cdot\rangle
is the usual Japanese bracket;
\langle x\rangle^{2}\coloneqq 1+|x|^{2}
.

\|f\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}|f_{\lambda}|^{2}.

(A.3)

Similarly, a
(1,1)
tensor
A={A}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
lies in the Sobolev space
H^{s}
if

\|{A}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{i,j=1}^{D}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}\left|{\left(A_{\lambda}\right)}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}\right|^{2}<+\infty.

(A.4)

Definition A.3
.

Let
L:\mathbb{Z}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function that grows no faster than polynomially. We define the
symbol

\mathcal{L}
associated to
L
, as follows:
For
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{D})
smooth, let
\mathcal{L}f
be the (smooth) function whose Fourier coefficients are given by the following expression

\left(\mathcal{L}f\right)_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}L(\lambda)f_{\lambda}&\text{ if }\lambda\neq 0,\\[3.00003pt]
0&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Next, following the symbol calculus of
[
41
]
, for
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
F\circ T:\mathbb{Z}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
grows no faster than polynomially, define the operator
F(\mathcal{L}):C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{D})\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{D})
to be such that

\left(F(\mathcal{L})f\right)_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}F(\mathcal{L})\,(F\circ L(\lambda))f_{\lambda}&\text{ if }\lambda\neq 0,\\[3.00003pt]
0&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

For
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we say that
\mathcal{L}
is in the
symbol class

S^{m}
if
L(\lambda)
is bounded by a constant multiple of
\langle\lambda\rangle^{m}
. If
\mathcal{L}
is in the symbol class
S^{m}
, then for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{L}
may be extended as a bounded operator from
H^{s}
to
H^{s-m}
.

As promised, we end this section with a brief discussion of the properties of the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}
, which is defined in Definition
1.1
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{*}
be as in Definition
1.1
. Then:

(1)

The symbol
\log(\mathcal{T}_*)
lies in the symbol class
S^{\varepsilon}
if and only if
\varepsilon>0
.

(2)

For
p=\max p_{i}
, the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{-1}
lies in the symbol class
S^{N}
if and only if
N\geq\frac{1}{1-p}
.

(3)

For
P=\min p_{i}
, the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}
lies in the symbol class
S^{N}
if and only if
N\geq-\frac{1}{1-P}
.

Proof.

Using Definition
1.1
, the lemma will follow from an understanding of the growth rates of the function
t_{*}:\mathbb{Z}^{D}\to(0,+\infty)
such that
t_{*}(\lambda)=t_{\lambda*}
satisfies
t_{*}(0)=1
and

\sum_{i=1}^{D}\lambda_{i}^{2}t_{\lambda*}^{2-2p_{i}}=1,\text{ for }\lambda\neq 0.

For each
\lambda\neq 0
, it is immediate from this definition that for all
i
, one has
t_{\lambda*}\leq\lambda_{i}^{-\frac{1}{1-p_{i}}}
, and that this is inequality is sharp if
\lambda_{i}
is the only nonvanishing component of
\lambda
. This proves (3) immediately.

To show (2), note that for
\lambda\neq 0
, there must be some
i\in\{1,\ldots D\}
such that
\lambda_{i}^{2}t_{\lambda*}^{2-2p_{i}}\geq\frac{1}{D}
. Therefore for this
i
, we have that
t_{\lambda*}^{-1}\lesssim\lambda_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-p_{i}}}\leq\langle\lambda\rangle^{\frac{1}{1-p}}
. Thus if
N\geq\frac{1}{1-p}
, the symbol
\mathcal{T}_{*}
lies in the symbol class
S^{N}
. Conversely, if without loss of generality
p=p_{1}
, then considering
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},0,\ldots,0)
proves that we cannot have
t_{\lambda*}^{-1}\lesssim\langle\lambda\rangle^{N}
for any smaller
N
.

Finally, since we have shown both that
t_{\lambda*}\leq\langle\lambda\rangle^{-\frac{1}{1-P}}
and that
t_{\lambda*}^{-1}\leq\langle\lambda\rangle^{\frac{1}{1-p}}
, it is clear that
\log t_{\lambda*}^{-1}\asymp\log\langle\lambda\rangle
. From this, the claim (1) is also straightforward.
∎

Appendix B
Fuchsian ODE systems

A
Fuchsian PDE
is an equation of the following form, where
\mathbf{V}:(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
A
is a suitable matrix-valued operator, and
f
vanishes suitably as
t\to 0
.

\left(t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+A\right)\mathbf{V}=f[t,\mathbf{V}].

Such equations originally arose in the “Fuchs-Frobenius” study of special functions and their singularities in the complex plane.
We refer the reader to the introduction to
[
45
]
for details.

The above definition of Fuchsian PDEs is somewhat imprecise, and in this article we only make concrete definitions in the case of ODEs. In this case, the definition will be as follows.

Definition B.1

(Fuchsian ODEs)
.

Let
\mathbf{V}:[0,T)\subset\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a continuous vector-valued function, and let
\varepsilon>0
. We say that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies a
Fuchsian ODE with
t^{\varepsilon}
weight
if for
t\in(0,t)
, the following equation holds:

\left(t\frac{d}{dt}+A\right)\mathbf{V}=t^{\varepsilon}F[t,\mathbf{V}]

(B.1)

where
A
is a constant
N\times N
matrix, and
F:[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
is continuous in both arguments and locally Lipschitz in its second argument.

Remark
.

One can make more general definitions where the RHS of (
B.1
) tends to
0
at a slower rate than
t^{\epsilon}
, or where the regularity assumptions are somewhat relaxed. However we do not need to do this here, and we refer the interested reader to
[
45
, Chapter 5]
.

We would like a local existence and uniqueness theory for
\mathbf{V}
satisfying Fuchsian ODEs of the form (
B.1
), with initial data given at the ‘singularity’ at
t=0
. The result, which parallels the usual Picard–Lindelöf theorem for regular ODEs, is as follows:

Lemma B.1
.

Consider a Fuchsian ODE of the form (
B.1
), and suppose moreover that the matrix
A
is such that (the operator norm of) the matrix
\sigma^{A}=\exp( A \log\sigma)
is uniformly bounded for
\sigma\in(0,1)
. Then

(a)

Consider the initial data
\mathbf{V}(0)=0
. Then for some
T_{*}>0
, there exists a unique continuous
\mathbf{V}:[0,T_{*})\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
which has
V(0)=0
and satisfies (
B.1
) for
t\in(0,T_{*})
. Further, the solution satisfies the bound
|\mathbf{V}(t)|\lesssim t^{\varepsilon}
.

(b)

Now let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be in the kernel of
A
, and consider initial data
\mathbf{V}(0)=\mathbf{v}
. Then for some
T_{*}>0
, there exists a unique continuous
\mathbf{V}:[0,T_{*})\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
which has
V(0)=\mathbf{V}
and satisfies (
B.1
) for
t\in(0,T_{*})
. Further, the solution satisfies the bound
|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{v}|\lesssim t^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

For part (a), we employ a contraction mapping argument in the following subset of
C([0,T_{*}),\mathbb{R}^{N})
:

X=\{\mathbf{W}\in C([0,T_{*}),\mathbb{R}^{N}):\mathbf{W}(0)=0,\,|\mathbf{W}(t)|\leq M\},

where
T_{*}>0
and
M>0
will be fixed later. The idea is to use the following map
S:X\to X
.

S(\mathbf{W})(0)=0,\quad S(\mathbf{W})(t)=t^{\varepsilon}\int^{1}_{0}\sigma^{A-1+\varepsilon}F[\sigma t,\mathbf{W}(\sigma t)]\,d\sigma\qquad\forall t\in(0,T_{*}).

(B.2)

Firstly, we note that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies the Fuchsian ODE (
B.1
) with
\mathbf{V}(0)=0
if and only if
S(\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{V}
. This is because the integral in (
B.2
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle t^{\varepsilon}\int^{1}_{0}\sigma^{A-1+\varepsilon}F[\sigma t,\mathbf{W}(\sigma t)]\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=t^{-A}\int^{1}_{0}(\sigma t)^{A+\varepsilon}F[\sigma t,\mathbf{W}(\sigma t)]\,\frac{d\sigma}{\sigma}

\displaystyle=t^{-A}\int^{t}_{0}\tilde{\sigma}^{A+\varepsilon}F[\tilde{\sigma},\mathbf{W}(\tilde{\sigma})]\,\frac{d\tilde{\sigma}}{\tilde{\sigma}}.

Therefore
S(\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{V}
if and only if we have that

t^{A}\mathbf{V}(t)=\int^{t}_{0}\tilde{\sigma}^{A+\epsilon}F[\tilde{\sigma},\mathbf{V}(\tilde{\sigma})]\,\frac{d\tilde{\sigma}}{\tilde{\sigma}},\qquad\forall t\in(0,T_{*})

which is obviously equivalent to
\mathbf{V}
satisfying (
B.1
) for
t\in(0,T_{*})
.

We next show that for correctly chosen
M
and
T_{*}
, the map
S
sends the domain
X
to itself. The fact that
S(\mathbf{W})
is continuous on
[0,T_{*})
is easily checked, while for the boundedness let us first suppose that
K
is the Lipschitz constant of
F
with respect to its second argument (since this lemma is local in nature, we assume without loss of generality that
K
is a global Lipschitz constant). Then we have

\displaystyle|S(\mathbf{W})(t)|

\displaystyle\leq t^{\varepsilon}\int^{1}_{0}\|\sigma^{A}\|_{op}\,\sigma^{-1+\epsilon}|F[\sigma t,\mathbf{W}(\sigma t)]|\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq t^{\epsilon}\sup_{\sigma\in(0,1)}\|\sigma^{A}\|_{op}\cdot\left(\sup_{s\in(0,t)}F[s,0]+KM\right)\cdot\int^{1}_{0}\sigma^{-1+\epsilon}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1}t^{\epsilon}\sup_{\sigma\in(0,1)}\|\sigma^{A}\|_{op}\cdot\left(\sup_{s\in(0,t)}F[s,0]+KM\right).

Therefore for any
M>0
, for
T_{*}
chosen sufficiently small we have
|S(\mathbf{W})(t)|\leq M
, hence
S:X\to X
as required.
A similar computation shows that
S
is a contraction mapping with respect to the
C^{0}
norm. Indeed,

\displaystyle|S(\mathbf{W}_{1})(t)-S(\mathbf{W}_{2})(t)|

\displaystyle\leq t^{\epsilon}\int^{1}_{0}\|\sigma^{A}\|_{op}\,\sigma^{-1+\epsilon}|F[\sigma t,\mathbf{W}_{1}(\sigma t)]-F[\sigma t,\mathbf{W}_{2}(\sigma t)]|\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq t^{\epsilon}\sup_{\sigma\in(0,1)}\|\sigma^{A}\|_{op}\cdot\int^{1}_{0}\sigma^{-1+\epsilon}\cdot K|\mathbf{W}_{1}(\sigma t)-\mathbf{W}_{2}(\sigma t)|\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq K\epsilon^{-1}t^{\epsilon}\sup_{\sigma\in(0,1)}\|\sigma^{A}\|_{op}\cdot\sup_{s\in(0,T_{*})}|\mathbf{W}_{1}(s)-\mathbf{W}_{2}(s)|.

Therefore
S
is a contraction mapping so long as
T_{*}
is chosen such that
K\epsilon^{-1}T_{*}^{\epsilon}\sup_{\sigma\in(0,1)}\|\sigma^{A}\|_{op}<1
.

Hence by the contraction mapping theorem, there is a unique fixed point
\mathbf{V}
of
S
in the set
X
. This completes the existence part of the proof. For uniqueness, first observe that the contraction argument shows that two solutions
\mathbf{V}_{1}
and
\mathbf{V}_{2}
to (
B.1
) agree on an interval
[0,T_{-}]\subset[0,T_{*})
so long as they remain bounded by
M
. Moreover, by the above bounds they must have
|\mathbf{V}_{1}(t)|,|\mathbf{V}_{2}(t)|\lesssim t^{\epsilon}
for
t\in[0,T_{-}]
.

Therefore, by a straightforward continuity argument, they must agree in the whole interval
[0,T_{*})
, and satisfy the bound
|\mathbf{V}_{1}(t)|=|\mathbf{V}_{2}(t)|\lesssim t^{\epsilon}
everywhere in this interval of existence. This concludes the proof of (a).

Finally, part (b) follows immediately from part (a) simply by taking
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{v}
and, upon using the fact that
A\mathbf{v}=0
, rewriting the Fuchsian ODE (
B.1
) as

\left(t\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}+A\right)=t^{\epsilon}\tilde{F}[t,\tilde{\mathbf{V}}],\qquad\tilde{F}[t,\mathbf{W}]=F[t,\mathbf{W}+\mathbf{v}].

Applying part (a) to the above equation, this concludes the proof of (b).
∎

Appendix C
Propagation of constraints

In Appendix
C
, we state and prove two propositions explaining how the constraint equations (and gauge conditions) are propagated in evolution. The first, Proposition
C.1
, shows that in the CMCSH evolution of Proposition
4.4
(i), the constraints (
4.15
)–(
4.20
) and the gauge conditions (
4.4
) and (
1.39
), remain true on all Cauchy slices
\Sigma_{t}
. The second, Proposition
C.2
, starts with the asymptotic constraint equations of Lemma
4.5
and shows that the solution produced by Proposition
7.4
solves the actual constraint equations (
4.15
)–(
4.20
) as well as the CMC condition
\tr\upkappa=0
.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
({\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}(t),\upphi(t),\uppsi(t),\upnu(t),\upchi^{j}(t))
be a solution to the elliptic-hyperbolic system (
4.6
), (
4.7
), (
4.9
), (
4.10
), (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.14
) for
t>0
. Suppose furthermore that for some time
T>0
, the constraint equations (
4.15
), (
4.16
), (
4.19
) and (
4.20
) hold at
t=T
, and also that the CMCSH gauge constraints
\tr\hat{\upkappa}=0
and (
1.39
) hold at
t=T
. Then the whole system (
4.6
)–(
4.20
) holds for
t>0
.

Proof.

Let us start with the symmetry constraints (
4.19
) and (
4.20
). For this purpose, we define

\mathcal{A}^{ac}\coloneqq\mathring{g}^{ab}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-\mathring{g}^{cb}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}},\quad\mathcal{B}^{ac}\coloneqq\mathring{g}^{ab}({\hat{\upkappa}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}){\hat{\upeta}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}})-\mathring{g}^{cb}({\hat{\upkappa}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}){\hat{\upeta}}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{a}}).

Using only (
4.6
) and (
4.7
), one may derive evolution equations for
\mathcal{A}^{ac}
and
\mathcal{B}^{ac}
:

t\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}^{ac}=\mathcal{B}^{ac}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}})\mathcal{A}^{bc}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}})\mathcal{A}^{ab},\quad t\partial_{t}\mathcal{B}^{ac}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}^{ac}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{b}\partial_{d}\mathcal{A}^{dc}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{cb}\partial_{b}\partial_{d}\mathcal{A}^{ad}.

Now, assuming the spatially harmonic gauge condition (
1.39
) holds, the expression
t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{b}\partial_{d}\mathcal{A}^{dc}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{cb}\partial_{b}\partial_{d}\mathcal{A}^{ad}
will vanish. Unfortunately we have not yet shown that (
1.39
) is true for all times, but our strategy will be first apply a gauge transformation, as in Proposition
4.4
(ii), to create a new solution to the elliptic-hyperbolic system (
4.6
), (
4.7
), (
4.9
), (
4.10
), (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.14
) that is spatially harmonic.

Then since
\mathcal{A}^{ac}
and
\mathcal{B}^{ac}
are gauge invariant under the transformations (
4.21
) and (
4.22
),
it still suffices to deal with the case where we assume (
1.39
), in which case our equations reduce to

t\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}^{ac}=\mathcal{B}^{ac}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}})\mathcal{A}^{bc}+(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}})\mathcal{A}^{ab},\quad t\partial_{t}\mathcal{B}^{ac}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}^{ac}.

Hence
\mathcal{A}^{ac}
and
\mathcal{B}^{ac}
obey a linear and homogeneous first order hyperbolic system, and therefore their vanishing at
t=T
implies their vanishing everywhere. To be explicit, one can use the following energy estimate:

\text{ if }\;\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\mathring{g}_{ab}\mathring{g}_{cd}(t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}\mathcal{A}^{ac}\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}^{bd}+\mathcal{B}^{ac}\mathcal{B}^{bd}+\mathcal{A}^{ac}\mathcal{A}^{bd})\,dx,\qquad\text{ then }\;t\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}(t)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}(t).

Grönwall’s inequality therefore shows that
\mathcal{A}^{ac}=\mathcal{B}^{ac}=0
for all
t>0
, proving (
4.19
) and (
4.20
).

We next move onto the Hamiltonian and momentum constraints (
4.15
) and (
4.16
). Instead of dealing with these equations directly, we instead define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\tr\hat{\upeta}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\partial_{i}\partial_{a}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}){\hat{\upkappa}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\uppsi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i}=\partial_{j}{\hat{\upkappa}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}+\partial_{i}((t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}){\hat{\upeta}}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi)-(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\partial_{j}\tr\hat{\upeta}.

Then using (
4.6
), (
4.7
), (
4.9
) and the elliptic equation (
4.12
), one derives the evolution equations:

t\partial_{t}\mathcal{H}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}\mathcal{P}_{j},\quad t\partial_{t}\mathcal{P}_{i}=\partial_{i}\mathcal{H}.

Note that the constraints being satisfied at
t=T
imply that
\mathcal{H}=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}=0
at
t=T
. Now

\text{ if }\;\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{P}}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}(t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\mathcal{P}_{i}\mathcal{P}_{j}+\mathcal{H}^{2})\,dx,\qquad\text{ then }\;t\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{P}}(t)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{P}}(t).

Therefore,
\mathcal{H}=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}=0
for all times
t>0
. Furthermore, using also the elliptic equations (
4.12
) and (
4.14
) one derives that

t\partial_{t}\tr\hat{\upkappa}=0,\quad t\partial_{t}(2\partial_{j}{\hat{\upeta}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-\partial_{i}\tr\hat{\upeta})=2\mathcal{P}_{i}.

Since we already have
\mathcal{P}_{i}=0
, this implies the gauge conditions (
4.4
) and (
1.39
) remain true in evolution. Combining this with
\mathcal{H}=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}=0
, we also deduce (
4.15
) and (
4.16
). Finally, (
4.17
) may be derived as a consequence of (
4.16
), (
4.19
) and (
4.20
).
∎

Proposition C.2
.

As in Proposition
6.5
, let
({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\lambda},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda})
be a solution to the ODE system (
6.3
)–(
6.6
) together with (
4.42
), is such that
({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\lambda},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda})\to({((\upkappa_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{((\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\infty})_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},(\uppsi_{\infty})_{\lambda},(\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\infty})_{\lambda})
as
t\to 0
.

Suppose moreover that these limiting quantities satisfy the asymptotic constraints (
4.51
)–(
4.55
), with
T=\mathcal{T}_{*}
. Then the solution
({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\lambda},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda},\upnu_{\lambda})
obeys the constraint equations (
4.45
)–(
4.50
). (Here
{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda}
are related to
{(\upeta_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
and
\upphi_{\lambda}
by (
6.1
) and (
6.2
).)

Proof.

The strategy is to combine the constraint propagation method of Proposition
C.1
with some Fuchsian analysis. We start with the symmetry constraints (
4.49
) and (
4.50
). Instead of dealing with
{\upeta}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}
directly, we first define

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac}\coloneqq\mathring{g}^{ab}(t_{\lambda*}){(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-\mathring{g}^{cb}(t_{\lambda*}){(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac}\coloneqq\mathring{g}^{ab}(t_{\lambda*})({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{c}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}){(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{c}})-\mathring{g}^{cb}(t_{\lambda*})({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}-(2t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{d}}){(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{d}}^{\phantom{{d}}{a}}).

We use that by the proof of Lemma
4.5
,
((\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac})=(0,0)
if and only
((\mathcal{A}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})^{ac})=(0,0)
, where
\mathcal{A}^{ac}
and
\mathcal{B}^{ac}
were defined in the proof of Proposition
C.1
.

By the assumptions of Proposition
C.2
,
((\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac})
are bounded and tend to
(0,0)
as
t\to 0
. Thus by the Fuchsian analysis of Lemma
B.1
, particularly the uniqueness statement, one may deduce that
((\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac})
, and therefore also
((\mathcal{A}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})^{ac})
, vanish identically if we can prove that for the vector
\mathbf{C}=((\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac}:a,c=1,\ldots,D)
, there is some bounded (time-dependent) matrix
A_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
such that

t\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{C}=t^{\updelta}A_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbf{C}.

(C.1)

One may verify (
C.1
) directly by using the system (
6.3
), (
6.4
), (
4.42
) and going through a lengthy calculation. We propose an alternative approach using linear algebra. Firstly, by Proposition
6.4
(ignoring all the appearances of
t_{\lambda*}
), for a solution of the system (
6.3
)–(
6.6
) and (
4.42
) there does exist a bounded (time-dependent) matrix
B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
such that for
\mathbf{V}=({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\lambda},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda})
, such that we verify the Fuchsian ODE:

t\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{C}=t^{\updelta}B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}\cdot\mathbf{V}.

On the other hand there is the natural linear map
L:({(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}},\uppsi_{\lambda},\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda})\mapsto((\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac})
, which is surjective onto the
D(D-1)
-dimensional space
(\Skew_{D\times D})^{2}
, meaning two copies of the space of skew-symmetric matrices.

Thus (
C.1
) holds if
B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
factorizes as
B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}=A_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}\circ L
for some bounded
A_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
. The key observation is that
\ker L\subset\ker B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
; this follows since by Proposition
C.1
, for
t>0
we know that
t\frac{d}{dt}((\mathcal{A}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})^{ac})=0
if
((\mathcal{A}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})^{ac})=0
and thus the same holds true for
((\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac},\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac})
. Therefore, one may apply the linear algebra fact that since
\ker L\subset\ker B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
and
L
is surjective onto
(\Skew_{D\times D})^{2}
, there must exist some map
A_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}:(\Skew_{D\times D})^{2}\to(\Skew_{D\times D})^{2}
such that
B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
factorizes as
B_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}=A_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}\circ L
.

In fact, one may apply the linear algebra fact in such a way that
A_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}}
remains uniformly bounded in time. Therefore the Fuchsian ODE (
C.1
), and thus the propagation of the symmetry constraints follows. We summarize the strategy as suitably renormalized constraints (i.e.
((\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{ac},(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})^{ac})
) + good derivative bounds (i.e. Proposition
6.4
) + constraint propagation at
t>0
(i.e. Proposition
C.1
) implies constraint propagation from asymptotic data.

One can play the same game for the Hamiltonian and momentum constraints. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b}\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}\int^{t_{\lambda*}}_{t}\mathring{g}_{bp}(s)\mathring{g}^{iq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}+(t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}){(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\upphi_{\lambda},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda})_{i}=-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j}{\upkappa}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}}-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}((t{\mathring{k}}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{a}}){(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{a}}^{\phantom{{a}}{b}}+2p_{\phi}\tilde{\upvarphi}_{\lambda})+\mathrm{i}(t{\mathring{k}}_{{i}}^{\phantom{{i}}{j}})\lambda_{j}\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}\hskip 170.71652pt

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}\log( \frac{t}{t_{\lambda*}} )\left(t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b}\tr\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda}-t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}{(\tilde{\Upupsilon}_{\lambda})}_{{b}}^{\phantom{{b}}{i}}+t^{2}\mathring{g}^{ab}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{a}\int^{t_{\lambda*}}_{t}\mathring{g}_{bp}(s)\mathring{g}^{iq}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\cdot{(\upkappa_{\lambda})}_{{q}}^{\phantom{{q}}{p}}\right).

It is straightforward to check that given the assumptions of the proposition,
(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},(\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda})_{i})
are bounded and tend to
(0,0)
as
t\to 0
. Since
(\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda})_{i}=(\mathcal{P}_{\lambda})_{i}+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}\log( \frac{t}{t_{\lambda*}})\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
, for
\mathcal{P}_{i}
as in the proof of Proposition
C.1
, we also have constraint propagation at
t>0
. Finally, from Proposition
6.4
one may prove good derivative bounds in the sense that for some bounded (time-dependent) matrix
B_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{Q}}
and
\mathbf{V}
as before,

t\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\\[1.00006pt]
(\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda})_{i}\end{pmatrix}=t^{\updelta}B_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{Q}}\cdot\mathbf{V}.

Therefore following the same program as before we deduce that
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
and
(\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda})_{i}
vanish for all
t>0
, and therefore the constraints (
4.45
) and (
4.46
) are satisfied.

As always, (
4.47
) follows from (
4.46
) and (
4.49
)–(
4.50
). This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎
